# Psychology — Answers

Questions: [questions.md](questions.md). Answer numbers match question numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1 AOS 1: How does the brain function?

#### Approaches to understanding the brain

**A1.** B. verbally name the object
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-1 -->

Explanation: Information from the left visual field goes to the right hemisphere, and language production is usually lateralised to the left hemisphere. With the corpus callosum cut, the information cannot reach the left hemisphere, so the person cannot name it, though the left hand (right hemisphere) can still select it.

**A2.** D. Functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI)
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-2 -->

Explanation: fMRI measures the blood oxygen level dependent (BOLD) signal and gives good spatial resolution. EEG measures electrical activity at the scalp with excellent temporal but poor spatial resolution; CT shows structure only.

**A3.** C. high temporal resolution
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-3 -->

Explanation: EEG has high temporal resolution (milliseconds) but poor spatial resolution. Detailed anatomy is the strength of CT or MRI, and radioactive tracers are used in PET.

**A4.** C. a case study that suggested a link between the frontal lobe and personality
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-4 -->

Explanation: Gage is a classic case study, which provides rich detail on one individual but limits generalisation. It suggested the frontal lobe is involved in personality and social behaviour; it was not an experiment.

**A5.** B. Findings may not generalise because the damage is unique to each individual
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-5 -->

Explanation: Each injury differs in location, extent and the person's prior history, so results from one case may not generalise. Case studies do provide detailed, in-depth information.

**A6.** Similarity: both are non-invasive (do not require surgery) and can be used with healthy participants to study brain activity during tasks (1); Difference: fMRI has high spatial resolution because it detects blood oxygen changes in specific regions, whereas EEG has poor spatial resolution (1); EEG has high temporal resolution (milliseconds) whereas fMRI has lower temporal resolution because blood flow changes are slower (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A7.** (a) The participant would say they saw 'key' (1); (b) The right visual field is processed by the left hemisphere, which controls speech, so the word 'key' can be named (1); the left visual field is processed by the right hemisphere, which lacks language production, and the corpus callosum cannot transfer the information so 'ring' cannot be verbalised (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A8.** Technique: fMRI (or PET) (1); Strength: shows which specific regions have increased blood flow/oxygen use during the task, with good spatial resolution (1); Limitation: expensive, participant must lie still in a scanner, and poor temporal resolution so it cannot show rapid changes (1). Accept a justified alternative such as EEG if its strength and limitation are correct.
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A9.** A. the left frontal lobe
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-9 -->

Explanation: Tan's damage was in the left frontal region linked to speech production, while understanding was relatively preserved. Right temporal damage is not the typical cause of this pattern, and the occipital lobe and cerebellum are not associated with fluent speech.

**A10.** C. researchers cannot control the location or extent of the damage
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-10 -->

Explanation: Damage from stroke or injury occurs naturally, so researchers cannot choose its location or size, and it may spread across several regions. Damage can usually be seen on CT or MRI, and the other options are not general features of lesion analysis.

**A11.** D. computed tomography (CT)
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-11 -->

Explanation: CT is fast and readily shows bleeding and skull fractures, which makes it suited to emergencies. EEG and fMRI measure activity rather than giving a quick structural picture, and PET is slower and requires a radioactive tracer.

**A12.** B. the uptake of a radioactive tracer that indicates metabolic activity
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-12 -->

Explanation: PET tracks a radioactive tracer, such as a glucose analogue, to show where metabolic activity is high. Blood oxygen changes describe fMRI, scalp electrical signals describe EEG, and X-ray absorption describes CT.

**A13.** A. gives more detailed soft-tissue images without ionising radiation
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-13 -->

Explanation: MRI uses magnetic fields and radio waves, so it avoids ionising radiation and shows soft tissue in more detail than CT. MRI is generally slower and more costly than CT, while electrical activity and radioactive tracers belong to EEG and PET.

**A14.** C. isolate activity linked to the cognitive process of interest
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-14 -->

Explanation: Both tasks share general visual and motor demands, so subtraction leaves activity specific to the process being studied. Subtraction does not change temporal resolution, which remains limited by the slow BOLD response.

**A15.** D. touch from the right hand is processed by the left hemisphere, which controls speech
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-15 -->

Explanation: Touch from each hand is sent to the opposite hemisphere, so input from the right hand reaches the left hemisphere, where language is usually produced. This input does not need to cross the corpus callosum, so it can be named.

**A16.** B. some functions are mainly controlled by one hemisphere rather than the other
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-16 -->

Explanation: Lateralisation describes specialised functions, such as language production in the left hemisphere for most people. The other options describe equal distribution of function or misread the term.

**A17.** C. an experiment, because the researcher manipulates the lesion (the independent variable)
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-17 -->

Explanation: The researcher controls which rats receive the lesion and compares them with a control group, which defines an experiment. Correlational designs measure existing variables without manipulating them, and this study compares groups rather than in-depth single cases.

**A18.** B. move or heat metal objects in the body
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-18 -->

Explanation: The strong magnet can pull on ferromagnetic implants and heat some metals, creating a serious safety risk. MRI does not use X-rays or ionising radiation, and absorbing X-rays describes CT.

**A19.** C. the synchronised activity of large groups of cortical neurons
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-19 -->

Explanation: EEG electrodes on the scalp detect summed activity from large populations of cortical neurons firing together. Single deep neurons are too far from the scalp to be recorded, and blood flow and tracer decay are measured by fMRI and PET.

**A20.** A. results may not generalise to humans because of species differences in brain structure and function
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-20 -->

Explanation: Animal lesion studies allow controlled placement of damage, but the brains of rats or monkeys differ from the human brain, so findings must be applied to humans cautiously. Lesions in animals can often be placed precisely, and these studies do yield information about brain function.

**A21.** D. a stroke that damages the left frontal lobe
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-21 -->

Explanation: Strokes, injuries and disease produce damage without researchers' intervention, which is what makes them natural lesions studied in case studies. Deliberate surgical or electrical lesions are made by researchers for experimental purposes.

**A22.** B. an indirect measure of neural activity, based on changes in oxygenated blood flow
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-22 -->

Explanation: Active neurons increase demand for oxygen, and the local blood flow response changes the ratio of oxygenated to deoxygenated haemoglobin, which fMRI detects. The other options describe EEG, PET and structural imaging respectively.

**A23.** C. scanning is expensive and time-consuming, which limits the number of participants
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-23 -->

Explanation: fMRI equipment is costly and each session takes time, so sample sizes are often limited for practical reasons. There is no standard ethical limit of ten participants, and fMRI participants can often be scanned repeatedly, which is a strength over PET.

**A24.** A. transfer information between the two cerebral hemispheres
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-24 -->

Explanation: The corpus callosum is a large bundle of nerve fibres connecting the two hemispheres, so it lets them share information. The other options describe the motor cortex, memory systems and visual pathways rather than this structure.

**A25.** Case study: an in-depth investigation of one person or a small group with naturally occurring brain damage or a condition, in which the researcher does not manipulate variables [1]; Experiment: the researcher manipulates an independent variable under controlled conditions, usually with a control group, to establish cause and effect [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-25 -->

Explanation:

**A26.** Informed consent: participants, or a legally recognised substitute if their capacity is impaired, must understand and agree to take part [1]; this is important because people with brain injuries may have reduced ability to make decisions, so their rights must be protected [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-26 -->

Explanation:

**A27.** (a) fMRI [1], as it detects changes in blood oxygenation that indicate which regions are active during the task, with good spatial resolution [1]; (b) MRI (or CT) [1], as it shows the brain's structure in detail so abnormalities can be identified, and MRI does not use ionising radiation [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-27 -->

Explanation:

**A28.** Computed tomography (CT) [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-28 -->

Explanation:

**A29.** Stimulating a specific area and recording the movement or sensation the patient reported links that area to a function [1]; limitation: it is only possible in the small number of patients undergoing surgery, so the sample is limited and may not be representative [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-29 -->

Explanation:

**A30.** The finding is correlational: it shows an association between activity and the task, not cause and effect [1]; the activity could be incidental or a consequence of the task, so evidence such as lesions or stimulation is needed to show necessity [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-30 -->

Explanation:

**A31.** fMRI does not use a radioactive tracer, so the participant is not exposed to radiation and can be scanned repeatedly [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-31 -->

Explanation:

**A32.** After removal of the hippocampus, H.M. could not form new long-term (declarative) memories, suggesting the hippocampus is needed for memory formation [1]; limitation: this is a single case, and the surgical damage extended beyond the hippocampus, so the conclusion may not generalise [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-32 -->

Explanation:

**A33.** Structural techniques show the anatomy of the brain, such as CT or MRI [1]; functional techniques show activity or processes in the brain, such as fMRI or PET [1]; functional techniques are used to link regions to tasks, whereas structural techniques are used to identify damage or abnormalities [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-33 -->

Explanation:

**A34.** Strength: case studies provide detailed, in-depth information about the effects of specific injuries, which may be rare and cannot be deliberately produced in humans [1]; they can suggest hypotheses about the role of particular regions [1]; limitation: each injury differs in location, extent and the person's prior functioning, so findings may not generalise [1]; the researcher cannot control the damage, so cause and effect cannot be firmly established [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-34 -->

Explanation:

**A35.** B. It has poor spatial resolution, so the source of activity is hard to locate precisely
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-35 -->

Explanation: EEG records electrical activity from the scalp, which gives excellent timing but makes it difficult to locate where the activity originates. EEG does record cortical activity and needs no tracer, as tracers are used in PET, and the magnet option describes MRI rather than EEG.

**A36.** C. The lesions would cause permanent, unnecessary harm to people who gain no benefit from the study
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-36 -->

Explanation: Deliberately causing permanent brain damage in healthy people breaches the ethical principle of avoiding harm, because participants gain no therapeutic benefit. Lesions can be detected on scans and memory can be measured in adults, while sample size is a methodological concern rather than the deciding ethical issue.

**A37.** A. To check whether the finding is reliable and can be reproduced, rather than a chance result from a small sample
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-37 -->

Explanation: Replication with a larger, independent sample tests whether a result holds up and is not an artefact of chance or a small group. Replication does not by itself prove causation, tracer use is a feature of PET, and sample size does not change the size of the brain.

**A38.** CT has good spatial resolution and shows the brain's structure in detail [1]; CT produces a static image and has poor temporal resolution, so it cannot track changes over time [1]; EEG has good temporal resolution, recording changes within milliseconds, but poor spatial resolution [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-38 -->

Explanation:

**A39.** The control group receives no lesion (or a sham operation) and shows normal performance, providing a baseline for comparison [1]; any difference between the groups can then be attributed to the lesion rather than to other factors [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-39 -->

Explanation:

**A40.** C. fMRI, because it detects changes in blood oxygen linked to neural activity
<!-- id: vce-psych12-ab-c1 -->

Explanation: Functional techniques (fMRI, PET) show activity; structural techniques (CT, MRI) show anatomy. Phrenology has no scientific support.

#### Structure and function of the brain

**A41.** A. Broca's area
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-1 -->

Explanation: Broca's area, in the left frontal lobe, is involved in speech production. Damage to Wernicke's area typically results in fluent but meaningless speech and poor comprehension.

**A42.** B. Hippocampus
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-2 -->

Explanation: The hippocampus is critical for forming new explicit (declarative) memories. The amygdala is associated with emotional responses such as fear and with emotional memories.

**A43.** B. parietal lobe
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-3 -->

Explanation: The primary somatosensory cortex receives touch, temperature and pain information and lies in the parietal lobe. The primary motor cortex is in the frontal lobe.

**A44.** D. Cerebellum
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-4 -->

Explanation: The cerebellum coordinates voluntary movement, balance and posture. Prefrontal cortex damage would be expected to affect planning and decision-making.

**A45.** D. The corpus callosum is a bundle of fibres that connects the hemispheres
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-5 -->

Explanation: The corpus callosum allows communication between the hemispheres. Each hemisphere mainly controls the contralateral (opposite) side of the body, and language is usually lateralised to the left hemisphere.

**A46.** Accept two well-justified structures, e.g. temporal lobe (involved in recognising faces/auditory and object recognition) (1+1); amygdala (processes fear and emotional responses, so reduced fear suggests damage) (1+1). Each structure named (1) and linked to its function (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-6 -->

Explanation:

**A47.** Frontal lobe: involved in voluntary movement (motor cortex), planning, decision-making, reasoning, judgement and personality (1-2); Example: damage to the prefrontal cortex may lead to impulsive behaviour, poor judgement or changes in personality/emotional regulation (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-7 -->

Explanation:

**A48.** Hippocampus: forms new explicit/declarative memories and is involved in spatial memory (1); Amygdala: processes emotions, especially fear and aggression, and adds emotional significance to memories (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-8 -->

Explanation:

**A49.** A. occipital lobe
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-9 -->

Explanation: The primary visual cortex sits in the occipital lobe at the back of the brain. The temporal lobe is mainly associated with hearing and object recognition, so it is a tempting but incorrect choice.

**A50.** B. temporal lobe
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-10 -->

Explanation: The primary auditory cortex lies in the temporal lobe, on the side of the brain above the ears. The occipital lobe is the visual area, so choosing it confuses hearing with vision.

**A51.** C. Medulla oblongata
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-11 -->

Explanation: The medulla oblongata regulates vital automatic functions such as heart rate and breathing. The cerebellum controls balance and coordination, so it is a common confusion because it is also found towards the back of the brain.

**A52.** D. Thalamus
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-12 -->

Explanation: The thalamus acts as a relay station, directing sensory signals to the appropriate areas of the cortex. The hypothalamus regulates hunger, thirst and body temperature, so it is a tempting but incorrect choice.

**A53.** A. Hypothalamus
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-13 -->

Explanation: The hypothalamus sits below the thalamus and maintains homeostasis by regulating hunger, thirst and body temperature. The amygdala is involved in fear and emotion, so it is a tempting but incorrect choice.

**A54.** B. Awareness of space and of the positions of objects around the body
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-14 -->

Explanation: The parietal lobe integrates sensory information to represent space, and right parietal damage often leads to neglect of the left side. Understanding spoken words is linked to Wernicke's area rather than spatial awareness.

**A55.** D. It increases the surface area available for neurons
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-15 -->

Explanation: Folding packs a large area of neural tissue into the limited space of the skull, so more neurons fit. Folding does not change the number of hemispheres, which is always two.

**A56.** A. Right hemisphere
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-16 -->

Explanation: In most people the right hemisphere is more involved in visual-spatial processing. The left hemisphere is usually dominant for language, so choosing it confuses spatial and language functions.

**A57.** B. Medulla oblongata
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-17 -->

Explanation: The medulla is part of the brainstem, not the limbic system, which includes the amygdala, hippocampus and hypothalamus. Each of the other three options is a limbic structure.

**A58.** C. Cerebellum
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-18 -->

Explanation: The cerebellum is small compared with the cerebrum but packs in the majority of the brain's neurons. The cerebral cortex is far larger in volume but contains fewer neurons.

**A59.** D. Hypothalamus
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-19 -->

Explanation: The hypothalamus controls the pituitary gland, which in turn regulates hormone release. The hippocampus and amygdala are limbic structures involved in memory and emotion rather than hormone control.

**A60.** A. Left occipital lobe
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-20 -->

Explanation: The left primary visual cortex processes information from the right visual field, so damage there causes loss of right-side vision. Right occipital damage would affect the left visual field instead.

**A61.** B. Four
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-21 -->

Explanation: Each cerebral hemisphere has four lobes: frontal, parietal, temporal and occipital. Choosing three is a common mistake that overlooks one of these lobes.

**A62.** C. Brainstem
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-22 -->

Explanation: The brainstem, which includes the medulla, pons and midbrain, connects the spinal cord to higher brain structures. The limbic system is involved in emotion and memory, and the corpus callosum connects the two cerebral hemispheres.

**A63.** D. White matter
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-23 -->

Explanation: The corpus callosum is a bundle of myelinated axons, which is white matter. Grey matter, made of cell bodies and dendrites, is found mainly in the cortex, so choosing it confuses the two tissue types.

**A64.** A. Temporal lobe
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-24 -->

Explanation: The hippocampus sits deep within the temporal lobe, close to the amygdala. The frontal lobe, at the front of the brain, is mainly involved in movement, planning and decision-making, so it is a common confusion.

**A65.** B. Flashes of light
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-25 -->

Explanation: Stimulating the primary visual cortex typically produces visual sensations such as flashes of light, because that is where visual signals are processed. Stimulating the somatosensory cortex would produce touch sensations rather than light.

**A66.** C. Central sulcus
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-26 -->

Explanation: The central sulcus separates the frontal lobe from the parietal lobe, with the primary motor cortex lying just in front of it. The longitudinal fissure divides the two hemispheres rather than the lobes, so it is a common mix-up.

**A67.** Body parts requiring fine, precise movement have a larger representation in the motor cortex [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-27 -->

Explanation:

**A68.** Temporal lobe [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-28 -->

Explanation:

**A69.** Motor pathways cross over in the lower brainstem, so each hemisphere controls the opposite side of the body [1]; the right motor cortex therefore controls the left side of the body, so the left side is weakened [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-29 -->

Explanation:

**A70.** The cerebellum fine-tunes movement, coordinating timing and balance as the skill is practised [1]; with repetition, movements become smoother and more automatic [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-30 -->

Explanation:

**A71.** The hypothalamus contains a group of cells (the suprachiasmatic nucleus) that acts as the body's internal clock [1]; it uses light signals from the eyes to regulate daily rhythms of sleepiness and alertness [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-31 -->

Explanation:

**A72.** Auditory pathways are bilateral, so each ear sends signals to both auditory cortices [1]; the right temporal lobe, which is undamaged, still receives input from both ears [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-32 -->

Explanation:

**A73.** The left visual field is processed by the right hemisphere [1]; the right hemisphere controls the left hand, so the patient can point to or pick up a key with the left hand [1]; the patient cannot say 'key' aloud because the information cannot cross the severed corpus callosum to the left hemisphere's language areas [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-33 -->

Explanation:

**A74.** The hippocampus is involved in spatial memory and navigation [1]; the larger hippocampus suggests that learning and storing detailed spatial maps is associated with changes in its structure [1]; the study is correlational, so it cannot prove that the training caused the enlargement [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-34 -->

Explanation:

**A75.** Myelin is a fatty insulating sheath that wraps around axons [1]; it allows the electrical impulse to jump between gaps in the sheath, which is faster than travelling along the whole axon [1]; so messages travel more quickly between brain regions [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-35 -->

Explanation:

**A76.** Medulla oblongata: controls automatic functions such as breathing and heart rate [1]; Cerebellum: coordinates balance and smooth voluntary movement [1]; Damage to the medulla can disrupt breathing and heart rate, which are essential for survival [1]; Cerebellum damage affects coordination but does not directly control vital functions, so it is less immediately life-threatening [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-36 -->

Explanation:

**A77.** The amygdala detects the threat and triggers a rapid fear response [1]; it sends signals that produce physiological arousal and an immediate reaction [1]; the prefrontal cortex evaluates the situation and sends regulatory signals that can dampen the amygdala's response [1]; this allows the person to respond to the threat in a controlled rather than impulsive way [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-37 -->

Explanation:

**A78.** Frontal lobe: voluntary movement, planning and decision-making [1]; Parietal lobe: processing touch, pain and temperature [1]; Temporal lobe: auditory processing and object recognition [1]; Occipital lobe: visual processing [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-38 -->

Explanation:

**A79.** B. Pons
<!-- id: psych12a1-stru-39 -->

Explanation: The pons lies between the medulla and midbrain and carries signals between the cerebral cortex and the cerebellum. The medulla controls breathing and heart rate, the midbrain is involved in visual and auditory reflexes, and the amygdala is a limbic structure rather than part of the brainstem.

**A80.** B. Fluent speech that makes little sense, and poor understanding of language
<!-- id: vce-psych12-sf-c1 -->

Explanation: Wernicke's area is linked to language comprehension. Broca's area (frontal lobe) is linked to speech production; the cerebellum to coordination.

#### Neuroplasticity and adolescent brain development

**A81.** A. the elimination of weaker, rarely used synaptic connections
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-1 -->

Explanation: Pruning removes less-used synapses to make neural networks more efficient. Strengthening through repeated use is long-term potentiation, and myelination is a separate process.

**A82.** B. a long-lasting strengthening of synaptic connections after repeated stimulation
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-2 -->

Explanation: LTP is the long-lasting increase in synaptic strength that follows repeated activation, underlying learning and memory. The weakening of synapses with disuse is long-term depression (LTD).

**A83.** A. a prefrontal cortex that is still developing, with the limbic system relatively more active
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-3 -->

Explanation: The prefrontal cortex (planning, impulse control) continues developing into the mid-20s, while the limbic system matures earlier. This helps explain greater risk-taking and emotional reactivity in adolescence.

**A84.** A. adaptive plasticity involving rerouting
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-4 -->

Explanation: Adaptive plasticity after injury can involve rerouting, where neural connections are redirected around damaged areas, and sprouting of new connections. Pruning removes unused synapses and LTD weakens them.

**A85.** A. Neurons forming new dendrites and synaptic connections
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-5 -->

Explanation: Sprouting is the growth of new nerve endings and synaptic connections. Elimination of unused connections is pruning; weakening of synapses is LTD.

**A86.** Neuroplasticity is the brain's ability to change its structure and function in response to experience (1); repeated practice repeatedly activates the same neural pathways, strengthening synaptic connections through long-term potentiation (1); new synaptic connections may form (sprouting) while unused connections weaken or are pruned, making the neural network more efficient so skills improve (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-6 -->

Explanation:

**A87.** Changes (any two): synaptic pruning of unused connections; myelination increasing the speed of neural transmission; ongoing development of the prefrontal cortex; limbic system maturing earlier (1 for two changes identified); Link: the prefrontal cortex, responsible for judgement and impulse control, is still developing while the limbic system (reward and emotion) is highly active, so adolescents may be more prone to risk-taking (2).
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-7 -->

Explanation:

**A88.** LTP: long-lasting strengthening of synapses after frequent/repeated stimulation, making transmission more efficient (1); LTD: long-lasting weakening of synaptic connections after infrequent or low stimulation, which can lead to the connection being pruned (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-8 -->

Explanation:

**A89.** A. change its structure and function in response to experience
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-9 -->

Explanation: Neuroplasticity is the capacity of the brain to reorganise its structure and function in response to learning, experience and injury. The idea that connections are fixed after childhood is outdated, and the brain does not rapidly replace large numbers of lost neurons.

**A90.** B. faster transmission of nerve impulses along axons
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-10 -->

Explanation: Myelin is a fatty sheath around axons that speeds up the transmission of nerve impulses, and myelination continues into early adulthood. The reduction in synapses is caused by pruning, while most cortical neurons are generated before birth.

**A91.** C. long-term, intensive spatial navigation is associated with structural change in the hippocampus
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-11 -->

Explanation: The finding shows an association between extensive navigation experience and hippocampal structure, which supports experience-driven plasticity. It does not prove cause and effect on its own, and it does not show that the hippocampus controls movement.

**A92.** D. cross-modal reorganisation in response to altered sensory experience
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-12 -->

Explanation: The visual cortex, normally used for sight, is recruited for touch when vision is absent, which shows plasticity in response to altered experience. A reflex is an automatic, rapid response that does not involve this kind of cortical reorganisation, and myelin loss would not explain the recruitment.

**A93.** A. neural connections that are rarely activated are more likely to be weakened or eliminated
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-13 -->

Explanation: Frequently used connections are strengthened, while rarely used connections are weakened or pruned. Learned skills can decline with disuse, and neurons do not die within a year simply because they are not used every day.

**A94.** B. experience can change the physical structure of the brain
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-14 -->

Explanation: The brains of the enriched rats changed physically in response to their surroundings, which is evidence of experience-dependent plasticity. The other options contradict this finding: structure is not fixed by genes alone, branching increased rather than decreased, and the environment did not remove myelin.

**A95.** C. synaptic pruning and myelination
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-15 -->

Explanation: Pruning removes unused synapses, which reduces grey matter, while myelination adds myelin, which is white matter, to axons. Neurogenesis is largely complete before birth in the cortex, so it cannot explain the adolescent pattern.

**A96.** D. a time of heightened sensitivity to particular experiences, such as exposure to language
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-16 -->

Explanation: During a critical period the brain is especially responsive to particular experiences, such as language exposure in early childhood. Plasticity does not end abruptly at the close of this period; the brain can still change afterwards, usually less readily.

**A97.** A. are more plastic and better able to reorganise neural networks
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-17 -->

Explanation: Younger brains show greater plasticity, so other regions and networks can take over functions after damage. Children do undergo synaptic pruning, and their brains are not fully myelinated, so those options are incorrect.

**A98.** B. maladaptive plasticity, in which reorganisation is linked to persistent pain
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-18 -->

Explanation: Cortical reorganisation that does not restore function and is linked to pain is maladaptive plasticity. The reorganisation cannot restore the missing limb, and long-term potentiation and neurogenesis do not describe this change.

**A99.** C. the strengthening of connections between neurons that are repeatedly active together
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-19 -->

Explanation: Connections between neurons that are repeatedly active at the same time are strengthened, which is one basis for learning and memory. Permanent removal of inactive neurons does not reflect the principle, and myelin is added to, not removed from, frequently used pathways.

**A100.** B. synaptic pruning of rarely used connections
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-20 -->

Explanation: A decline in synapse density across adolescence reflects pruning, which removes connections that are rarely used. Neurogenesis would increase the number of neurons rather than reduce synapses, and myelin wraps axons rather than synapses.

**A101.** C. oligodendrocytes, which form myelin sheaths around axons in the central nervous system
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-21 -->

Explanation: Oligodendrocytes wrap their processes around axons in the brain and spinal cord to form myelin. Schwann cells perform this role in the peripheral nervous system, while astrocytes support neurons and microglia act as immune cells.

**A102.** D. before birth
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-22 -->

Explanation: Most cortical neurons form during prenatal development, so later plasticity mainly involves changes in connections rather than large numbers of new neurons. Neurogenesis is not the main source of cortical neurons during adolescence or adulthood.

**A103.** A. neurogenesis
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-23 -->

Explanation: Neurogenesis is the formation of new neurons, which occurs mainly before birth and to a limited extent in some adult regions. Sprouting refers to growth of new connections between existing neurons, potentiation strengthens synapses, and myelination adds insulation to axons.

**A104.** B. a long period with little new sensory or mental stimulation
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-24 -->

Explanation: Challenging practice, rehabilitation and varied learning environments all promote plasticity, so they are tempting but incorrect answers. A prolonged lack of stimulation tends to reduce the strength and number of neural connections.

**A105.** C. plasticity continues throughout life, although it is generally greatest in childhood
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-25 -->

Explanation: The brain keeps changing throughout life, though young brains are generally more plastic. Plasticity is not limited to the first year of life, to adolescence, or to damaged areas.

**A106.** Frequently used synaptic connections can be strengthened, or new connections can form (sprouting) [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-26 -->

Explanation:

**A107.** The neural pathways for the skill are used less, so their synaptic connections are weakened and some are pruned [1]; without repeated activation the network becomes less efficient, so movements are slower and less accurate [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-27 -->

Explanation:

**A108.** Myelin insulates axons, which speeds the transmission of nerve impulses [1]; faster and more reliable signals between the brain and muscles improve the timing and coordination of the movement [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-28 -->

Explanation:

**A109.** The study is correlational, so it shows an association rather than proving that navigation experience caused the difference [1]; people with stronger spatial abilities may have been more likely to choose taxi driving, so pre-existing differences may explain the result [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-29 -->

Explanation:

**A110.** Adaptive plasticity is change that improves function or helps the brain adjust to experience [1], for example reorganisation that restores a lost ability after injury [1]; maladaptive plasticity is change associated with harmful or unhelpful outcomes [1], for example cortical reorganisation linked to persistent phantom limb pain [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-30 -->

Explanation:

**A111.** Early childhood is a critical (sensitive) period when the brain is especially responsive to language input [1]; children's greater plasticity allows language networks to form readily and efficiently [1]; adults have reduced plasticity and build new connections more slowly, so fluency develops more slowly [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-31 -->

Explanation:

**A112.** Connections that are not stimulated are less likely to be maintained, so they are pruned [1]; the networks that remain are those that were activated, which may limit the range of skills developed [1]; later enriched experiences can partly reorganise these networks, so the effects are not always permanent [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-32 -->

Explanation:

**A113.** Evidence: the prefrontal cortex, which supports planning and impulse control, continues developing into the mid-20s [1]; the limbic system, involved in reward and emotion, is highly responsive during adolescence, which can increase risk-taking [1]; evaluation: the claim is overstated, as adolescents can make good decisions and outcomes depend on context, emotional state and peer presence [1]; neuroplasticity means experience and training can improve decision-making skills, so development is not fixed [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-33 -->

Explanation:

**A114.** Most neurons are generated before birth, and neurogenesis in the adult brain is limited and restricted to a few regions [1]; neurons lost to injury are generally not replaced in large numbers [1]; recovery therefore depends on changes in existing networks, such as sprouting, rerouting and strengthening of surviving synapses [1]; rehabilitation that repeatedly uses the affected function encourages these changes [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-34 -->

Explanation:

**A115.** Neuroplasticity continues across the lifespan, so new synaptic connections can form and existing ones can be strengthened with practice [1]; learning is typically slower than in childhood because plasticity is generally greater earlier in life [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-35 -->

Explanation:

**A116.** C. experience-dependent plasticity during a critical period, in which reduced input from one eye weakens its cortical representation
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-36 -->

Explanation: Early visual experience shapes the visual cortex, and reduced input from one eye during the critical period weakens its connections, which is a form of experience-dependent plasticity. Fixed wiring ignores plasticity, neurogenesis does not replace visual neurons in this way, and a light reflex does not change cortical representation.

**A117.** D. his brain has learned to interpret the new pattern of sensory input, which is a form of neuroplasticity
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-37 -->

Explanation: Neuroplasticity allows the brain to learn to interpret unfamiliar patterns of sensory input, even when they arrive through a different sense. Tongue neurons do not acquire sensitivity to light, myelin loss would slow rather than speed signals, and reflexes do not explain learning to recognise shapes.

**A118.** Lasting changes depend on repeated stimulation that strengthens synapses over time, through long-term potentiation [1]; spacing allows the changes to consolidate between sessions, whereas a single intense session produces only short-lived changes [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-38 -->

Explanation:

**A119.** The study measures an association between practice and brain structure without manipulating practice [1]; the groups may already differ, for example in motor ability or motivation, which could explain the difference [1]; randomly assigning adolescents to a juggling training group or a control group, and scanning both groups before and after training, would test cause and effect [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-39 -->

Explanation:

**A120.** C. The amygdala and reward systems develop earlier than the prefrontal cortex, which matures in the mid-20s
<!-- id: vce-psych12-np-c1 -->

Explanation: Emotional and reward responses are strong while the regions for planning, judgement and impulse control are still developing.

#### Acquired brain injury and neurological disorders

**A121.** D. A stroke caused by a blocked artery
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-1 -->

Explanation: Stroke is a non-traumatic acquired brain injury, as it results from a blockage or bleed. The other options are traumatic brain injuries caused by external force.

**A122.** A. contralateral control of the body by each hemisphere
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-2 -->

Explanation: Each hemisphere mainly controls the opposite (contralateral) side of the body, so left motor cortex damage produces right-sided weakness.

**A123.** D. Dopamine
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-3 -->

Explanation: Parkinson's disease results from degeneration of dopamine-producing neurons, leading to tremor, rigidity and slowed movement.

**A124.** B. Difficulty forming new long-term memories
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-4 -->

Explanation: The hippocampus is critical for forming new explicit long-term memories. Speech production difficulty implies Broca's area, vision loss the occipital lobe, and balance the cerebellum.

**A125.** B. The effects depend on the location and severity of the injury
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-5 -->

Explanation: Effects depend on location and extent, and can include physical, cognitive, emotional and behavioural changes. Neuroplasticity can support partial recovery.

**A126.** Any two with justification: difficulty planning and making decisions; impulsivity or poor judgement; changes in personality or emotional regulation (e.g. irritability, aggression); reduced attention or problem solving; difficulty with voluntary movement (1 mark each).
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-6 -->

Explanation:

**A127.** Neuroplasticity allows the brain to reorganise after damage (1); undamaged areas can take over function through rerouting and sprouting new synaptic connections (1); rehabilitation such as physiotherapy, speech therapy or repeated practice of lost skills strengthens the new pathways (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-7 -->

Explanation:

**A128.** Traumatic: caused by external physical force to the head, e.g. a fall or car accident (1+1 for definition and example); Non-traumatic: caused by internal factors such as a blockage, bleed, infection or lack of oxygen, e.g. stroke or tumour (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-8 -->

Explanation:

**A129.** A. Slow, effortful speech with impaired grammar
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-9 -->

Explanation: Broca's area is needed to produce speech, so damage causes non-fluent expressive aphasia while comprehension is largely preserved. Fluent but meaningless speech with poor comprehension is the pattern of Wernicke's area damage, which is the tempting mistake here.

**A130.** B. Fluent, rapid speech that is often meaningless, with poor comprehension
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-10 -->

Explanation: Wernicke's area in the temporal lobe is needed to understand language, so its damage produces fluent speech that is often nonsensical. Halting speech with good understanding is the reverse of the pattern caused by Broca's area damage.

**A131.** C. GABA
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-11 -->

Explanation: The neurons of the striatum that degenerate in Huntington's disease release GABA, and their loss produces involuntary jerky movements called chorea. Dopamine is the neurotransmitter lost in Parkinson's disease, which is a common confusion.

**A132.** D. Acetylcholine
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-12 -->

Explanation: Cholinergic neurons in the basal forebrain, which supply acetylcholine to the hippocampus and cortex, degenerate early in Alzheimer's disease, and cholinesterase inhibitors are used to raise acetylcholine levels. Dopamine is the neurotransmitter associated with Parkinson's disease rather than with Alzheimer's memory loss.

**A133.** A. Thiamine (vitamin B₁)
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-13 -->

Explanation: Thiamine deficiency damages the thalamus and mammillary bodies, producing severe memory impairment and confabulation. Vitamin C deficiency causes scurvy, which does not produce this type of memory loss.

**A134.** B. It is a mild traumatic brain injury that can occur without loss of consciousness
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-14 -->

Explanation: A concussion is a mild traumatic brain injury that often occurs without loss of consciousness and usually shows no large structural bleed on standard scans. The claim that symptoms always last for years is wrong, because most symptoms resolve within days to weeks.

**A135.** C. Damage occurs at the site of impact and on the opposite side, as the brain strikes the inside of the skull
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-15 -->

Explanation: In a coup-contrecoup injury the brain moves within the skull and is bruised both at the point of impact (coup) and on the opposite side (contrecoup). Damage at the impact site alone is a common mistake, because it does not include the opposite-side injury.

**A136.** D. Rapid acceleration and rotation of the head that stretches and tears axons
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-16 -->

Explanation: Rotational forces shear and tear axons across widespread white matter, producing diffuse damage. A localised clot, a blocked artery or oxygen deprivation each produce different types of injury, so they are common mistakes.

**A137.** A. Anoxic (hypoxic) brain injury
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-17 -->

Explanation: Lack of oxygen can kill neurons across the brain, causing a non-traumatic anoxic brain injury. A traumatic brain injury and a subdural haematoma usually follow a blow to the head, and multiple sclerosis is an immune condition that is not caused by loss of oxygen.

**A138.** B. The myelin sheath
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-18 -->

Explanation: In MS the immune system damages the myelin sheath around axons in the central nervous system, which slows or blocks nerve signals. The cell body and dendrites are not the main target, so they are tempting but incorrect.

**A139.** D. Transferring information between the two cerebral hemispheres
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-19 -->

Explanation: The corpus callosum is the large bundle of nerve fibres connecting the two cerebral hemispheres, so cutting it blocks information transfer between them. The other options depend on the hypothalamus, visual cortex and hippocampus, which the corpus callosum does not directly serve.

**A140.** B. Loss of memories for events that happened before the injury
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-20 -->

Explanation: Retrograde amnesia is the loss of memories formed before the injury, often for events shortly before it. Inability to form new memories describes anterograde amnesia, which is the tempting wrong answer.

**A141.** C. Restraining the unaffected limb so the affected limb is used more intensively
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-21 -->

Explanation: Constraint-induced movement therapy restrains the stronger limb so the weaker, affected limb is used repeatedly, which strengthens the neural pathways controlling it. The other options describe surgery, drug treatment or diet, none of which is the basis of this therapy.

**A142.** D. Ischaemic stroke results from a blocked blood supply, and haemorrhagic stroke from a burst blood vessel
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-22 -->

Explanation: An ischaemic stroke occurs when blood flow is blocked, while a haemorrhagic stroke occurs when a blood vessel ruptures and bleeds into the brain. The reversed option swaps the two definitions, which is a common mistake.

**A143.** A. Loss or impairment of vision
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-23 -->

Explanation: The occipital lobe houses the primary visual cortex, so damage can cause visual loss or impairment. Arm movement is controlled by the motor cortex in the frontal lobe, memory relies on the hippocampus, and balance is coordinated by the cerebellum.

**A144.** B. a tendency to have recurrent seizures caused by sudden, abnormal electrical discharges in the brain
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-24 -->

Explanation: Epilepsy is a neurological disorder in which a person has recurrent seizures caused by abnormal, excessive electrical activity in the brain. The loss of dopamine neurons describes Parkinson's disease, and the jerky movements describe Huntington's disease.

**A145.** C. Progressive difficulty remembering recent events and new information
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-25 -->

Explanation: Difficulty remembering recent events is one of the earliest and most typical symptoms of Alzheimer's disease, due to damage in the hippocampus and cortex. Loss of balance suggests cerebellar damage, and involuntary jerky movements suggest Huntington's disease rather than Alzheimer's.

**A146.** Any two, [1] each: inherited, autosomal dominant genetic disorder; involuntary, jerky movements (chorea) caused by degeneration of neurons in the basal ganglia; progressive cognitive decline and changes in mood or personality.
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-26 -->

Explanation:

**A147.** Amyloid-β plaques build up between neurons, interfering with synaptic signalling and triggering inflammation [1]; tau tangles form inside neurons and disrupt the internal transport of nutrients along axons [1]; the resulting neuronal death causes brain atrophy and memory loss [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-27 -->

Explanation:

**A148.** Visual agnosia [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-28 -->

Explanation:

**A149.** The left hemisphere is usually dominant for language, so a left-sided stroke may cause aphasia, such as difficulty speaking or understanding [1]; the right hemisphere is more involved in spatial awareness and attention, so right-sided damage may cause neglect of the left side of space [1]; each hemisphere controls the opposite side of the body, so a left stroke causes right-sided weakness and a right stroke causes left-sided weakness [1]; the size and location of the damage determine the severity and the specific deficits [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-29 -->

Explanation:

**A150.** Their damaged memory system cannot accurately store or retrieve past events [1]; the brain fills the gaps with plausible but false information, which the person may believe is true [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-30 -->

Explanation:

**A151.** Each concussion can disrupt neuronal function and cause further damage before the brain has recovered from the previous one [1]; repeated injury can lead to longer-lasting cognitive, emotional or behavioural problems [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-31 -->

Explanation:

**A152.** Levodopa crosses the blood-brain barrier and is converted into dopamine in the brain, which relieves movement symptoms [1]; it replaces some of the lost dopamine rather than stopping the degeneration of dopamine neurons [1]; the neurons continue to die, so the disease progresses and treatment does not cure it [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-32 -->

Explanation:

**A153.** A group of neurons produces sudden, excessive and abnormal electrical discharges [1]; the abnormal activity spreads or synchronises across brain regions, disrupting normal function and causing altered consciousness, movement or sensation [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-33 -->

Explanation:

**A154.** Neurons need a continuous oxygen supply and begin to die within minutes without it [1]; reduced oxygen affects the whole brain rather than a single localised area, so damage is diffuse [1]; the hippocampus and cortex are particularly vulnerable, which can cause memory and attention deficits [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-34 -->

Explanation:

**A155.** Any two, [1] each: brain tumour; infection such as encephalitis or meningitis; toxic exposure, such as long-term alcohol or drug misuse.
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-35 -->

Explanation:

**A156.** Myelin is the fatty insulating sheath around axons that allows impulses to travel quickly by jumping between nodes [1]; in MS the immune system attacks and destroys myelin, a process called demyelination [1]; without myelin, signals are slowed or blocked, causing symptoms such as weakness, numbness, fatigue or vision problems [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-36 -->

Explanation:

**A157.** Repeated structured practice of word-finding or naming tasks [1]; melodic intonation therapy, which uses singing and rhythm to engage intact right-hemisphere areas [1]; neuroplasticity allows repeated practice to strengthen existing or newly formed connections [1]; reorganisation can shift some language function to undamaged regions, including the right hemisphere [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-37 -->

Explanation:

**A158.** C. Changes in personality, planning and self-control
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-38 -->

Explanation: The frontal lobe supports planning, judgement and self-control, so a growing tumour there often changes personality and behaviour. Colour vision and face recognition relate to the occipital and temporal lobes, and breathing is controlled by the medulla, so those options are less likely.

**A159.** A stroke is caused by a sudden blockage or bleed that deprives brain cells of oxygen, so neurons die within minutes or hours [1]; Alzheimer's disease involves progressive degeneration of neurons and connections over years, so symptoms emerge slowly [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-acqu-39 -->

Explanation:

**A160.** A. Loss of dopamine-producing neurons in the substantia nigra
<!-- id: vce-psych12-ab2-c1 -->

Explanation: Low dopamine disrupts the circuits that control smooth voluntary movement. Bursts of abnormal electrical activity describe epilepsy.

### Unit 1 AOS 2: What influences psychological development?

#### Nature, nurture and the biopsychosocial approach

**A161.** A. nature
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-1 -->

Explanation: Nature refers to inherited genetic and biological factors. Nurture refers to environmental influences such as parenting, culture and experience.

**A162.** C. behaviour results from the interaction of biological, psychological and social factors
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-2 -->

Explanation: The biopsychosocial approach treats biological, psychological and social factors as interacting influences on behaviour and mental processes.

**A163.** C. Pattern of negative thinking
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-3 -->

Explanation: Psychological factors include thinking, emotion, coping and learning. Genetics and neurotransmitters are biological; peer influence is social.

**A164.** C. genetics
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-4 -->

Explanation: Identical (monozygotic) twins share 100% of their genes, so similarity despite different environments suggests genetic influence. Twin studies cannot rule out all environmental effects.

**A165.** C. biological, psychological and social factors can interact
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-5 -->

Explanation: Both biological predisposition and environmental stressors contributed, consistent with the biopsychosocial approach.

**A166.** Nature: genetic and biological factors that influence development, e.g. inherited capacity for language/brain structures (1); Nurture: environmental influences, e.g. exposure to language, interaction with caregivers (1); both interact, as a child needs both the biological capacity and exposure to language to develop speech (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-6 -->

Explanation:

**A167.** Biological: genetic predisposition, imbalance of neurotransmitters, or hormonal changes (1); Psychological: negative thought patterns, low self-esteem or poor coping skills (1); Social: bullying, family conflict, social isolation or lack of support (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-7 -->

Explanation:

**A168.** Identical twins raised together share both genes and environment, so it is hard to separate the two influences (1); twins raised apart are rare, giving small samples, and they may still share prenatal or similar environments, limiting generalisability (1). Accept another valid limitation.
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-8 -->

Explanation:

**A169.** A. their biological parents and their adoptive parents
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-9 -->

Explanation: Similarity to biological parents points to genetic influence, while similarity to adoptive parents points to environmental influence. Comparing only with adoptive parents ignores genes, which is the common mistake of treating the home environment as the sole cause.

**A170.** B. maturation, a genetically programmed sequence of physical development
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-10 -->

Explanation: Maturation is the biologically programmed unfolding of development with age, which occurs in a similar sequence and timing across children. The other options are nurture influences, and the scenario rules them out as the main cause.

**A171.** C. socialisation through cultural learning, a form of nurture
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-11 -->

Explanation: Cultural norms are passed on to children through everyday routines and modelling, which makes this an environmental (nurture) influence. Genetic inheritance and maturation are nature influences, and a neurotransmitter imbalance is a biological factor rather than a learned one.

**A172.** D. a genetic influence on the trait
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-12 -->

Explanation: Identical twins share virtually all their genes and non-identical twins share about half, so a higher identical concordance points to a genetic contribution. Twin data do not show that genes fully determine a trait, or that the environment plays no role.

**A173.** A. family conflict: social; low self-esteem: psychological; hormone levels: biological
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-13 -->

Explanation: Family conflict is a social factor, low self-esteem is a psychological factor, and hormone levels are biological. Each distractor swaps at least one category, which is a common error when classifying influences.

**A174.** B. being taught to use a knife and fork by a parent at mealtimes
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-14 -->

Explanation: Being taught skills by a parent is an environmental learning experience, which is nurture. The other options describe inherited or biologically timed characteristics, which are nature influences.

**A175.** C. several interacting factors, such as disrupted sleep, self-critical thinking and reduced contact with friends
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-15 -->

Explanation: The biopsychosocial approach explains low mood as the interaction of biological, psychological and social influences. Each single-cause option is a reductionist explanation, which the approach rejects as incomplete.

**A176.** D. explaining all anxiety disorders purely in terms of genes
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-16 -->

Explanation: Reductionism explains a complex phenomenon using only one level of analysis, and attributing all anxiety disorders to genes does exactly that. The other options each describe a multi-level, holistic view, which is the opposite of reductionism.

**A177.** A. a critical period in which language exposure is needed for normal development
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-17 -->

Explanation: The lasting difficulty after missing early exposure suggests a critical period, a limited window in which environmental input is needed. Maturation alone cannot explain the outcome, because the scenario shows that the environment is required.

**A178.** B. 50%
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-18 -->

Explanation: Non-identical twins develop from two separate eggs and share, on average, about half of their genes, like ordinary siblings. Identical (monozygotic) twins share virtually all their genes, which is why 100% is a tempting but incorrect answer here.

**A179.** C. Development results from the ongoing interaction of inherited and environmental factors
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-19 -->

Explanation: Genes are expressed in response to environmental conditions, and experiences shape how inherited potential develops, so the two continually interact. The last option is a common mistake that treats development as a fixed nature phase followed by a nurture phase.

**A180.** D. assessing physical symptoms, stress and coping, and family and work circumstances together
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-20 -->

Explanation: The biopsychosocial model assesses biological, psychological and social influences together, and the correct option does this. The first three options each address only one level, which is reductionist even when the chosen level is relevant.

**A181.** A. biological factors
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-21 -->

Explanation: Hormone levels are a physiological (biological) influence on mood and sleep, even though stress triggers them. Stress appraisal would be psychological and a difficult family situation would be social, but the hormonal change itself is biological.

**A182.** B. observational learning, a form of nurture
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-22 -->

Explanation: The child acquired the fear by observing a model, which is observational learning from the environment (nurture). An inherited temperament might influence how readily a child fears things, but the scenario shows this specific fear was learned from watching the brother.

**A183.** C. exposure to alcohol in the womb
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-23 -->

Explanation: Exposure to alcohol in the womb is an environmental influence acting before birth. Inherited eye colour is a genetic influence, and the other two options occur after birth.

**A184.** A. a long-running question about how much inherited and environmental factors contribute to development
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-24 -->

Explanation: The nature-nurture debate asks how much inherited and environmental factors contribute to development, and most psychologists now see them as interacting rather than competing. The other options misstate the debate, for example by treating it as settled or as a question about the timing of development.

**A185.** B. nature and nurture interacting, so that inherited potential develops through environmental input
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-25 -->

Explanation: Inherited potential is realised only through environmental experience, which shows nature and nurture interacting. The distractors each treat one influence as acting alone, and the option claiming the lessons created the ability ignores the genetic tendency given in the scenario.

**A186.** C. checking vision and hearing, building reading strategies and confidence, and working with family and teachers to stop the teasing
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-26 -->

Explanation: The biopsychosocial approach addresses biological (for example vision and hearing), psychological (confidence and reading skills) and social (teasing, family and school) influences together. The single-level options are reductionist, and the school-move option ignores social factors that can be changed.

**A187.** Socialisation is the process by which people learn the behaviours, values and norms of their culture or community through interaction with others [1]; e.g. a child learning to take turns during a game with friends [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-27 -->

Explanation:

**A188.** A consistent, early-emerging pattern of emotional and behavioural reactivity to the environment [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-28 -->

Explanation:

**A189.** Socioeconomic status is a social factor reflecting family income, parental education and neighbourhood resources [1]; lower income can increase family stress and reduce access to enriching activities [1]; parental stress may reduce responsive parenting, affecting the child's emotional development [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-29 -->

Explanation:

**A190.** Biological: disrupted sleep or raised stress hormones such as cortisol [1]; Psychological: catastrophic thinking about failing, or a fear-of-failure belief [1]; Social: high pressure from parents or school, or limited support from friends [1]; Interaction: poor sleep reduces concentration and recall, which increases self-critical thinking, which intensifies anxiety [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-30 -->

Explanation:

**A191.** Depression usually results from several interacting biological, psychological and social influences [1]; a single-cause explanation ignores these factors and may lead to incomplete understanding or to treatment that addresses only one of them [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-31 -->

Explanation:

**A192.** Nurture: an environmental influence on development [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-32 -->

Explanation:

**A193.** A heritable trait is one whose variation is influenced by genes passed from parents to offspring [1]; a trait present at birth may instead be caused by the prenatal environment, such as infection during pregnancy, so being present at birth does not mean a trait is heritable [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-33 -->

Explanation:

**A194.** Effective coping strategies, such as problem-solving or seeking support, reduce the impact of the stressor on mood and wellbeing [1]; poor coping may increase distress and make the person more vulnerable to a mental health problem [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-34 -->

Explanation:

**A195.** The biopsychosocial approach considers biological, psychological and social factors together, whereas a purely biological model considers only physical causes [1]; biological: airway inflammation or an inherited tendency to asthma [1]; psychological: stress from exam pressure [1]; social: exposure to smoke in the household [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-35 -->

Explanation:

**A196.** Children's genes cannot be randomly assigned or manipulated [1]; deliberately creating harmful or deprived environments would be unethical [1]; researchers therefore rely on naturally occurring groups such as twins and adoptees, which contain confounding variables [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-36 -->

Explanation:

**A197.** Adoption agencies often place children in homes similar in socioeconomic status, which reduces the range of environments studied [1]; the early environment with the biological mother, including prenatal conditions, is not controlled, so genetic and environmental effects cannot be fully separated [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-37 -->

Explanation:

**A198.** Nurture: reading to a child is an environmental influence [1]; correlation does not establish cause, because children who are read to may differ in other ways, such as family income [1]; another factor the study should consider is the child's inherited verbal ability [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-38 -->

Explanation:

**A199.** C. About 60 per cent of the variation in the trait among people in that population is associated with genetic differences
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-39 -->

Explanation: Heritability describes the proportion of variation across a population that is associated with genetic differences, not how much of any one person's trait is genetic. It does not mean the environment has no effect, and it does not predict inheritance in individual offspring.

**A200.** A. Genetic predisposition; self-esteem; peer group
<!-- id: vce-psych12-nn-c1 -->

Explanation: Biological factors involve the body; psychological factors involve thoughts, feelings and beliefs; social factors involve the environment and relationships.

#### Emotional development and attachment

**A201.** A. insecure-avoidant
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-1 -->

Explanation: Insecure-avoidant infants show little distress at separation and avoid the caregiver on reunion. Insecure-resistant infants are very distressed at separation and are not comforted on return.

**A202.** B. uses the caregiver as a secure base, is distressed when they leave and is easily comforted on return
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-2 -->

Explanation: Secure infants explore using the caregiver as a secure base, are upset when separated, and are quickly comforted when the caregiver returns.

**A203.** D. spent more time clinging to the cloth mother, suggesting contact comfort is important for attachment
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-3 -->

Explanation: The monkeys spent most of their time with the cloth mother and went to the wire mother only to feed, showing that attachment is based on more than feeding. Note the ethical concerns raised by the study.

**A204.** A. Infant monkeys experienced significant psychological harm through deprivation
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-4 -->

Explanation: The infant monkeys suffered long-term emotional harm from maternal deprivation, raising concerns about harm to animals.

**A205.** B. A caregiver who responds sensitively and consistently to the infant's needs
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-5 -->

Explanation: Sensitive, consistent caregiving builds the infant's trust that needs will be met, which underpins secure attachment.

**A206.** Infant and caregiver are placed in an unfamiliar room with toys, with a series of episodes in which the caregiver and a stranger enter and leave, separating and reuniting the infant and caregiver (1+1); observers record the infant's exploration, reaction to separation, reaction to the stranger and behaviour on reunion to classify attachment type (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-6 -->

Explanation:

**A207.** Insecure-avoidant: little distress when the caregiver leaves, avoids or ignores the caregiver on return (1); Insecure-resistant: highly distressed on separation, seeks contact on return but also resists or is angry and is not easily comforted (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-7 -->

Explanation:

**A208.** Secure attachment (1); distress at separation, quick comfort on return and exploration using the caregiver as a secure base (1); Limitation: a single observation or behaviour in one context may not reflect attachment, and temperament or cultural differences may influence behaviour (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-8 -->

Explanation:

**A209.** A. Infants are biologically predisposed to form attachments with caregivers because this increases their chance of survival
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-9 -->

Explanation: Bowlby proposed that attachment is an innate behaviour that evolved to keep infants close to a protective caregiver, improving survival. The feeding option describes learning theory, which proposes that attachment is learned through reinforcement.

**A210.** B. Infants form one primary attachment that is special and qualitatively different from other attachments
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-10 -->

Explanation: Monotropy refers to the idea that a primary attachment figure holds a special place in the infant's life, and this attachment is qualitatively different from others. The first option is wrong because infants go on to form multiple attachments, so monotropy does not mean a single lifelong attachment.

**A211.** C. indiscriminate attachment
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-11 -->

Explanation: In indiscriminate attachment, infants respond positively to many people but do not yet show a clear preference for one caregiver. Specific attachment develops later, at about 7 months, and separation anxiety describes distress when a caregiver leaves rather than a stage of attachment.

**A212.** D. stranger anxiety, which typically emerges around 7 to 9 months
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-12 -->

Explanation: Stranger anxiety shows that the infant can tell familiar from unfamiliar people, and it typically appears around 7 to 9 months once a specific attachment has formed. The disorganised pattern is a Strange Situation classification, not a typical everyday reaction to a stranger.

**A213.** A. occurs during a short critical period and has a biological basis
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-13 -->

Explanation: Imprinting happens rapidly during a short critical period after hatching, which suggests attachment has a biological basis. The feeding option describes learning theory, but Lorenz's goslings followed whatever moving object they first saw, not necessarily one that provided food.

**A214.** B. The caregiver provides food, which reduces hunger, so the infant comes to associate the caregiver with comfort
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-14 -->

Explanation: Learning theory, sometimes called the cupboard-love explanation, proposes that attachment develops through feeding and the reduction of hunger. The genetic option describes Bowlby's evolutionary account, while the first-seen option describes imprinting, which Lorenz linked to a biological readiness rather than to learning.

**A215.** C. 6 to 8 weeks
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-15 -->

Explanation: Social smiling generally emerges at 6 to 8 weeks and is a response to people, which helps build interaction with caregivers. Newborns do smile at birth, but these smiles are usually reflexive and not directed at people.

**A216.** D. 18 to 24 months
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-16 -->

Explanation: Self-conscious emotions require a sense of self, which develops at around 18 to 24 months alongside self-recognition. Basic emotions such as happiness, sadness and anger appear much earlier, in infancy.

**A217.** A. co-regulation of emotion by the caregiver
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-17 -->

Explanation: Co-regulation is when a caregiver helps a child manage emotional arousal, and over time the child gradually takes on this skill as self-regulation. Self-regulation is not fully developed by 12 months, so the child still depends on the caregiver's help at this age.

**A218.** B. variation in attachment classification within a culture was greater than the variation between cultures
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-18 -->

Explanation: The meta-analysis found that differences among individuals within each culture were larger than differences between cultures, so attachment patterns cannot be assumed from nationality alone. The option claiming culture has no influence is wrong because the study did find some differences between cultural groups.

**A219.** B. how sensitively and promptly the caregiver responded to the infant's signals
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-19 -->

Explanation: Schaffer and Emerson found that attachment strength depended more on responsiveness to the infant's signals than on routine physical care such as feeding. This finding challenged learning theory's view that attachment is based on feeding.

**A220.** D. disorganised attachment
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-20 -->

Explanation: Disorganised attachment, identified by Main and Solomon, involves contradictory or disoriented behaviour such as approaching while looking away or freezing. Avoidant and resistant infants show more consistent strategies rather than these contradictions, so both are wrong.

**A221.** A. a set of expectations about self and others, formed through early caregiving experiences, that guides later relationships
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-21 -->

Explanation: The internal working model is a mental representation built from early attachment experiences, and it shapes how a person expects others to respond in later relationships. The genetic option is wrong because the model is shaped by experience and can change over time.

**A222.** B. substantial recovery, with children adopted earlier generally doing better than those adopted later
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-22 -->

Explanation: The English and Romanian Adoptees study found that many children recovered well after adoption, and earlier adoption was linked to better outcomes. This supports the idea of a sensitive period rather than a strict cut-off, because children adopted later still showed more persistent difficulties.

**A223.** D. culturally learned rules about which emotions may be shown, and when and how
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-23 -->

Explanation: Display rules are culturally learned norms that guide when and how people show their emotions, and children gradually learn them. The first option describes universal facial expressions, which is a different idea studied by Ekman.

**A224.** A. some basic emotional facial expressions are recognised across cultures, suggesting a biological basis
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-24 -->

Explanation: Ekman found that people from very different cultures could recognise several basic facial expressions, suggesting some emotional expressions are biologically based. The cultural learning option is tempting because display rules do vary across cultures, but the evidence points to some universal components.

**A225.** Bowlby proposed that attachment is an innate behaviour that evolved to keep infants close to protective caregivers and so improve survival [1]; learning theory proposes that attachment is learned through association with feeding and reinforcement [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-25 -->

Explanation:

**A226.** Infants progress from indiscriminate attachment from about 6 weeks to a specific attachment, usually to a primary caregiver, by around 7 months [1]; many then form multiple attachments [1]; attachment strength was linked to the caregiver's responsiveness rather than to feeding, which challenges the view that attachment is based on feeding [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-26 -->

Explanation:

**A227.** An internal working model is a mental representation of self and others, built from early caregiving experiences, that sets expectations about how relationships will work [1]; these expectations guide how a person interprets and behaves in later relationships, such as friendships and romantic partnerships [1]; one method is the Adult Attachment Interview, which asks adults about their childhood experiences [1]; Main, Kaplan and Cassidy (1985) found that parents' Adult Attachment Interview classifications predicted the attachment type of their infants in the Strange Situation [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-27 -->

Explanation:

**A228.** Stranger anxiety shows the infant can distinguish familiar from unfamiliar people [1]; distress towards strangers, especially when the familiar caregiver is not present, shows that a specific attachment to that caregiver has formed [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-28 -->

Explanation:

**A229.** Primary emotions, such as happiness, sadness, fear and anger, appear in infancy [1]; self-conscious emotions, such as embarrassment, pride and guilt, emerge later, around 18 to 24 months, and depend on a sense of self [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-29 -->

Explanation:

**A230.** The caregiver helps calm the infant's distress, so the infant's emotional arousal is managed through co-regulation [1]; repeated experiences teach the infant that distress is followed by comfort, building expectations that needs will be met [1]; over time the infant gradually develops self-regulation and manages emotions independently [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-30 -->

Explanation:

**A231.** Strength: the standardised, repeatable procedure allows researchers to compare attachment classifications across countries [1]; a large meta-analysis of studies from many countries (van IJzendoorn and Kroonenberg, 1988) was possible because the same procedure was used [1]. Limitation: the procedure takes place in an unfamiliar laboratory setting, which may not reflect how infants behave at home [1]; behaviours labelled insecure in one culture may be normal in another, so classifications may reflect cultural expectations rather than the quality of attachment [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-31 -->

Explanation:

**A232.** A sensitive period is a time in early childhood when an infant is most ready to form an attachment [1]; if attachment has not formed by the end of this period, forming one later may be more difficult [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-32 -->

Explanation:

**A233.** The caregiver acts as a secure base, a safe point from which the toddler can explore the environment [1]; the toddler ventures away but returns for comfort or reassurance when frightened [1]; knowing the caregiver is available allows the toddler to explore with confidence, for example playing on a playground and returning to the parent for reassurance after a fall [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-33 -->

Explanation:

**A234.** The social smile is directed at people and typically appears around 6 to 8 weeks, unlike the reflexive smiles of newborns [1]; it encourages caregivers to respond and interact, strengthening the bond between infant and caregiver [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-34 -->

Explanation:
