**A235.** Early secure attachment builds an internal working model with positive expectations about relationships [1]; learning to regulate emotions helps children cope with friendships and social situations [1]; limitation: later experiences, such as changes in family circumstances, may explain outcomes [1]; this makes it hard to show that early emotional development caused later relationships, because the link cannot be separated from other influences [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-35 -->

Explanation:

**A236.** B. social referencing
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-36 -->

Explanation: Social referencing is the infant's use of a caregiver's emotional expression to guide its response to an ambiguous situation. Stranger anxiety is distress towards unfamiliar people, a secure base describes using the caregiver as a base for exploration, and imprinting is an innate, rapid following response seen in birds.

**A237.** A. The emergence of early empathy, a response to another person's emotional state
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-37 -->

Explanation: Early empathy and prosocial acts appear in the second year as toddlers respond to others' distress. Pride is a self-conscious emotion about one's own achievement rather than concern for another person, a reflex is an automatic response, and object permanence is a cognitive skill about hidden objects.

**A238.** A strategy such as turning away from the frustrating task, seeking comfort from a caregiver or using a toy to distract themselves [1]; adults can help by naming the feeling, modelling calm responses and offering support so the child gradually manages emotions independently [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-38 -->

Explanation:

**A239.** Naming emotions helps children label what they feel, so the emotion is understood rather than overwhelming them [1]; recognising emotions in others allows the child to predict others' responses and adjust their own behaviour [1]; this emotional understanding supports the use of regulation strategies, such as calming down or asking for help [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-39 -->

Explanation:

**A240.** A. Insecure-resistant (ambivalent)
<!-- id: vce-psych12-ea-c1 -->

Explanation: Secure infants are comforted quickly on reunion; avoidant infants ignore the caregiver; disorganised infants show confused, contradictory behaviour.

#### Cognitive development and Piaget

**A241.** A. object permanence
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-1 -->

Explanation: Object permanence is understanding that objects continue to exist when out of sight, developing during the sensorimotor stage (birth to about 2 years).

**A242.** B. conservation
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-2 -->

Explanation: Failure to conserve volume is typical of the preoperational stage, as the child centres on one feature (height) and does not understand that quantity stays the same despite changes in appearance.

**A243.** D. Sensorimotor, preoperational, concrete operational, formal operational
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-3 -->

Explanation: The stages are sensorimotor (0-2), preoperational (2-7), concrete operational (7-11) and formal operational (11+).

**A244.** C. formal operational stage
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-4 -->

Explanation: Abstract and hypothetical reasoning marks the formal operational stage. Concrete operational thinking is logical but tied to concrete objects and events.

**A245.** C. accommodation
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-5 -->

Explanation: Accommodation involves changing or creating a schema to fit new information. Assimilation is fitting new information into existing schemas, as when the child first called the cat 'doggy'.

**A246.** Egocentrism is the inability to see a situation from another person's perspective (1); Example: a child holding a picture up so only they can see it while describing it to a parent, or assuming others see the same view they do (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-6 -->

Explanation:

**A247.** Assimilation: fitting new information into an existing schema (1); Accommodation: modifying an existing schema or creating a new one when new information doesn't fit (1); Example: a child with a 'bird' schema sees a bat, first calls it a bird (assimilation), then adjusts the schema or forms a new one for bat (accommodation) (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-7 -->

Explanation:

**A248.** Piaget may have underestimated children's abilities because tasks were complex or language-heavy, and later research shows younger children can succeed on simplified tasks (1+1). Accept other valid limitations such as the stages being less rigid than proposed or a lack of focus on social and cultural influences.
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-8 -->

Explanation:

**A249.** A. secondary circular reaction
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-9 -->

Explanation: Kicking to make an object move involves an object and produces an effect in the environment, which is a secondary circular reaction. A primary circular reaction repeats an action on the infant's own body, such as thumb sucking, so it is a tempting but wrong answer.

**A250.** B. symbolic play
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-10 -->

Explanation: Using one object to represent another is symbolic function, which emerges in the preoperational stage. Conservation of number and reversibility are concrete operational skills the child has not yet developed, so they are tempting but wrong.

**A251.** C. reversibility
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-11 -->

Explanation: Reversibility is the ability to mentally undo an action and return to the starting point, a key concrete operational skill. Centration, focusing on one feature only, is typical of the preoperational stage, so it is a tempting but wrong answer.

**A252.** A. animism
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-12 -->

Explanation: Attributing human feelings and intentions to inanimate objects is animism, a common belief in the preoperational stage. Centration is focusing on a single feature, which describes a different preoperational tendency, so it is tempting but wrong.

**A253.** B. hypothetico-deductive reasoning
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-13 -->

Explanation: Generating a hypothesis and testing it by systematically controlling variables is hypothetico-deductive reasoning, which requires formal operational thought. Transductive reasoning links two specific events without logical principles and is typical of preoperational children, so it is tempting but wrong.

**A254.** C. decentration
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-14 -->

Explanation: Considering several dimensions at once is decentration, which develops in the concrete operational stage. Centration, focusing on only one dimension, is the preoperational tendency that is tempting but wrong here.

**A255.** D. Working out what would follow from a contrary-to-fact premise, such as 'If Earth had two moons...'
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-15 -->

Explanation: Concrete operational children reason logically about real, observable objects and events but struggle with hypothetical, contrary-to-fact premises, which need formal operational thinking. Sorting by two features, ordering and conservation are all concrete operational skills, so they are tempting but wrong.

**A256.** A. deferred imitation
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-16 -->

Explanation: Imitating a model's action after a delay is deferred imitation, which Piaget linked to the emerging ability to form mental representations near the end of the sensorimotor stage. Symbolic play involves pretending but no copying of an earlier action, so it is tempting but wrong.

**A257.** B. a mental framework used to organise and interpret experience, which changes with development
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-17 -->

Explanation: A schema is a mental framework or pattern of knowledge that organises and interprets experience, and it becomes more complex as development progresses. A fixed trait or an unchanging reflex describes something other than a schema, and balancing knowledge is equilibration, so those options are tempting but wrong.

**A258.** C. the drive to resolve cognitive conflict by adjusting schemas when new information does not fit
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-18 -->

Explanation: Equilibration is the process of resolving cognitive conflict when new information does not fit existing schemas, by adjusting them. Egocentrism and classification describe other concepts, and reversibility is a single mental skill rather than the balancing process, so those options are tempting but wrong.

**A259.** D. It assumed the same stage ages apply to all children, and cross-cultural research has found variation
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-19 -->

Explanation: Piaget's stage ages have been challenged by cross-cultural and later research, which shows variation in when children reach each stage. Piaget's influence and his view that children think differently from adults are strengths, and he did not claim logical reasoning from birth, so those options are tempting but wrong.

**A260.** A. combinatorial reasoning
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-20 -->

Explanation: Systematically listing all possible combinations is combinatorial reasoning, a formal operational skill. Transductive reasoning links specific events in a preoperational way, so it is tempting but wrong.

**A261.** B. transductive reasoning
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-21 -->

Explanation: Linking two events because they occurred together, without a logical principle, is transductive reasoning, typical of the preoperational stage. Inductive and deductive reasoning are logical forms that involve general principles, and combinatorial reasoning is a formal operational skill, so those options are tempting but wrong.

**A262.** C. egocentrism
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-22 -->

Explanation: Choosing the view from their own position shows egocentrism, the inability to take another person's perspective, which is typical of the preoperational stage. Conservation of mass and reversibility are concrete operational concepts, so they are tempting but wrong.

**A263.** A. class inclusion (hierarchical classification)
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-23 -->

Explanation: A preoperational child centres on the subclass and cannot hold the whole class and its parts in mind together, which shows class inclusion has not yet developed. Animism and egocentrism are preoperational features unrelated to this task, and reversibility is a concrete operational skill that would not explain the answer, so those options are tempting but wrong.

**A264.** B. Active exploration and the resolution of cognitive conflict
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-24 -->

Explanation: Piaget saw children as active learners who build knowledge through exploration and who are driven to resolve cognitive conflict through equilibration. Direct adult teaching and reinforcement-based language learning are views Piaget did not regard as the primary driver, and maturation alone ignores the role of experience, so those options are tempting but wrong.

**A265.** C. an internalised, reversible mental action that can be organised into logical systems
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-25 -->

Explanation: An operation is an internalised mental action that is reversible and can be organised into logical systems, which underpins concrete operational thinking. A physical action on an object, a reflex and an emotional response are not operations, so those options are tempting but wrong.

**A266.** A primary circular reaction repeats an action on the infant's own body, e.g. repeatedly sucking the thumb [1]; a secondary circular reaction repeats an action on an object to produce an effect in the environment, e.g. repeatedly shaking a rattle to hear the sound [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-26 -->

Explanation:

**A267.** New information that conflicts with existing schemas creates cognitive disequilibrium [1]; the child uses assimilation and accommodation to resolve the conflict [1]; this produces a new, more stable state of understanding (equilibrium) that moves development to a higher level [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-27 -->

Explanation:

**A268.** Children form and test their own theories about the world, much like scientists [1]; they actively explore and revise their schemas when evidence does not fit their current understanding [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-28 -->

Explanation:

**A269.** Concrete operational thinking applies logic to concrete, observable objects and events [1], e.g. ordering objects by weight to work out which is heaviest [1]; formal operational thinking applies logic to abstract and hypothetical ideas and possibilities [1], e.g. reasoning about how a society would change if a rule were removed, or testing a hypothesis in a science experiment [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-29 -->

Explanation:

**A270.** Use hands-on, concrete materials so children can manipulate objects and test ideas [1]; include activities involving reversibility and conservation, such as pouring liquids or rearranging counters [1]; introduce abstract concepts only after concrete examples, rather than expecting hypothetical reasoning [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-30 -->

Explanation:

**A271.** Deductive reasoning [1]; it is valid because the conclusion must follow logically from the premises: if every student who passes has studied and Mia did not study, then Mia cannot have passed [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-31 -->

Explanation:

**A272.** Each stage involves a different kind of reasoning, not simply more information [1]; each stage builds on and reorganises the structures of the previous stage rather than adding to them [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-32 -->

Explanation:

**A273.** The child sees a quantity transformed in appearance, and is then asked whether the amount is still the same [1]; a child who says it is the same and gives a reason such as 'nothing was added or taken away' or 'it can be changed back' shows conservation [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-33 -->

Explanation:

**A274.** The theory emphasises that children are active learners and that teaching should match their stage of thinking [1]; this helps teachers design hands-on activities suited to the child's level rather than expecting abstract reasoning too early [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-34 -->

Explanation:

**A275.** Sensorimotor thinking is based on physical actions and sensory experience, whereas preoperational thinking involves the use of symbols and mental representations [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-35 -->

Explanation:

**A276.** Seriation, the systematic ordering of items along a dimension such as length, is not yet developed in preoperational children [1]; the child cannot yet mentally compare and organise all the sticks, so the arrangement is random [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-36 -->

Explanation:

**A277.** B. artificialism, because natural events are believed to be made by people
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-37 -->

Explanation: Artificialism is the preoperational belief that natural phenomena are created by humans or machines. Animism is attributing life to inanimate objects, egocentrism is difficulty seeing another person's perspective, and conservation is understanding that quantity stays the same.

**A278.** D. accommodation, because she modifies her existing schema to fit the new situation
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-38 -->

Explanation: Accommodation occurs when existing schemas must change to deal with new experiences, as with the altered grip and movement here. Assimilation would mean using the same spoon scheme unchanged, which does not fit the sticky porridge, while centration and reversibility do not describe a change in schema.

**A279.** A 12-year-old is in the formal operational stage, so can reason abstractly and systematically test combinations of variables [1]; a 7-year-old is in the concrete operational stage, so reasoning is tied to concrete, observable objects and does not yet extend to systematic testing of many variables [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-39 -->

Explanation:

**A280.** C. Pre-operational stage, because she cannot yet conserve
<!-- id: vce-psych12-pg-c1 -->

Explanation: Conservation (understanding quantity stays the same when appearance changes) develops in the concrete operational stage, around 7–11 years.

#### Psychosocial development and Erikson

**A281.** C. identity versus role confusion
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-1 -->

Explanation: In adolescence the crisis is identity versus role confusion, in which individuals explore who they are. Intimacy versus isolation follows in young adulthood.

**A282.** B. Trust versus mistrust
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-2 -->

Explanation: Trust versus mistrust occurs in the first year or so of life and is resolved through reliable, responsive care.

**A283.** C. autonomy versus shame and doubt
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-3 -->

Explanation: Autonomy versus shame and doubt (toddlerhood) concerns developing independence. Criticism for attempts at independence leads to shame and doubt. Initiative versus guilt typically applies from about age 3-5 to planning and starting activities.

**A284.** B. It recognises that development continues across the lifespan
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-4 -->

Explanation: Erikson's eight stages cover the whole lifespan. A criticism is that it is difficult to test scientifically and the stages are less rigid than described.

**A285.** C. a sense of industry
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-5 -->

Explanation: The school-age crisis is industry versus inferiority. Encouragement and success lead to a sense of competence (industry).

**A286.** A psychosocial crisis is a conflict between the individual's psychological needs and the demands of society at each stage (1); successful resolution leads to a psychological strength or virtue (e.g. hope, will) (1); unsuccessful resolution may make later crises harder to resolve, though earlier crises can be revisited (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A287.** The teenager is in the identity versus role confusion stage (1); exploring different roles, values and peer groups helps form a sense of self (1); if this goes well, a stable identity develops, otherwise they may experience role confusion and uncertainty about who they are (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A288.** Piaget focuses on cognitive development (how thinking and reasoning develop through stages) (1); Erikson focuses on psychosocial development, the development of identity and social relationships through crises across the lifespan (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A289.** C. Intimacy versus isolation
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-9 -->

Explanation: Intimacy versus isolation occurs in young adulthood, and successful resolution produces the strength of love. Identity versus role confusion is the adolescent crisis that comes before it.

**A290.** A. Generativity versus stagnation
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-10 -->

Explanation: Generativity versus stagnation occurs in middle adulthood, and a sense of contributing to future generations is the successful outcome. Integrity versus despair applies later in life, when people reflect on their whole life.

**A291.** D. Integrity versus despair
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-11 -->

Explanation: Integrity versus despair is the final stage, in late adulthood, when people review their lives, and a sense of integrity produces wisdom. Generativity versus stagnation belongs to middle adulthood and concerns contributing to the next generation.

**A292.** B. Initiative versus guilt
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-12 -->

Explanation: Initiative versus guilt occurs in the preschool years, and planning and leading activities supports the strength of purpose. Autonomy versus shame and doubt is an earlier toddler crisis about independence, and industry versus inferiority concerns school-age mastery.

**A293.** C. Its concepts are difficult to test scientifically
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-13 -->

Explanation: Concepts such as identity and intimacy are hard to define and measure, so the theory is difficult to test scientifically. Erikson's ideas came largely from clinical observation rather than controlled studies, and his stages are approximate rather than fixed to exact ages.

**A294.** A. Hope
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-14 -->

Explanation: Hope develops in infancy when care is reliable and responsive. Will follows autonomy versus shame and doubt, and competence follows industry versus inferiority.

**A295.** B. 40 to 65 years
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-15 -->

Explanation: Generativity versus stagnation is the seventh stage, in middle adulthood, roughly 40 to 65 years. Intimacy versus isolation covers 18 to 40 years, and integrity versus despair begins around 65.

**A296.** C. Little sense of contributing to others or future generations, with a focus on oneself
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-16 -->

Explanation: Stagnation involves self-absorption and a lack of contribution to others and the next generation. Regret and despair are the unsuccessful outcome of integrity versus despair, while doubt belongs to autonomy versus shame and doubt.

**A297.** A. 3 to 5 years
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-17 -->

Explanation: Initiative versus guilt typically occurs at about 3 to 5 years, when children plan and start activities. Autonomy versus shame and doubt occurs at about 1 to 3 years, and industry versus inferiority at about 6 to 12 years.

**A298.** B. Industry versus inferiority
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-18 -->

Explanation: School-age children, about 6 to 12 years, face industry versus inferiority, and falling behind peers can lead to a sense of inferiority. Initiative versus guilt applies to preschool children, and identity is primarily an adolescent crisis.

**A299.** C. Trust versus mistrust
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-19 -->

Explanation: Trust versus mistrust occurs in infancy and is the first of Erikson's eight stages. Autonomy and initiative come later in toddlerhood and early childhood, and industry in middle childhood.

**A300.** A. Intimacy versus isolation
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-20 -->

Explanation: Erikson argued that a secure identity supports the later task of forming intimate relationships in young adulthood. Trust and autonomy are earlier stages, and integrity is the final stage in late life.

**A301.** D. Stages occur roughly in sequence, though the timing varies between individuals
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-21 -->

Explanation: Erikson described the stages as occurring roughly in sequence, but the ages are approximate and individuals differ. Crises can be revisited, and the claim that the theory applies only to Western societies is a criticism rather than a feature of the theory.

**A302.** C. It involves choosing values, beliefs and occupations, which may be explored over time
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-22 -->

Explanation: Erikson saw identity as formed through exploring and then committing to values, beliefs and roles, mainly in adolescence. The other options deny the exploratory and social nature of identity.

**A303.** A. Each stage builds on the outcome of earlier stages, like parts of an embryo developing in sequence
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-23 -->

Explanation: Erikson borrowed the epigenetic principle from embryology, meaning each stage develops on the foundation of earlier ones. The other options describe genetic determinism, development that ends in adolescence, or biological maturation alone, which Erikson did not claim.

**A304.** D. Trust versus mistrust, autonomy versus shame and doubt, initiative versus guilt
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-24 -->

Explanation: The first three crises are trust versus mistrust in infancy, autonomy versus shame and doubt in toddlerhood, and initiative versus guilt in the preschool years. The other sequences place at least one crisis out of order.

**A305.** Intimacy versus isolation is the young adult crisis of forming close, committed relationships with others [1]; an unsuccessful resolution leads to loneliness, social withdrawal and difficulty trusting others in close relationships [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-25 -->

Explanation:

**A306.** Fidelity, the ability to commit to shared values, beliefs and relationships [1]; this helps adults stay loyal to partners, friends and roles such as work and family [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A307.** Eight stages [1]; the first crisis is trust versus mistrust and the last is integrity versus despair [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A308.** Initiative versus guilt occurs in the preschool years, about 3 to 5 years, and concerns planning and starting activities such as games [1]; industry versus inferiority occurs in middle childhood, about 6 to 12 years, and concerns mastering skills, especially at school [1]; successful initiative gives a sense of purpose, while successful industry gives a sense of competence [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A309.** The theory was developed mainly from observations of individuals in Western societies [1]; its emphasis on independence and an identity separate from family may not fit collectivist cultures, where identity is closely tied to family and community [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-29 -->

Explanation:

**A310.** Integrity versus despair is the final crisis, in late adulthood, when people review their lives [1]; a sense of integrity results when people accept their life as meaningful and worthwhile [1]; despair results when people regret missed opportunities and feel that life cannot be lived again [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-30 -->

Explanation:

**A311.** Autonomy versus shame and doubt occurs in toddlerhood, when children learn to do things for themselves and make choices [1]; successful resolution produces will, the determination to exercise free choice [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-31 -->

Explanation:

**A312.** Identity development continues across the lifespan, so the adult can re-examine their values, beliefs and roles [1]; changes such as a new job, a relationship or parenthood can prompt exploration and commitment to a clearer sense of self [1]; a secure identity formed in adulthood can then strengthen commitments in relationships and career [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-32 -->

Explanation:

**A313.** Competence and skills gained through successful industry build confidence and self-esteem [1]; this confidence allows adolescents to explore different roles and commit to a sense of who they are [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-33 -->

Explanation:

**A314.** Identity versus role confusion occurs in adolescence and intimacy versus isolation in young adulthood [1]; identity asks 'Who am I?' while intimacy asks 'Can I form close, committed relationships?' [1]; successful identity produces fidelity, while successful intimacy produces love [1]; a secure identity provides the foundation for successful intimacy [1].
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-34 -->

Explanation:

**A315.** A. Care
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-35 -->

Explanation: Successful resolution of generativity versus stagnation produces care, a commitment to guiding and nurturing the next generation. Love belongs to intimacy, wisdom to integrity and hope to trust, so each is a virtue of a different stage.

**A316.** A. Competence
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-36 -->

Explanation: Industry versus inferiority, in middle childhood, produces competence, the sense of being capable of mastering skills and tasks. Fidelity belongs to identity, wisdom to integrity and love to intimacy.

**A317.** D. Purpose
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-37 -->

Explanation: Successful resolution of initiative versus guilt produces purpose, a sense of direction and the confidence to plan and pursue goals. Will is linked to autonomy, hope to trust and care to generativity.

**A318.** A child who develops trust has learned that caregivers are reliable, so feels secure enough to explore and take risks [1]; a child who has developed mistrust may be wary of new situations and less willing to try new experiences [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-38 -->

Explanation:

**A319.** The crisis is industry versus inferiority [1]; because her successes depend on help, she may not develop a sense of competence and may feel inferior to her peers [1]; a likely outcome is reduced confidence and reluctance to attempt tasks independently [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-psyc-39 -->

Explanation:

**A320.** D. Identity versus role confusion
<!-- id: vce-psych12-er-c1 -->

Explanation: Adolescence is when people explore roles to form a stable sense of self. Intimacy versus isolation follows in young adulthood.

#### Typical and atypical behaviour

**A321.** B. Behaviour that is statistically uncommon or differs from what is expected for a person's age, culture and context
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-1 -->

Explanation: Atypical behaviour differs from what is typical for a given age, culture and context. It is not automatically a mental disorder; it may be harmless or even beneficial.

**A322.** D. adaptive
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-2 -->

Explanation: Adaptive behaviours support functioning and wellbeing, whereas maladaptive behaviours interfere with functioning and wellbeing.

**A323.** A. context in judging behaviour
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-3 -->

Explanation: Whether behaviour is typical or atypical depends on the social and cultural context in which it occurs.

**A324.** D. towards the mental wellbeing (flourishing) end
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-4 -->

Explanation: The continuum ranges from mentally healthy (high wellbeing, flourishing) through to mental health problems and mental disorder. Occasional mild stress is typical of good wellbeing.

**A325.** B. causes the person distress or interferes with daily functioning
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-5 -->

Explanation: Maladaptive behaviour impairs functioning, relationships or wellbeing. Being unusual alone is only atypical, not necessarily maladaptive.

**A326.** Atypical behaviour is behaviour that is uncommon or differs from the norm, but it may not harm the person or interfere with functioning (1); it can be adaptive or beneficial (1); Example: a person with exceptional musical talent or someone who lives a very different lifestyle but functions well and is content (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-6 -->

Explanation:

**A327.** Typical and atypical behaviour is judged relative to cultural norms (1); the same behaviour may be considered typical in one culture and atypical in another (1); so behaviour should be assessed in the cultural and social context, and not by one culture's standards (1).
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-7 -->

Explanation:

**A328.** Any two: the behaviour is atypical for the person's age, culture and context (1); it causes distress to the person or others (1); it is maladaptive or interferes with daily functioning (1); it persists over time or is severe in intensity (1). Maximum 2 marks.
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-8 -->

Explanation:

**A329.** A. A behaviour is judged atypical when only a small proportion of people in a comparable group show it
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-9 -->

Explanation: Statistical infrequency judges a behaviour by how rare it is among comparable people. The majority-based option reverses the idea, while distress and legality are different criteria.

**A330.** B. Statistical rarity does not, on its own, mean a behaviour is harmful or maladaptive
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-10 -->

Explanation: Rarity makes a behaviour statistically atypical but does not show that it causes harm or impairs functioning. The other options confuse rarity with disorder, overstate universality, or invent a threshold.

**A331.** C. Because the behaviour is common for young children at this stage, so age helps determine what is typical
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-11 -->

Explanation: Tantrums are common in early childhood, so age is a key influence on whether a behaviour is typical. The option claiming all tantrums are disorders ignores that many normal behaviours are tied to particular ages.

**A332.** D. The person is showing a temporary, typical response that does not stop them functioning
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-12 -->

Explanation: Brief distress that does not impair functioning is a common response to ordinary stress and is not, on its own, a sign of disorder. The distractors wrongly treat all distress as disorder or as proof of impaired functioning.

**A333.** A. The sadness is severe, has lasted for many months and interferes with daily functioning
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-13 -->

Explanation: Severity, persistence and interference with functioning are signs that a response may be a cause for concern. The other options describe expected grief responses that are common and temporary.

**A334.** B. Whether behaviour is typical depends on the cultural norms of the group in which it occurs
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-14 -->

Explanation: The same behaviour can be typical in one culture and atypical in another, so judgements must consider the group's norms. The options claiming universal meaning or minimal cultural effect contradict this.

**A335.** C. A mental disorder is a diagnosable condition, usually with persistent and significant effects on functioning, while a mental health problem may be less severe, shorter-lived or less disabling
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-15 -->

Explanation: Mental disorders are defined by diagnostic criteria and usually involve persistent, significant impairment, whereas mental health problems can be milder and less enduring. The option claiming problems are more serious reverses the relationship, and trauma is not a requirement for disorder.

**A336.** D. Typical behaviour can be maladaptive, and atypical behaviour can be adaptive
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-16 -->

Explanation: Typical and adaptive are different ideas: common behaviours such as heavy sugar consumption can harm wellbeing, while uncommon talents or lifestyles can be beneficial. Judging behaviour requires considering its effect on functioning, not just how common it is.

**A337.** A. A single atypical behaviour does not show whether the distress, impaired functioning or persistence criteria are met
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-17 -->

Explanation: Judging a behaviour as a concern requires looking at several criteria over time and in context, so one behaviour is not enough. The other options are false claims about diagnosis or about who may judge behaviour.

**A338.** C. A person who can memorise long strings of numbers accurately and uses this ability successfully in their work
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-18 -->

Explanation: An unusual ability that the person uses to function well is atypical but adaptive. The other options describe behaviours that cause distress or impair work and relationships, so they are maladaptive.

**A339.** D. Wearing a swimsuit to a formal wedding ceremony
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-19 -->

Explanation: Wearing a swimsuit to a formal ceremony breaks the expectations of that setting, whereas a rare blood type, red hair and a very high IQ are judged atypical by how rare they are.

**A340.** A. Social norms are shared expectations about how people should behave in a group, and they can differ between groups and change over time
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-20 -->

Explanation: Social norms are shared, group-based expectations that vary across cultures and over time. The distractors describe norms as universal, limited to young people, or as legal rules.

**A341.** B. How the behaviour compares with the person's age, culture and context, and whether it causes distress or impairs functioning
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-21 -->

Explanation: Psychologists consider the context and the effects on the person rather than a single source of information. Relying only on the person's opinion, rarity alone or the observer's preferences ignores key criteria.

**A342.** A. The behaviour is unusual, but it is not maladaptive or a cause for concern on the criteria given
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-22 -->

Explanation: Being uncommon and being a concern are different things, and this teenager is functioning well with no reported distress. The other options treat rarity or difference from others as enough to label behaviour a problem.

**A343.** C. The child's behaviour falls within the typical range of temperament, with no clear sign of concern
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-23 -->

Explanation: Shyness that does not stop a child forming friendships, enjoying school or coping with routines falls within normal variation. The other options treat a single trait as evidence of disorder or harm without considering functioning.

**A344.** Ethnocentrism [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-24 -->

Explanation:

**A345.** 15 ÷ 500 × 100 = 3% [1]; the behaviour is statistically uncommon (infrequent) in this group [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-25 -->

Explanation:

**A346.** A brief response to a stressful event is usually expected, whereas a behaviour that continues for a long time is more likely to indicate an ongoing problem [1]; persistence suggests the behaviour may be affecting functioning beyond the situation that triggered it [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-26 -->

Explanation:

**A347.** Typical behaviour is judged against age-based developmental expectations, so the same ability is expected at different ages [1]; a sixteen-year-old is expected to read fluently, so the difficulty is unusual for their age [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-27 -->

Explanation:

**A348.** A single atypical behaviour does not show distress, impaired functioning or persistence, so the label may not be accurate [1]; labels can lead to stigma and discrimination from others [1]; a label may change how the person sees themselves and affect their relationships, self-esteem or opportunities [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-28 -->

Explanation:

**A349.** The continuum shows a range of states rather than a simple healthy or unhealthy split [1]; a person can move along it over time, for example from flourishing to a mental health problem after a stressful event [1]; with support they can move back towards wellbeing [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-29 -->

Explanation:

**A350.** Whether a behaviour is adaptive depends on the person's circumstances, goals and context [1]; e.g., working long hours may be adaptive for a person whose job requires it and who has time for rest, but maladaptive for a person who is exhausted and neglecting their health [1]; judgements should focus on the effect on the person's functioning and wellbeing [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-30 -->

Explanation:

**A351.** Distress can be a typical response to events such as loss, illness or exam stress [1]; a behaviour is a concern when distress is combined with other criteria such as impaired functioning, persistence or severity [1]; whether it is atypical also depends on the person's age, culture and context [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-31 -->

Explanation:

**A352.** Her behaviour is a marked change from her usual pattern and atypical for her age [1]; she reports distress, feeling sad and tired most days [1]; it interferes with her functioning at school and in sport [1]; it has persisted for three months, so it is a cause for concern and warrants support from a professional [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-32 -->

Explanation:

**A353.** A. culture-bound syndrome
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-33 -->

Explanation: A culture-bound syndrome is a condition defined within a particular cultural context, so it cannot be fully judged by the standards of other cultures. A statistical outlier is judged by rarity, an ethnocentric judgement is a biased way of evaluating other cultures, and dysfunction refers to impaired functioning.

**A354.** D. A man who shouts at a referee, leading to a physical fight in which someone is injured
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-34 -->

Explanation: Behaviour that puts the person or others at risk of physical harm is a stronger cause for concern, regardless of how common it is. The other examples are harmless even though some are unusual or uncommon.

**A355.** B. It does not show whether a low score causes distress or impairment in the student's daily life
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-35 -->

Explanation: Statistical infrequency shows how rare a score is, but not whether it is distressing or impairs everyday functioning. The other options are not the central limitation of the approach.

**A356.** A criterion such as harm, dysfunction or distress [1]; it considers the impact on the person and others, rather than only how rare the behaviour is, so harmless rare behaviours are not labelled as problems [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-36 -->

Explanation:

**A357.** The clinician should consider whether the behaviour is typical within the client's cultural group rather than judging it by the clinician's own norms [1]; the behaviour may be adaptive and expected within her family norms, so it should not be labelled atypical without further context [1]; the clinician should still assess whether it causes distress or impairment in her daily life [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-37 -->

Explanation:

**A358.** Duration: the behaviour has lasted five weeks, which is longer than a temporary reaction [1]; dysfunction: it prevents the child from attending school and taking part in family life [1]
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-38 -->

Explanation:

**A359.** B. Being statistically rare is not the same as being maladaptive or a problem
<!-- id: vce-psych12-ta-c1 -->

Explanation: Each approach (statistical, socio-cultural, functional, historical, situational) has limits; combining them gives a fuller judgement.

#### Mental wellbeing and categorising mental disorders

**A360.** B. A state of emotional, social and cognitive functioning in which a person can cope with normal stressors and contribute to their community
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-1 -->

Explanation: Mental wellbeing is a positive state of emotional, social and cognitive functioning. It is not merely the absence of disorder (A), it can change over time (C), and it does not require avoiding negative emotions (D).

**A361.** C. flourishing
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-2 -->

Explanation: Flourishing is the high end of the continuum, with high emotional, psychological and social wellbeing. Languishing is the opposite end.

**A362.** A. a categorical approach
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-3 -->

Explanation: A categorical approach classifies a person as either having or not having a disorder based on meeting criteria, as in the DSM-5-TR. A dimensional approach rates symptoms along a continuum of severity.

**A363.** D. may ignore variation in symptom severity and treat people just below the threshold as having no disorder
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-4 -->

Explanation: Categorical systems use cut-offs, so a person with significant but sub-threshold symptoms may be classed as 'no disorder'. A dimensional approach addresses this by recording severity.

**A364.** B. Symptoms are rated on a continuum of severity
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-5 -->

Explanation: The dimensional approach places people along a spectrum of severity rather than in discrete categories.

**A365.** Categorical approach: a disorder is diagnosed as present or absent when a person meets a set of specified criteria (e.g. DSM-5-TR); dimensional approach: symptoms are rated along a continuum of severity, so people differ in degree rather than kind; one valid difference in use, e.g. categorical gives a clear diagnosis that guides treatment, while dimensional captures severity and sub-threshold cases.
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-6 -->

Explanation:

**A366.** Mia is likely flourishing; she shows positive emotional, social and psychological functioning (social connection, coping, personal growth); Jo is likely languishing; low emotional and social wellbeing even without a diagnosed disorder shows that mental health is not just the absence of disorder.
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-7 -->

Explanation:

**A367.** Mental health is a continuum and wellbeing varies over time and across emotional, social and cognitive domains; with effective treatment, supports and coping strategies, a person with a disorder can function well and report positive wellbeing, so disorder and wellbeing are not opposites.
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-8 -->

Explanation:

**A368.** A. moderately mentally healthy
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-9 -->

Explanation: Keyes' continuum has three categories: flourishing, moderately mentally healthy and languishing. A diagnosed disorder is classified separately from wellbeing, so 'mentally unwell' is not a category on the continuum.

**A369.** B. comorbidity
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-10 -->

Explanation: Comorbidity is the co-occurrence of two or more disorders in the same person. A provisional diagnosis is used when criteria are nearly but not fully met, a dimensional rating measures severity, and a specifier adds detail to a single diagnosis.

**A370.** C. a provisional diagnosis
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-11 -->

Explanation: A provisional diagnosis is recorded when a disorder is strongly suspected but there is insufficient information to confirm all criteria. A comorbid diagnosis refers to two disorders together, and a remission specifier applies to a disorder that has already been diagnosed.

**A371.** D. Clinically significant distress or impairment in important areas of functioning
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-12 -->

Explanation: Most DSM-5-TR criteria require clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas. Brain scans are not a general requirement, durations vary by disorder, and diagnosis can be made by other trained clinicians.

**A372.** A. the extent to which different clinicians reach the same diagnosis for the same person
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-13 -->

Explanation: Inter-rater reliability is consistency between clinicians. Accuracy in describing the disorder is validity, while diagnostic stability over time and recovery rates are different concepts.

**A373.** B. accurately identifies the condition it claims to describe
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-14 -->

Explanation: Validity is the accuracy with which a system identifies the condition it describes. Consistent application across clinicians is reliability, and cost or the source of information does not determine validity.

**A374.** C. The Cultural Formulation Interview
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-15 -->

Explanation: The Cultural Formulation Interview helps clinicians understand how culture shapes a person's experience and presentation of symptoms. The other options either remove culture from assessment or are not features of the manual.

**A375.** D. A clear sense of purpose and direction in life
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-16 -->

Explanation: Psychological wellbeing includes purpose in life, self-acceptance, personal growth, autonomy and environmental mastery. Positive emotions and life satisfaction are emotional wellbeing, and belonging to a community is social wellbeing.

**A376.** A. Five
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-17 -->

Explanation: At least five symptoms must be present during the same two-week period, and one of them must be depressed mood or loss of interest. Two or three symptoms are too few, and all nine are not required.

**A377.** B. a manual that classifies disorders mainly by criteria, with some severity specifiers
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-18 -->

Explanation: The DSM-5-TR is largely categorical, using criteria and specifiers, although it includes some dimensional elements. It is not a biological test for brain damage, and it does not measure overall wellbeing.

**A378.** C. It can lead to stigma and self-fulfilling expectations about what a person can do
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-19 -->

Explanation: Labels can attract stigma from others and create self-fulfilling expectations. A diagnosis does not guarantee improved wellbeing, remove stigma or end the need for treatment.

**A379.** D. Wellbeing is shaped by biological, psychological and social factors together, such as sleep, relationships and coping
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-20 -->

Explanation: The biopsychosocial view holds that biological, psychological and social factors interact to shape wellbeing. The other options each name a single factor or claim that wellbeing cannot change, which the view rejects.

**A380.** A. mental health and mental illness are related but separate continua
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-21 -->

Explanation: The two-continuum model treats mental illness and positive mental health as related but separate dimensions, so a person can have a disorder and still have high wellbeing. The single-continuum view is the one the model rejects.

**A381.** B. describe an additional feature of the diagnosed disorder
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-22 -->

Explanation: Specifiers give further detail about a diagnosis, such as particular features or course. They do not confirm a cause, rule out other disorders or change the diagnostic threshold.

**A382.** C. to allow clinicians to communicate, guide treatment and support research
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-23 -->

Explanation: Shared categories help clinicians communicate, guide treatment decisions and support research. They do not predict individual outcomes perfectly, replace assessment or establish a single cause.

**A383.** D. A rating scale that scores symptom severity on a continuum
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-24 -->

Explanation: A rating scale that scores severity on a continuum is dimensional. The other options rely on yes/no judgements or cut-offs, which are categorical.

**A384.** A. social contribution
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-25 -->

Explanation: Social wellbeing includes social contribution, social integration, social acceptance, social coherence and social actualisation. Life satisfaction is emotional wellbeing, while purpose in life and self-acceptance are psychological wellbeing.

**A385.** B. ICD-11
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-26 -->

Explanation: The International Classification of Diseases, 11th edition (ICD-11), is published by the WHO. The DSM-5-TR is published by the American Psychiatric Association, and the other options are not classification systems.

**A386.** C. research and clinical evidence about the symptoms and course of a disorder changes
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-27 -->

Explanation: Criteria are revised as research improves understanding of a disorder's symptoms and course. Changes in clinician numbers, treatment costs or patient requests are not the basis for revising diagnostic criteria.

**A387.** The co-occurrence of two or more mental disorders in the same person at the same time [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-28 -->

Explanation:

**A388.** A provisional diagnosis is recorded when a disorder is strongly suspected but there is insufficient information to confirm all criteria [1]; for example, symptoms are present but their duration cannot yet be established [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-29 -->

Explanation:

**A389.** Self-acceptance, e.g. holding a positive view of oneself and one's past [1]; purpose in life, e.g. having goals that give one's life direction and meaning [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-30 -->

Explanation:

**A390.** Reliability means different clinicians reach the same diagnosis for the same person [1]; validity means the classification accurately describes the disorder it names [1]; without both, people may receive inconsistent or inaccurate diagnoses and inappropriate treatment [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-31 -->

Explanation:

**A391.** Advantage: clear criteria give a diagnosis that supports consistent communication between clinicians [1]; advantage: a clear diagnosis can guide treatment decisions and eligibility for services [1]; limitation: people just below the threshold may be excluded, so their severity is ignored [1]; limitation: labels can oversimplify a complex experience and may create stigma [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-32 -->

Explanation:

**A392.** The degree or severity of symptoms along a continuum [1]; changes over time, such as improvement or worsening, including people below a diagnostic cut-off who still have significant difficulties [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-33 -->

Explanation:

**A393.** A categorical approach would not diagnose major depressive disorder, because Jade has four symptoms and five are required [1]; a dimensional approach would rate the severity of her symptoms, for example as mild to moderate [1]; this captures her real difficulties and allows her progress to be tracked over time [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-34 -->

Explanation:

**A394.** Symptoms and distress may be expressed differently across cultures [1]; some beliefs or idioms of distress are culturally normal, so a clinician may mistake them for a disorder or miss a disorder [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-35 -->

Explanation:

**A395.** Emotional wellbeing [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-36 -->

Explanation:

**A396.** Social wellbeing is most affected [1]; he lacks social integration, as he does not feel he belongs to any community [1]; his social contribution is low, as he believes he has nothing useful to offer others [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-37 -->

Explanation:

**A397.** A categorical diagnosis gives a clear classification for communication, service access and treatment guidelines [1]; a dimensional rating captures the severity of symptoms and how they change over time [1]; used together, the clinician can diagnose and also track severity for the same client [1]; benefit: a fuller picture guides treatment choices and allows progress to be monitored [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-38 -->

Explanation:

**A398.** D. the proportion of a population who have a disorder at a particular point in time or period
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-39 -->

Explanation: Prevalence describes how many people in a population currently have a disorder, expressed as a proportion of the whole population. New cases are described by incidence, agreement between clinicians is reliability, and validity is about whether a test measures what it claims to measure.

**A399.** A. They assume a clear boundary between 'disordered' and 'normal', and labels can lead to stigma
<!-- id: vce-psych12-mc-c1 -->

Explanation: Categorical approaches give a yes/no diagnosis; dimensional approaches describe symptoms on a continuum.

### Unit 2 AOS 1: Social cognition and social influence

#### Person perception and attributions

**A400.** C. the process of forming impressions and making judgements about other people
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-1 -->

Explanation: Person perception is the process of forming impressions of others, influenced by factors such as the halo effect and physical appearance. B describes conformity, C stereotypes, D is not a standard definition.

**A401.** A. the halo effect
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-2 -->

Explanation: The halo effect is the tendency to let one positive trait (e.g. attractiveness) influence judgements about other, unrelated traits.

**A402.** D. the fundamental attribution error
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-3 -->

Explanation: The fundamental attribution error is the tendency to overemphasise dispositional (internal) factors and underestimate situational factors when explaining others' behaviour.

**A403.** A. the self-serving bias
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-4 -->

Explanation: The self-serving bias attributes successes to internal factors and failures to external factors, protecting self-esteem.

**A404.** C. 'I was late because the road was closed for repairs.'
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-5 -->

Explanation: Situational attributions explain behaviour by factors outside the person, such as road closures. A, B and D are dispositional.

**A405.** Dispositional attribution: behaviour explained by internal characteristics such as personality, ability or attitudes; situational attribution: behaviour explained by external factors such as circumstances, other people or the environment; two appropriate examples, e.g. 'he is rude' (dispositional) vs 'he was stressed by a deadline' (situational).
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A406.** Actor-observer bias (a form of the fundamental attribution error when judging others); we explain others' behaviour dispositionally (unpleasant person); we explain our own behaviour situationally (being exhausted); we have more access to our own situational context than to others'.
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A407.** An interviewer's unfavourable first impression of one trait (e.g. appearance, nervousness or accent) can generalise to unrelated traits; the applicant may be rated as less competent or reliable than their actual skills warrant, so they are unfairly judged.
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A408.** A. the tendency for information presented first to carry more weight in forming an impression than later information
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-9 -->

Explanation: The primacy effect means early information shapes the overall impression more strongly than later information, which is then interpreted in its light. The recency effect is the reverse, where the latest information dominates, while the other options describe similarity bias and the just world belief.

**A409.** B. a mental framework of organised knowledge about a type of person or situation that guides expectations
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-10 -->

Explanation: A schema is an organised body of knowledge and expectations that helps people interpret and predict what they encounter. A single memory is an episode rather than a framework, while physical traits and emotional reactions are inputs or responses, not the schema itself.

**A410.** C. high consensus, high distinctiveness, high consistency
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-11 -->

Explanation: A situational attribution is most likely when many others behave the same way (high consensus), the person behaves this way only in this situation (high distinctiveness) and does so reliably (high consistency). The first option points to a dispositional attribution, while the other two give mixed signals.

**A411.** D. an expectation about someone that leads that person to behave in a way that confirms the expectation
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-12 -->

Explanation: In a self-fulfilling prophecy, a perceiver's expectation changes how they treat someone, which elicits the expected behaviour and so appears to confirm the belief. The other options describe fixed-trait beliefs, selective memory and accurate prediction, none of which involves the expectation causing its own confirmation.

**A412.** A. generally more pronounced in individualist cultures than in many collectivist cultures
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-13 -->

Explanation: Individualist cultures place more emphasis on personal traits and independence, so dispositional explanations are typically more common there, while collectivist cultures attend more to context. The 'absent entirely' and 'exactly the same degree' options overstate the findings, and education is not the explanatory factor.

**A413.** B. a negative impression formed from one unfavourable trait, which then colours judgements of other, unrelated traits
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-14 -->

Explanation: The horns effect is the negative counterpart of the halo effect: one unfavourable trait generalises to unrelated judgements. The positive version describes the halo effect itself, while the other options describe primacy-type memory and a belief that appearance reveals character.

**A414.** C. A colleague says a worker who was dismissed must have deserved it, because bad things only happen to people who do something wrong.
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-15 -->

Explanation: The just world hypothesis is the belief that people get what they deserve, so misfortune must be the victim's fault. The driver example is a fundamental attribution error and the team example is the self-serving bias, while the promotion decision is simply a fair outcome rather than an attribution.

**A415.** D. a concern that one's performance will confirm a negative stereotype about one's group, which can itself reduce performance
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-16 -->

Explanation: Stereotype threat is the pressure felt when a person worries that their performance will confirm a negative stereotype, and this anxiety can lower performance. The other options describe fear of physical harm, conformity and ingroup bias, which are related to prejudice but are different concepts.

**A416.** A. confirmation bias, the tendency to seek out and interpret information in ways that support an existing belief
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-17 -->

Explanation: Confirmation bias leads Mia to notice evidence that fits her view and discount evidence that does not. The primacy and halo effects describe different impression-forming errors, and cognitive dissonance concerns conflicting beliefs or actions rather than how information is selected.

**A417.** B. explain negative behaviour of outgroup members in terms of their character, but explain the same behaviour by ingroup members as the result of circumstances
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-18 -->

Explanation: The ultimate attribution error extends the fundamental attribution error to groups: outgroup wrongdoing is seen as dispositional, while the same behaviour by ingroup members is excused as situational. The other options describe related but different biases, such as ingroup perceptions, the reverse of the fundamental attribution error and selective memory.

**A418.** C. the tone of voice and eye contact an applicant uses during an interview
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-19 -->

Explanation: Non-verbal cues such as tone of voice, eye contact and posture strongly influence first impressions, often within seconds. The other options are verbal or written information, which can be assessed separately from how a person communicates in person.

**A419.** A. the availability heuristic
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-20 -->

Explanation: The availability heuristic bases judgements on how easily examples come to mind, which can be skewed by vivid or recent cases. The representativeness heuristic judges by how closely something matches a prototype, anchoring relies on an initial reference value, and the gambler's fallacy wrongly expects reversals in random events.

**A420.** B. First impressions form within seconds and can be difficult to change, even when new information contradicts them.
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-21 -->

Explanation: Research suggests people form impressions within seconds, and these can persist because of primacy and confirmation bias. The other statements are inaccurate, since impressions form quickly, are not always correct and draw heavily on non-verbal cues as well as words.

**A421.** C. the deliberate effort to control the image that others form of us
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-22 -->

Explanation: Impression management involves deliberately shaping how others see us, for example through dress, behaviour or the information we choose to share. The spotlight effect is the overestimation of how much others notice us, while the other options describe impression formation and conformity.

**A422.** D. A stereotype is a belief about the characteristics of a group; prejudice is a negative attitude or feeling towards members of that group.
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-23 -->

Explanation: A stereotype is a cognitive belief about a group's characteristics, while prejudice is an affective, usually negative, attitude towards its members. Discrimination is the behaviour that can follow from either, so the option describing prejudice as an action confuses the two, and the others swap or merge the definitions.

**A423.** A. the outgroup homogeneity effect
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-24 -->

Explanation: The outgroup homogeneity effect leads people to perceive outgroup members as all alike, while they see their own group as more diverse. The false consensus effect is overestimating how many others share one's views, while the other two describe favouritism towards one's group and self-focus.

**A424.** B. implicit personality theory
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-25 -->

Explanation: Implicit personality theory is a lay belief about how traits cluster together, which can produce the halo effect when one trait is used to predict others. The spotlight effect, actor-observer bias and cognitive dissonance describe different processes.

**A425.** C. assuming a quiet, bookish person is more likely to be a librarian than a salesperson, because they match the librarian prototype
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-26 -->

Explanation: The representativeness heuristic judges the likelihood of a category by how closely a person matches its prototype, while ignoring base rates. The plane crash example is the availability heuristic, the price example is anchoring, and the coin example is the gambler's fallacy.

**A426.** Vivid, widely reported accidents come to mind easily, so the driver overestimates how common such accidents are among the brand's drivers [1]; the judgement ignores base rates, so it is biased and inaccurate [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-27 -->

Explanation:

**A427.** The teacher's expectation shapes how they treat the student, reducing opportunities and feedback [1]; the student responds to these cues with lower confidence and effort, so their results decline [1]; the decline appears to confirm the original expectation, creating a self-reinforcing cycle [1]; the cycle could be broken by the teacher giving the student challenging tasks and feedback, or by the student being shown evidence that contradicts the expectation [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-28 -->

Explanation:

**A428.** The strong opening is early information, which is given more weight in forming the overall impression [1]; the marker interprets the later, weaker paragraphs in light of that favourable first impression, so their weaknesses are underweighted [1]; the mark therefore does not accurately reflect the whole essay [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-29 -->

Explanation:

**A429.** The student worries that poor performance will confirm the stereotype, which creates anxiety and pressure [1]; this anxiety uses up attention and working memory, lowering performance on the test [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-30 -->

Explanation:

**A430.** The observer believes people get what they deserve, so the victim must have done something to bring the robbery on [1]; blaming the victim protects the observer's belief that the world is fair and that similar misfortune can be avoided [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-31 -->

Explanation:

**A431.** A non-verbal cue such as eye contact or tone of voice [1]; steady eye contact may lead the interviewer to judge the applicant as confident and honest, making the impression more favourable [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-32 -->

Explanation:

**A432.** The coach notices and remembers the player's mistakes because they fit the belief [1]; the coach overlooks or discounts good plays that contradict it [1]; the belief is therefore maintained despite balanced evidence, so the evaluation is biased [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-33 -->

Explanation:

**A433.** Outgroup homogeneity: members of the outgroup are seen as all alike, so one negative example is generalised to the whole group [1]; ultimate attribution error: negative behaviour by outgroup members is attributed to their dispositions, while the same behaviour by ingroup members is excused as situational [1]; negative outgroup behaviour is therefore seen as typical and inherent, while ingroup failings are explained away [1]; individual counter-examples are dismissed as exceptions, so the prejudice resists contrary evidence [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-34 -->

Explanation:

**A434.** A positive mood can lead the person to interpret the stranger's ambiguous behaviour more favourably [1]; a negative mood can lead to more unfavourable judgements, so the impression reflects the perceiver's state as well as the stranger's behaviour [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-35 -->

Explanation:

**A435.** C. illusory correlation
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-36 -->

Explanation: An illusory correlation occurs when people perceive a link between two things that is not present in the data, often because distinctive or memorable events stand out. The fundamental attribution error concerns explaining others' behaviour in terms of personality, the self-serving bias concerns explaining one's own success or failure, and the halo effect concerns generalising a positive trait.

**A436.** The tendency to attribute successes to internal, dispositional factors and failures to external, situational factors, which protects self-esteem [1]; for example, a student who does well credits her ability, but blames an unfair test when she does poorly [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-37 -->

Explanation:

**A437.** The representativeness heuristic, in which a person is judged by how closely they match a stereotype or prototype of a group [1]; the owner ignores the applicant's qualifications and work history, which are better evidence of ability [1]; this could lead to rejecting a capable applicant for irrelevant reasons [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-38 -->

Explanation:

**A438.** D. The fundamental attribution error
<!-- id: vce-psych12-pp-c1 -->

Explanation: We tend to overestimate personal (internal) causes and underestimate situational (external) causes of other people's behaviour.

#### Attitudes and cognitive dissonance

**A439.** B. a person's feelings about an object
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-1 -->

Explanation: The affective component is the emotional response (e.g. 'I love dogs'). The cognitive component is beliefs and the behavioural component is actions or intentions.

**A440.** D. an uncomfortable mental tension that arises when a person holds inconsistent cognitions or when behaviour conflicts with attitudes
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-2 -->

Explanation: Festinger's cognitive dissonance is the psychological discomfort from inconsistent cognitions, which motivates the person to reduce it.

**A441.** B. changing or minimising the importance of his attitude or belief
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-3 -->

Explanation: He is rationalising and minimising the belief about harm rather than changing his behaviour, which reduces dissonance.

**A442.** C. A person who values the environment starts cycling instead of driving
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-4 -->

Explanation: Changing behaviour to match the attitude (cycling) removes the inconsistency. B and D change or protect cognition, and A trivialises the attitude.

**A443.** A. greater cognitive dissonance in the small payment group, resolved by changing their attitude
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-5 -->

Explanation: With little external justification for lying, dissonance is higher and is reduced by changing the attitude to match the behaviour (Festinger and Carlsmith).

**A444.** Cognitive: beliefs or thoughts about the object (e.g. recycling reduces landfill); affective: feelings about it (e.g. feeling proud or good about recycling); behavioural: how the person acts or intends to act (e.g. sorting rubbish into the recycling bin).
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-6 -->

Explanation:

**A445.** She experiences dissonance (psychological discomfort) because her behaviour conflicts with her attitude about honesty; reduction by changing the attitude (e.g. 'white lies are kind'), adding a new cognition (it avoided hurting her friend) or changing behaviour (telling the friend the truth); one strategy clearly linked to the scenario.
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-7 -->

Explanation:

**A446.** Behaviour can be shaped by situational factors such as social pressure, cost or convenience; attitudes may be weak or conflicting across components, or the person may not have the opportunity or ability to act on them.
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-8 -->

Explanation:

**A447.** A. Signing a petition to ban single-use plastic bags
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-9 -->

Explanation: Signing a petition is an observable action, so it is the behavioural component. Believing that bags harm marine life is cognitive and feeling angry is affective, so both are tempting but wrong.

**A448.** B. The attitude is strong, based on direct personal experience and often thought about
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-10 -->

Explanation: Strong attitudes formed through direct experience are more accessible in memory, so they guide behaviour more consistently. Weak or borrowed attitudes, and attitudes about unfamiliar topics, are less likely to predict what a person actually does.

**A449.** C. observational learning from a trusted model
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-11 -->

Explanation: Mia has learned her attitude by observing and modelling a person she trusts, without any personal experience of the sport. Mere exposure would tend to produce liking rather than dislike, and operant conditioning depends on consequences of her own behaviour, which are not described.

**A450.** D. A learned and relatively enduring evaluation of a person, object or issue
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-12 -->

Explanation: An attitude is a learned evaluation, positive or negative, that tends to last over time. A mood is temporary, a trait is largely inborn, and an automatic behaviour is not an evaluation at all.

**A451.** A. A person who believes regular exercise is important skips every training session and then feels guilty
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-13 -->

Explanation: Dissonance arises when a held belief and a behaviour conflict, and the guilt signals that tension. The other options all show beliefs and behaviour that match, so no tension arises.

**A452.** B. dissonance reduction, because the recruits increase the value they place on the club to justify their suffering
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-14 -->

Explanation: Enduring pain for a club they do not value highly would create tension, so the recruits resolve it by valuing the club more. Conformity and the self-serving bias do not involve resolving this inconsistency, and the halo effect is a judgement bias rather than a dissonance process.

**A453.** B. dissonance reduction after a decision, by changing how the options are evaluated
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-15 -->

Explanation: Having rejected an attractive option creates tension, which Rahul reduces by making his choice look better and the alternative look worse. The foot-in-the-door technique, bystander effect and fundamental attribution error do not explain this post-decision shift.

**A454.** D. Strong social pressure from peers to act against the attitude
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-16 -->

Explanation: Strong social pressure to act against an attitude can override it, weakening the attitude-behaviour link. Ease of action, specificity and direct experience all tend to strengthen that link.

**A455.** D. Attitudes can influence behaviour, and behaviour can also change attitudes, so the influence can run in both directions
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-17 -->

Explanation: Dissonance research shows that acting in a certain way can shift attitudes, while attitudes also guide behaviour. The other options are absolute claims that the evidence does not support.

**A456.** A. classical conditioning, because a neutral stimulus has been repeatedly paired with an unpleasant experience
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-18 -->

Explanation: The drill sound was neutral but became associated with pain, so it now triggers the emotional response on its own. Operant conditioning depends on the consequences of her own behaviour, which are not described, and cognitive dissonance is about inconsistent cognitions.

**A457.** B. operant conditioning, because positive reinforcement strengthens the expressed view
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-19 -->

Explanation: Praise following his statements is a reinforcer that increases the likelihood he repeats them, which can lead to holding the attitude. Classical conditioning involves an involuntary response to paired stimuli, and mere exposure involves no reward.

**A458.** C. I feel frustrated every time I have to put on the uniform
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-20 -->

Explanation: Frustration is an emotional response, so it is the affective component. The cost and tradition statements are beliefs (cognitive) and the letter is an action (behavioural).

**A459.** D. a valued belief conflicts with an action the person freely chose to take
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-21 -->

Explanation: Dissonance is greatest when a freely chosen action clashes with an important belief, because there is no outside reason for the action. Pressure from an authority gives external justification and reduces dissonance, while trivial or unnoticed inconsistencies create little discomfort.

**A460.** C. Living through a flood and losing property
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-22 -->

Explanation: Losing property in a flood is first-hand experience, which tends to produce strong, lasting attitudes. The other options are second-hand information from the media or from other people.

**A461.** C. Attitudes include an evaluation of something as good or bad, whereas beliefs are information a person holds about it
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-23 -->

Explanation: An attitude is an evaluation (positive or negative) of an object, while a belief is what a person thinks is true about it. Beliefs are not guaranteed to be true, and beliefs do not carry the like-or-dislike evaluation that attitudes do.

**A462.** A. The cognitive and affective components of his attitude are inconsistent
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-24 -->

Explanation: His belief that flying is safe is cognitive, while his fear is affective, and these clash. The other options either deny the inconsistency or mislabel the components.

**A463.** A. the mere exposure effect
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-25 -->

Explanation: Repeated exposure to a neutral stimulus alone can increase liking, which is the mere exposure effect. The other options describe processes that involve inconsistency, compliance or attribution, none of which are present here.

**A464.** C. Adding consistent cognitions by seeking support for her decision
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-26 -->

Explanation: Seeking colleagues who agree adds supporting cognitions, which reduces the conflict created by the criticism. Returning to the old job would be a behavioural change, and changing her attitude towards her friend does not address the decision itself.

**A465.** A message that only presents facts engages only the cognitive component, which may be insufficient because attitudes are also driven by emotion [1]; an appeal to feelings such as guilt or fear engages the affective component and can motivate action [1]; a stronger affective response makes it more likely that the behavioural component (intention to act) will change [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-27 -->

Explanation:

**A466.** Her behaviour (helping) conflicts with her belief that hunting is cruel, creating cognitive dissonance [1]; the family request provides external justification, which may ease the tension somewhat [1]; she could change her attitude, e.g. by deciding that hunting is a tradition that is not truly cruel [1]; or she could change her behaviour or add a new cognition, e.g. by telling herself she helped only to support her family and declining future events [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-28 -->

Explanation:

**A467.** A reason outside the person, such as a reward or pressure from others, that explains why they acted against their attitude, so less dissonance is felt [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-29 -->

Explanation:

**A468.** She feels tension because the new information conflicts with her decision to buy the phone [1]; she could reduce it by focusing on the strengths of her phone and devaluing the competitor's model [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-30 -->

Explanation:

**A469.** The person may hold beliefs that public transport is cheap and efficient (cognitive) [1]; while also feeling anxious or annoyed about crowded trains (affective), so the two components do not match [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-31 -->

Explanation:

**A470.** Acting in a way that conflicts with a belief creates dissonance, so the person may change the belief to match the action [1]; e.g. someone who spends heavily on a brand comes to believe its products are superior to those of other brands [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-32 -->

Explanation:

**A471.** The child watches and imitates models, such as parents or teachers, who read enthusiastically [1]; the child sees reading valued or enjoyed and so adopts the attitude (e.g. wanting to read because a parent visibly enjoys books) [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-33 -->

Explanation:

**A472.** Behavioural component [1].
