<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-34 -->

Explanation:

**A473.** B. Reducing cognitive dissonance by changing a belief to fit the behaviour
<!-- id: vce-psych12-ac-c1 -->

Explanation: Conflicting thoughts and actions cause discomfort; people reduce it by changing the behaviour, the belief, or adding justifying beliefs.

#### Stereotypes, prejudice and discrimination

**A474.** B. a generalised belief about the characteristics of members of a social group
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-1 -->

Explanation: Stereotypes are cognitive generalisations about a group. Negative feelings are prejudice (A) and unfair actions are discrimination (C).

**A475.** A. discrimination
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-2 -->

Explanation: Discrimination is the unfair treatment of people based on group membership. Prejudice is the attitude and stereotype is the belief.

**A476.** B. Stereotype: cognitive; prejudice: affective; discrimination: behavioural
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-3 -->

Explanation: Stereotypes are beliefs (cognitive), prejudice is a feeling or attitude (affective) and discrimination is action (behavioural).

**A477.** B. Ignoring or discounting information that disconfirms it
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-4 -->

Explanation: Stereotypes persist because people attend to information that confirms them and discount contradictory cases.

**A478.** B. Cooperative contact between groups working towards shared goals on an equal footing
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-5 -->

Explanation: Cooperative contact on equal status towards superordinate goals reduces prejudice. Competition (D) tends to increase it.

**A479.** Prejudice: an unjustified, usually negative, attitude or feeling towards people because of their group membership; discrimination: unfair behaviour or treatment of people because of their group membership; examples, e.g. disliking colleagues who wear a hijab (prejudice) vs. not promoting them because of it (discrimination).
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A480.** Stereotypes and prejudice can lead to discriminatory behaviour even when applicants' qualifications are identical; the employers' beliefs affected who was selected; strategy, e.g. blind CV screening, cooperative contact, or diversity training that challenges stereotypes.
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A481.** People notice and remember information that supports the stereotype (confirmation of belief) while ignoring exceptions; this reinforces the belief and may shape behaviour towards the group in ways that elicit stereotype-consistent responses.
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A482.** B. an unjustified, usually negative, attitude towards members of a group because of their group membership
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-9 -->

Explanation: Prejudice is the affective component: a usually negative attitude or feeling held because of group membership. A generalised belief describes a stereotype, and unfair treatment describes discrimination.

**A483.** C. in-group favouritism
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-10 -->

Explanation: In-group favouritism is the tendency to favour one's own group over others. Out-group homogeneity is the perception that members of other groups are all alike, illusory correlation is a perceived link between two things that are not actually related, and the fundamental attribution error explains behaviour by personality rather than situation.

**A484.** A. out-group homogeneity effect
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-11 -->

Explanation: The out-group homogeneity effect is the tendency to see members of other groups as more alike than members of one's own group. In-group favouritism concerns favourable evaluation of one's own group, the halo effect is a general positive impression spreading across unrelated traits, and self-serving bias is attributing success to oneself.

**A485.** D. illusory correlation
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-12 -->

Explanation: An illusory correlation is the perception of a relationship between two things that the data do not support, often when rare, distinctive events happen together. A self-fulfilling prophecy changes behaviour to match expectations, the just-world hypothesis is the belief that people get what they deserve, and the fundamental attribution error explains behaviour in terms of personality rather than circumstances.

**A486.** A. subtyping
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-13 -->

Explanation: Subtyping creates a separate sub-category for the exception so that the broader stereotype is protected. Conformity is changing behaviour to match a group norm, scapegoating blames a group for problems it did not cause, and realistic group conflict arises from competition over scarce resources.

**A487.** C. self-fulfilling prophecy
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-14 -->

Explanation: A self-fulfilling prophecy occurs when an expectation changes behaviour so that the expected outcome occurs, which then appears to confirm the expectation. The other options describe different processes: attributing behaviour to personality, favouring one's own group, and competing over scarce resources.

**A488.** B. institutional discrimination
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-15 -->

Explanation: Institutional discrimination occurs when rules or practices that appear neutral disadvantage a group, regardless of intent. Prejudice and stereotypes are attitudes and beliefs held by people, and no such attitude or belief is described here, while in-group favouritism concerns favouring one's own group.

**A489.** D. Stereotypes can be positive or negative, and may be accurate, partly accurate or inaccurate
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-16 -->

Explanation: Stereotypes are cognitive generalisations that can be positive or negative and vary in accuracy. Feelings describe prejudice rather than stereotypes, and people often form stereotypes indirectly through the media, family and culture.

**A490.** B. Simply being categorised into a group can produce in-group favouritism
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-17 -->

Explanation: This is the minimal group effect: categorisation alone, with no conflict or contact, is enough to produce in-group favouritism. The other options describe conditions that are not necessary for these processes to occur.

**A491.** A. scapegoating
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-18 -->

Explanation: Scapegoating directs hostility at a group that is blamed for problems it did not cause, often when people feel frustrated. The contact hypothesis describes ways to reduce prejudice, self-serving bias is attributing success to oneself, and out-group homogeneity is perceiving other groups as alike.

**A492.** B. An automatic, often unintentional negative association with a group that a person may not consciously endorse
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-19 -->

Explanation: Implicit prejudice operates automatically and may be unconscious, so a person can hold it without agreeing with it when they reflect. Explicit prejudice is consciously held and openly reported, while deliberately chosen beliefs and secret actions do not describe implicit prejudice.

**A493.** B. Providing education that challenges inaccurate stereotypes
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-20 -->

Explanation: Education that challenges inaccurate stereotypes works on beliefs, which are the cognitive component. Anti-discrimination laws act on behaviour, separating groups reduces the contact that can weaken prejudice, and repeating typical media images reinforces stereotypes.

**A494.** A. She holds prejudice, the affective component, without having discriminated
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-21 -->

Explanation: Prejudice is an attitude and can exist without any unfair behaviour. Discrimination requires an action that treats people unfairly, and the scenario describes feelings rather than a belief about the group's characteristics.

**A495.** A. social categorisation
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-22 -->

Explanation: Social categorisation is the first step, sorting people into in-groups and out-groups. Social identification involves adopting the identity of a group, and social comparison involves evaluating one's own group against others, usually favourably. Conformity is changing behaviour to match group norms and is not one of the theory's processes.

**A496.** C. A manager promotes a less qualified male colleague over a female colleague because of gender
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-23 -->

Explanation: Discrimination is an unfair action based on group membership, here the promotion decision. The other options describe beliefs or feelings, which are the cognitive and affective components of prejudice, and do not in themselves involve an unfair action.

**A497.** D. They simplify information processing about groups, but can lead to errors
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-24 -->

Explanation: Stereotypes are a cognitive shortcut that speeds up judgements about groups, but they overgeneralise and can be wrong. Stereotypes are beliefs rather than behaviours, and people without prejudice can still hold them.

**A498.** D. Giving every student the same challenging tasks and the same quality of feedback
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-25 -->

Explanation: Equal challenge and feedback removes the differential treatment that drives the expected outcome. Telling teachers about expectations reinforces them, grouping by expected ability can produce the same effect, and lowering expectations for all students still limits what everyone is asked to achieve.

**A499.** C. individuation
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-26 -->

Explanation: Individuation means attending to a person's unique attributes, which reduces reliance on stereotypes. Social facilitation is improved performance in the presence of others, deindividuation is loss of self-awareness in a crowd, and reactance is a response to a threat to personal freedom.

**A500.** A social group that a person does not belong to, which is often perceived as different from, and less favourably than, their own in-group. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-27 -->

Explanation:

**A501.** People overestimate the link between rare, distinctive behaviours and a minority group, because such pairings stand out in memory. [1] This leads people to believe the group is associated with that behaviour, which becomes a stereotype. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-28 -->

Explanation:

**A502.** Members of an out-group are seen as more alike than members of the in-group, so individual differences are overlooked. [1] This makes it easier to apply one negative belief to all out-group members, supporting prejudice. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-29 -->

Explanation:

**A503.** Direct discrimination: a person or organisation deliberately treats someone less favourably because of their group membership, e.g. refusing to hire someone because of their religion. [1] Institutional discrimination: a policy or practice that appears neutral disadvantages a group regardless of intent, e.g. a roster that excludes people with caring responsibilities. [1] Both produce unfair outcomes based on group membership; the key difference is intent. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-30 -->

Explanation:

**A504.** Individuating information gives specific personal details that the stereotype does not describe. [1] Because a stereotype is a generalisation, this makes it less likely to be applied to the person. [1] Judgements then rely on the person's own characteristics rather than their group membership. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-31 -->

Explanation:

**A505.** Recategorisation places members of both groups within a shared, superordinate category, so they are seen as one in-group. [1] Former out-group members are then evaluated more favourably as part of the shared group. [1] This weakens the us-versus-them boundary, reducing prejudice and in-group bias. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-32 -->

Explanation:

**A506.** Categorisation: people sort themselves and others into groups, creating in-groups and out-groups. [1] Identification: people adopt their group's identity, which becomes part of their self-concept. [1] Comparison: people compare their in-group with out-groups and evaluate their own group more positively to protect self-esteem, leading to favouritism. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-33 -->

Explanation:

**A507.** Laws target the behavioural component by making discriminatory actions illegal. [1] Prejudice also has affective (feelings) and cognitive (stereotype) components, which laws do not directly change. [1] A person may stop discriminating while still holding negative feelings and stereotypes, so prejudice can persist. [1] Interventions that change beliefs and feelings, such as education and positive contact, are needed to address these components. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-34 -->

Explanation:

**A508.** Competition for scarce resources creates real conflicts of interest between the groups. [1] Groups come to see each other as rivals, increasing hostility and negative stereotypes of the out-group. [1] Realistic group conflict theory predicts that prejudice increases as competition intensifies. [1] A strategy such as introducing superordinate goals that require both groups to cooperate can reduce the conflict. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-35 -->

Explanation:

**A509.** Believing that people get what they deserve leads people to see members of a disadvantaged group as responsible for their own misfortune. [1] This justifies unfair treatment or indifference towards the group, supporting prejudice and discrimination. [1]
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-36 -->

Explanation:

**A510.** C. The groups work cooperatively, with equal status, towards a shared goal supported by authority
<!-- id: vce-psych12-sp-c1 -->

Explanation: Contact alone can increase hostility; cooperation and equal status (as in the jigsaw classroom) are needed.

#### Heuristics and cognitive biases

**A511.** C. a mental shortcut that allows quick judgements and decisions but may lead to errors
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-1 -->

Explanation: Heuristics are rules of thumb that speed up judgement, sacrificing some accuracy.

**A512.** A. Availability heuristic
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-2 -->

Explanation: The availability heuristic judges probability by how easily examples come to mind, and vivid news stories make shark attacks seem common.

**A513.** C. representativeness heuristic
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-3 -->

Explanation: The representativeness heuristic judges likelihood by how well something matches a prototype, ignoring base rates.

**A514.** A. Seeking, interpreting and remembering information that supports existing beliefs while overlooking contradictory evidence
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-4 -->

Explanation: Confirmation bias is a cognitive bias in which we favour information consistent with our beliefs. D describes the availability heuristic.

**A515.** D. confirmation bias
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-5 -->

Explanation: She is selecting and interpreting information to fit her existing view, which is confirmation bias.

**A516.** Cognitive bias: a systematic error in thinking or judgement that can affect decisions; heuristic: a mental shortcut or rule of thumb used to make quick decisions; heuristics are the strategies and biases are the systematic errors that can result, e.g. availability heuristic causing overestimation of vivid events.
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A517.** Availability heuristic; the vivid crash coverage makes plane crashes easy to recall, so he overestimates their probability; statistically driving is riskier per distance travelled, so the decision relies on a biased estimate rather than actual risk.
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A518.** Advantage: fast and efficient, saving time and cognitive effort when information is limited; disadvantage: can lead to errors or biased judgements, e.g. ignoring base rates or overestimating vivid events.
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A519.** B. Anchoring effect
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-9 -->

Explanation: The original price acts as an anchor, and the shopper's judgement of value is not adjusted sufficiently away from it. The availability heuristic depends on how easily examples come to mind, and hindsight bias concerns judging past events as predictable.

**A520.** B. Representativeness heuristic
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-10 -->

Explanation: The mixed sequence looks more like people's idea of a random sequence, so it is judged as more representative of a fair coin. The affect heuristic relies on emotional reactions, and anchoring relies on an initial reference value, so neither explains a judgement about how a sequence looks.

**A521.** A. Affect heuristic
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-11 -->

Explanation: The affect heuristic involves judging an option by the emotional response it provokes, here dislike of the company. The availability heuristic would depend on how easily negative examples come to mind, not on feelings about the company.

**A522.** C. Hindsight bias
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-12 -->

Explanation: Hindsight bias is the tendency to believe, once an outcome is known, that it was predictable beforehand. Confirmation bias concerns seeking and interpreting information that supports existing beliefs, which is not what the fan is doing here.

**A523.** D. False consensus effect
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-13 -->

Explanation: The false consensus effect is the tendency to overestimate how many others share our own views, here based on a small circle of friends. Optimism bias concerns underestimating personal risk, which is not the issue in this scenario.

**A524.** A. Belief perseverance
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-14 -->

Explanation: Belief perseverance is the tendency to keep a belief after the evidence that created it has been discredited. The halo effect involves one positive trait shaping overall impressions, which does not describe a belief that survives discredited evidence.

**A525.** C. Overconfidence (illusory superiority)
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-15 -->

Explanation: Overconfidence, or illusory superiority, is the tendency to overrate one's own abilities relative to others. The gambler's fallacy involves wrongly expecting chance outcomes to balance out, which has nothing to do with self-assessment.

**A526.** A. Framing effect
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-16 -->

Explanation: The framing effect is the tendency for the same information, presented in different ways, to change decisions. The availability heuristic depends on how easily examples come to mind, not on how information is worded.

**A527.** A. Base rate neglect
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-17 -->

Explanation: Base rate neglect is ignoring how common a condition is in the population when judging the probability for an individual. Anchoring involves being pulled towards a starting figure, which is not the main error in Maya's reasoning.

**A528.** B. Illusion of control
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-18 -->

Explanation: The illusion of control is the belief that one can influence outcomes that are actually determined by chance. The gambler's fallacy is the mistaken expectation that a random outcome is 'due' after a run of other outcomes, which is a different error.

**A529.** C. Gambler's fallacy
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-19 -->

Explanation: Each spin is independent, so a run of reds does not raise the chance of black, yet the gambler's fallacy expects a balancing outcome. Illusion of control would involve believing one can influence the spin, which Jin does not claim.

**A530.** C. Sunk cost fallacy
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-20 -->

Explanation: The sunk cost fallacy is continuing a course of action because of resources already spent, even though those costs cannot be recovered. Confirmation bias concerns seeking information that supports existing beliefs, which does not fit this decision about how to spend time.

**A531.** D. Optimism bias
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-21 -->

Explanation: Optimism bias is the tendency to believe that negative events are less likely to happen to oneself than to others. Self-serving bias concerns explaining one's own outcomes, not forecasting one's own future risk.

**A532.** C. Recognition heuristic
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-22 -->

Explanation: The recognition heuristic chooses the recognised option when only one option is familiar. The availability heuristic judges by ease of recalling examples, which is a different process from simply recognising a name.

**A533.** B. They are systematic errors in thinking that can occur without the person being aware of them
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-23 -->

Explanation: Cognitive biases are systematic patterns of error that often operate automatically and outside awareness. The other options are wrong because biases are not deliberate, affect people across all levels of intelligence, and cannot be eliminated by a single decision.

**A534.** D. Halo effect
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-24 -->

Explanation: The halo effect is when one positive trait shapes judgements about unrelated qualities. Confirmation bias would involve seeking evidence for an existing belief, rather than forming a general impression from a single trait.

**A535.** D. Conjunction fallacy
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-25 -->

Explanation: The conjunction fallacy is judging a combination of two events as more probable than one of them alone, because the combined description seems more representative of Linda. Base rate neglect involves ignoring how common a category is, which is not the error in this pairing.

**A536.** The first salary figure offered acts as an anchor or reference point [1]; later offers and the final agreement are adjusted insufficiently away from that figure, so the outcome is pulled towards it [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A537.** Hindsight bias [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A538.** Representativeness heuristic: the diagnosis is judged by how closely the symptoms resemble the doctor's prototype of the virus [1]; base rate (prevalence) information is ignored, so the likelihood of this rare virus is overestimated [1]; the judgement relies on resemblance rather than the statistical probability of the illness [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A539.** Sunk cost fallacy [1]; her decision is driven by past, unrecoverable costs rather than the expected future costs and benefits [1]; past investment should be irrelevant to whether further investment is worthwhile [1]; a strategy such as evaluating only future prospects or seeking an independent, objective review [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-29 -->

Explanation:

**A540.** When only one option is recognised, that option is chosen [1]; this can be accurate because recognition often correlates with the quantity being judged, such as a city's population [1]; it fails when recognition reflects factors unrelated to the judged criterion, such as a small town being well known because of a famous sporting team [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-30 -->

Explanation:

**A541.** The overall frequency or proportion of a condition or event in a population, which should inform judgements about an individual case [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-31 -->

Explanation:

**A542.** The tendency to overestimate how many other people share one's own beliefs, attitudes or behaviours [1]; the person treats their own views as typical of the majority [1]; this can lead to misperceiving social norms, such as believing most people support a policy simply because the person does [1]; it can also lead to inaccurate predictions of others' behaviour and a reduced understanding of differing views [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-32 -->

Explanation:

**A543.** The belief that one can influence outcomes that are actually determined by chance [1]; e.g. a player who believes that choosing 'lucky' numbers improves their chance of winning a lottery [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-33 -->

Explanation:

**A544.** Availability heuristic: likelihood is judged by how easily examples come to mind [1]; e.g. a resident who recalls several recent local burglaries overestimates the crime rate in their suburb [1]; representativeness heuristic: likelihood is judged by how closely something matches a prototype or category [1]; e.g. judging an unfamiliar man who is neatly dressed and punctual to be an accountant because he matches that stereotype [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-34 -->

Explanation:

**A545.** D. The availability heuristic: vivid, easily recalled examples seem more common
<!-- id: vce-psych12-hb-c1 -->

Explanation: Judgements of how likely something is are distorted by how easily examples come to mind. Driving is statistically far more dangerous per trip.

#### Obedience

**A546.** A. following the orders or commands of an authority figure
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-1 -->

Explanation: Obedience is a form of social influence where a person complies with directions from an authority figure. A describes conformity.

**A547.** D. Around 65%
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-2 -->

Explanation: In the baseline study 65% of participants delivered the maximum shock, a far higher proportion than psychiatrists had predicted.

**A548.** A. the experimenter left the room and gave instructions by telephone
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-3 -->

Explanation: Obedience fell when the authority figure was physically absent. The Yale setting added prestige and did not reduce obedience.

**A549.** D. Participants experienced psychological distress and were deceived about the true purpose
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-4 -->

Explanation: Deception and the distress caused to participants raised concerns about informed consent and protection from harm. Debriefing was provided after the study.

**A550.** A. The authority figure wears a uniform and appears legitimate
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-5 -->

Explanation: Legitimacy and symbols of authority such as a uniform increase obedience. B and C reduce it, and D (taking personal responsibility) also reduces it.

**A551.** Authority figure physically present/close to the participant; prestigious or legitimate setting (e.g. Yale), lab coat; learner at a distance from the participant; gradual escalation of shocks (any two, each described).
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-6 -->

Explanation:

**A552.** Perceived legitimacy and expertise of the authority figure (doctor in a hospital setting); diffusion of responsibility, believing the doctor is responsible for the outcome; a third factor such as the pressure of hierarchy or gradual escalation; at least two reasons linked to the scenario.
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-7 -->

Explanation:

**A553.** Breach identified, e.g. informed consent (participants were deceived), or protection from harm/withdrawal rights (participants were pressed to continue); explanation of how it is breached; modern approach, e.g. full debriefing, ethics committee approval, risk assessment, stopping at lower shock levels, or allowing withdrawal without pressure.
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-8 -->

Explanation:

**A554.** A. an actor who pretended to receive the shocks and gave scripted responses
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-9 -->

Explanation: The learner was a confederate who received no real shocks and followed a script of protests, while teachers believed he was a real volunteer. A genuine fellow volunteer in that role would have meant a real person receiving shocks, which Milgram avoided.

**A555.** B. The draw was rigged so the real participant always became the teacher and the confederate the learner
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-10 -->

Explanation: The draw looked random but was fixed, so the real participant was always the teacher and the confederate always the learner. Genuine random allocation would have given each person an equal chance of either role, so the computer option misses what actually happened.

**A556.** C. 30
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-11 -->

Explanation: Dividing 450 by 15 gives 30 switches, one for each 15-volt step. Choosing 15 confuses the number of switches with the lowest voltage on the generator.

**A557.** D. 40 men aged 20 to 50 from a range of occupations, recruited through newspaper advertisements
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-12 -->

Explanation: The baseline sample was 40 men aged 20 to 50 from varied occupations, recruited through newspaper advertising. The other options describe samples that Milgram did not use.

**A558.** A. 21 of the 22 nurses were prepared to give an unauthorised dose on the phone order of an unknown doctor
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-13 -->

Explanation: 21 of 22 nurses were prepared to give twice the stated maximum dose when a doctor they did not know ordered it by telephone. The refusal and pharmacist options contradict the finding, and the physical presence option is wrong because the doctor was not on the ward.

**A559.** B. seeing oneself as the agent of an authority figure, so responsibility is shifted to that authority
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-14 -->

Explanation: In an agentic state a person acts as the instrument of another's wishes and feels less responsible for what follows. Feeling personally responsible for every outcome describes the autonomous state, which Milgram contrasted with the agentic state.

**A560.** C. conformity, because she changed her behaviour to match the majority
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-15 -->

Explanation: Conformity is changing behaviour to match a group majority without a direct order. Obedience requires a command from an authority figure, and no one in this scenario gives one.

**A561.** D. women obeyed at roughly the same rate as men
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-16 -->

Explanation: Milgram found that women obeyed at roughly the same rate as men, which challenged the idea that gender strongly affects obedience. The other options contradict this finding.

**A562.** A. a human research ethics committee (HREC), which reviews a study before it begins
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-17 -->

Explanation: Research involving humans in Australia is normally reviewed by a human research ethics committee before it begins, to assess risks and the consent process. The other bodies do not approve research ethics, and supervisor review alone is not independent.

**A563.** B. It increases obedience, because the driver may feel less responsible for the outcome
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-18 -->

Explanation: Shifting responsibility to an authority encourages an agentic state, so people feel less personally responsible and comply more. The option that reduces obedience reverses this effect, and the no-effect option ignores the situational factors Milgram identified.

**A564.** C. nervous laughter, trembling and sweating
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-19 -->

Explanation: Many teachers showed distress through nervous laughter, trembling and sweating, yet most continued. The calm acceptance and cheering options misrepresent the observations, and immediate refusal was not the typical response.

**A565.** D. the highest shock level the teacher was willing to administer
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-20 -->

Explanation: Obedience was measured as the highest shock level a teacher was prepared to administer, with 450 volts representing full obedience. The other options are not the measure Milgram used.

**A566.** A. the authoritarian personality, a set of traits linked to submission to authority
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-21 -->

Explanation: Adorno and colleagues proposed the authoritarian personality, a dispositional explanation linking submission to authority with a set of traits. The agentic state is Milgram's situational concept, not a personality type.

**A567.** C. was highly standardised, which allowed it to be replicated
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-22 -->

Explanation: Standardised procedures, such as scripted prods and set shock levels, allowed other researchers to replicate the study. The other options are either false or describe weaknesses rather than strengths.

**A568.** Teachers experienced conflict between their own values (not harming another person) and the experimenter's instructions [1]; continuing despite this shows that the authority's demands can override personal conscience, a key finding of the study [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-23 -->

Explanation:

**A569.** Obedience is following a direct order or command from an authority figure [1]; conformity is changing behaviour to match a group or majority without a direct order [1]; valid examples of each, e.g. a soldier following a commander's order (obedience) and a teenager adopting the clothing style of friends (conformity) [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-24 -->

Explanation:

**A570.** Strength: the standardised procedure (set shock levels, scripted prods and a confederate learner) allowed the study to be replicated [1]; strength: later replications have supported the general finding that ordinary people obey strongly, adding reliability [1]; limitation: the original sample was small and all male, limiting generalisability [1]; limitation: deception and psychological distress raise serious ethical concerns [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-25 -->

Explanation:

**A571.** The prods were scripted instructions to continue (e.g. 'The experiment requires that you continue') [1]; they standardised the pressure so every participant received the same instructions, allowing fair comparison [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-26 -->

Explanation:

**A572.** The study was stopped for ethical reasons, to reduce the risk of psychological harm to participants [1]; 150 volts is where the learner first protests and asks to be released, so the most distressing shocks were avoided [1]; obedience up to that point was similar to Milgram's original results, supporting the replicability of the findings [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-27 -->

Explanation:

**A573.** An authority figure whose power is seen as rightful or accepted, for example because of their position, expertise or the institution they represent [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-28 -->

Explanation:

**A574.** Each step was only slightly higher than the last, so refusing at any point felt inconsistent with having already complied [1]; once teachers had continued through earlier levels they were committed to going on, a foot-in-the-door effect [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-29 -->

Explanation:

**A575.** Advantage: variables such as shock levels and prods can be controlled, so cause-and-effect conclusions and replication are possible [1]; limitation: the artificial setting may reduce ecological validity, so results may not generalise to everyday situations [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-30 -->

Explanation:

**A576.** Participants were ordinary people from varied occupations, and most obeyed to the maximum, suggesting obedience is common rather than limited to unusual personalities [1]; obedience changed substantially when situational features changed, such as the authority figure's presence or the learner's proximity, which fixed traits cannot explain [1]; so the situation is a key determinant of obedience [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-31 -->

Explanation:

**A577.** D. The experimenter gave instructions by telephone instead of in person
<!-- id: vce-psych12-ob-c1 -->

Explanation: Obedience fell when the authority figure was absent; the original set-up (learner in another room, at Yale) gave about 65% full obedience.

#### Conformity and group influence

**A578.** D. adjusting one's behaviour or beliefs to match those of a group or social norm
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-1 -->

Explanation: Conformity is changing behaviour or attitudes in response to real or imagined group pressure. A is obedience.

**A579.** B. all other group members, who were confederates, gave the same incorrect answer
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-2 -->

Explanation: The majority's unanimous incorrect responses led about a third of responses to be incorrect; having an ally who gave the right answer reduced conformity.

**A580.** D. normative influence leading to compliance
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-3 -->

Explanation: Normative influence is the desire to be liked or accepted, producing outward conformity without private acceptance.

**A581.** B. Having at least one other person disagree with the majority
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-4 -->

Explanation: Dissent from even one other person breaks unanimity and reduces conformity. Ambiguity increases conformity.

**A582.** B. individuals put in less effort when working in a group than when working alone
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-5 -->

Explanation: Social loafing is reduced individual effort in a group, often because contributions are not individually identifiable.

**A583.** Group size (conformity increases up to a point, e.g. 3 or 4 members); unanimity (a dissenter reduces conformity); also task ambiguity, group status or whether responses are public/private (any two, each described).
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-6 -->

Explanation:

**A584.** Social loafing; make individual contributions identifiable or assess them individually; use smaller groups or give each member a defined role; (any two strategies, linked to the concept).
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-7 -->

Explanation:

**A585.** Normative influence: conforming to be accepted or avoid rejection by the group (e.g. wearing a brand because friends do); informational influence: conforming because the group is seen as having correct information in an ambiguous situation (e.g. copying others' choice of exit in an unfamiliar building); clear difference in motivation (belonging vs accuracy).
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-8 -->

Explanation:

**A586.** B. informational influence, where people look to others to judge an ambiguous situation
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-9 -->

Explanation: The light gave no objective reference point, so the situation was ambiguous and participants used others' estimates as a guide to accuracy. Normative influence is wrong because the aim was to be right, not to be accepted by the group.

**A587.** C. collectivist cultures, which emphasise group harmony and belonging
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-10 -->

Explanation: Collectivist cultures value group harmony and interdependence, so their members are more inclined to align with the group. Individualist cultures tend to show lower conformity, so a similar rate in every culture is also wrong.

**A588.** D. decreased, showing social loafing
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-11 -->

Explanation: Output per person fell as group size grew, which is the reduction in individual effort known as social loafing. An increase in per-person output would be the opposite of loafing, and no change would mean group size had no effect.

**A589.** B. group discussion to strengthen members' initial shared views, so the group decision becomes more extreme than the average individual decision
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-12 -->

Explanation: Discussion pushes the group towards a more extreme version of the view its members already shared, whether that view was cautious or risky. A compromise between extremes is a different process and is not what polarisation describes.

**A590.** A. an illusion of invulnerability, in which members believe the group cannot make a serious mistake
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-13 -->

Explanation: Groupthink includes an illusion of invulnerability, in which members believe the group cannot make a serious mistake. The other options describe open, critical decision making, which is the opposite of groupthink.

**A591.** C. are anonymous in a large crowd and feel less personally accountable
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-14 -->

Explanation: Anonymity in a crowd reduces self-awareness and personal accountability, which can weaken normal restraint. Being identifiable or working alone with personal goals increases self-awareness, so these options describe conditions that reduce deindividuation.

**A592.** B. lower than when answers were given publicly
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-15 -->

Explanation: Responding publicly increases conformity because participants fear disapproval, and private responding removes some of that pressure. Private responding reduces conformity but does not remove it entirely, so 'eliminated entirely' is a common overstatement.

**A593.** C. is consistent and confident in its position over time
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-16 -->

Explanation: Consistency over time makes a minority appear confident and can lead the majority to reconsider its view. Random or inconsistent answers reduce the minority's credibility, so they are a common mistake.

**A594.** A. 12
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-17 -->

Explanation: Asch used 18 trials, of which 12 were critical trials with unanimous incorrect answers from confederates and 6 were neutral. Using all 18 trials, or counting only the 6 neutral ones, are the common errors.

**A595.** D. 33%
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-18 -->

Explanation: 4 ÷ 12 = 0.33, so 33%. Dividing by all 18 trials gives 22%, which wrongly includes the neutral trials where conformity could not occur.

**A596.** B. informational influence, as passengers use others' inaction as evidence about the situation
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-19 -->

Explanation: Passengers treat others' lack of response as evidence about how serious the situation is, which is informational influence in an ambiguous setting. Normative influence would apply if the main concern were being judged by others rather than finding out what is happening.

**A597.** C. they knew the correct answer but gave the group's answer to avoid looking foolish
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-20 -->

Explanation: Asch's interviews revealed that some participants knew the correct answer but gave the group's answer to avoid looking foolish. This shows that conformity can occur without any change in private belief.

**A598.** A. a task that team members see as meaningful and important
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-21 -->

Explanation: People loaf less when the task matters to them, because their own motivation rises. Measuring only the group total and sharing rewards regardless of effort encourage loafing, so they are the wrong direction.

**A599.** B. compliance
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-22 -->

Explanation: Compliance is changing behaviour to gain a reward or avoid punishment without private acceptance, and here the goggles are worn only when observed. Internalisation would mean genuinely accepting the rule, and informational influence would apply only if the worker were unsure what was safe.

**A600.** D. the need to be liked and accepted by others
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-23 -->

Explanation: The need to be liked and accepted underlies normative influence, while the need for accurate information underlies informational influence. Independence and obedience are not the second need in this model, so they are tempting but wrong.

**A601.** C. about 75%
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-24 -->

Explanation: Asch found that about three-quarters of participants conformed at least once, even though on average only about a third of critical responses were incorrect.

**A602.** A. highly cohesive, isolated from outside opinions and under time pressure
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-25 -->

Explanation: Janis linked groupthink to high cohesion, isolation from outside views, a directive leader and time pressure, which together discourage members from voicing doubts. The other options describe conditions that promote critical thinking, so they are tempting but wrong.

**A603.** A naive participant was seated among confederates who had been instructed in advance to give answers [1]; the group judged which of three comparison lines matched a standard line over 18 trials [1]; on the 12 critical trials the confederates unanimously gave the same incorrect answer, and the participant usually answered last [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-26 -->

Explanation:

**A604.** Compliance: changing behaviour to gain a reward or avoid punishment, without private acceptance [1]; identification: adopting behaviour to maintain a satisfying relationship with a respected person or group, with private beliefs possibly unchanged [1]; internalisation: genuinely accepting the belief or behaviour, so it is maintained even when no one is watching [1]; the key difference is motivation and permanence, as compliance and identification depend on the social context while internalisation depends on the person's own values [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-27 -->

Explanation:

**A605.** Diffusion of responsibility: each bystander feels less personally responsible for acting, so responsibility is shared among those present [1]; informational influence: bystanders look to one another, see no one acting, and conclude that help is not needed [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-28 -->

Explanation:

**A606.** Anonymity in a crowd reduces a person's sense of individual identity and accountability [1]; lower self-awareness weakens normal self-restraint and personal moral standards [1]; the crowd's emotional arousal and norms can then encourage behaviour, such as vandalism, that the person would not normally perform [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-29 -->

Explanation:

**A607.** Self-censorship: the directors with doubts keep quiet [1]; pressure on dissenters: the chair labels critics as disloyal [1]; illusion of unanimity: the board treats silence as agreement [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-30 -->

Explanation:

**A608.** Conformity: changing behaviour or beliefs to match a group or social norm, without a direct order [1]; obedience: following a direct order from an authority figure [1]; example of conformity: a student wears the same style of clothing as her friends to fit in [1]; example of obedience: a nurse gives a patient a medication dose because a doctor instructs it [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-31 -->

Explanation:

**A609.** An estimate formed through group interaction continued to be used by participants when they were later tested alone [1]; this showed the norm had been accepted privately, not just displayed for others [1]; participants relied on the group's estimate as a guide to accuracy because the task was ambiguous [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-32 -->

Explanation:

**A610.** An actor who secretly works with the researcher and gives pre-arranged answers [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-33 -->

Explanation:

**A611.** Average output = 28 ÷ 4 = 7 units per student [1]; this is less than the 10 units each student produced alone [1]; the reduction in individual effort in a group is consistent with social loafing [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-34 -->

Explanation:

**A612.** Limitation: the task was artificial and low-stakes, so it has low ecological validity [1]; limitation: the sample was an all-male group of American college students, so the findings may not generalise to women or other cultures [1]; strength: the controlled procedure and clear measure of incorrect answers allowed reliable comparison across conditions [1]; limitation: participants were deceived by confederates and may have experienced stress, raising ethical concerns [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-35 -->

Explanation:

**A613.** Appointing a devil's advocate to challenge the proposal [1], which encourages critical discussion and reduces the pressure to agree [1]; asking members to form and record independent views before the group discusses the issue [1], so that doubts are voiced rather than suppressed [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-36 -->

Explanation:

**A614.** C. One other group member (a confederate) gave the correct answer
<!-- id: vce-psych12-cg-c1 -->

Explanation: A single dissenter breaks the group's unanimity, reducing normative pressure. Answering privately also reduces conformity.

### Unit 2 AOS 2: Perception

#### Sensation and the visual system

**A615.** D. the detection and initial registration of stimulus energy by sensory receptors
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-1 -->

Explanation: Sensation involves receptors detecting stimulus energy and converting it into neural signals; perception is the interpretation of those signals.

**A616.** C. Retina
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-2 -->

Explanation: The retina contains the photoreceptors (rods and cones). The cornea and lens focus light and the optic nerve carries signals to the brain.

**A617.** D. more sensitive to low light and found mainly in the periphery of the retina
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-3 -->

Explanation: Rods function in dim light and are concentrated in the periphery; cones are concentrated in the fovea and are responsible for colour and detail.

**A618.** D. accommodation
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-4 -->

Explanation: Accommodation is the adjustment of the lens shape. Transduction is the conversion of light energy to neural signals.

**A619.** B. dark adaptation as rods become more sensitive
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-5 -->

Explanation: Dark adaptation involves increased sensitivity of rods, taking several minutes. Light adaptation is the reverse.

**A620.** Light enters through the cornea and passes through the pupil and lens (focused by accommodation); falls on the retina where rods and cones detect it (transduction into neural signals); signals travel via bipolar and ganglion cells through the optic nerve; to the thalamus and then the visual cortex in the occipital lobe.
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-6 -->

Explanation:

**A621.** The fovea contains a high density of cones, which provide sharp detail and colour vision, so reading is impaired; the periphery of the retina has mostly rods, which remain intact; rods detect motion and low-light stimuli in the periphery.
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-7 -->

Explanation:

**A622.** Sensation: detection of light by photoreceptors and conversion into neural signals; perception: the brain's organisation and interpretation of these signals, e.g. recognising a face.
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-8 -->

Explanation:

**A623.** A. cornea
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-9 -->

Explanation: The cornea bends incoming light and supplies about two-thirds of the eye's focusing power. The lens makes fine adjustments, the pupil is only an opening, and the retina is where light is detected.

**A624.** B. the opening in the iris through which light passes into the eye
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-10 -->

Explanation: The pupil is the opening in the centre of the iris, and the iris muscles change its size to regulate the light entering the eye. The coloured ring is the iris itself, and lens shape is changed by the ciliary muscles, not by the iris.

**A625.** C. the optic nerve leaves the eye at a point with no photoreceptors
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-11 -->

Explanation: The optic disc, where the optic nerve leaves the eye, has no rods or cones, so light falling there is not detected. A tempting wrong answer is the claim about the fovea, but the fovea is made up of cones rather than rods.

**A626.** D. sclera
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-12 -->

Explanation: The sclera is the tough white outer layer of the eyeball. The retina is the light-sensitive inner layer, the iris controls pupil size, and the choroid is the layer that carries blood to the retina.

**A627.** A. the ciliary muscles contract, making the lens thicker and more curved
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-13 -->

Explanation: Contracting the ciliary muscles slackens the suspensory ligaments, so the lens rounds up and gains focusing power for near objects. Relaxed ciliary muscles flatten the lens for distant objects, and turning both eyes inward is convergence, not a change in the lens.

**A628.** B. the central region of the retina with the highest concentration of cones and the sharpest vision
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-14 -->

Explanation: The fovea is a small central region packed with cones, which gives the sharpest vision and colour detail. Rods are concentrated in the periphery rather than the fovea, which is why a faint object is often seen better when looked at slightly to one side.

**A629.** C. sensory adaptation
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-15 -->

Explanation: Sensory adaptation is a decline in responsiveness to a constant, unchanging stimulus, so the hum fades from awareness. Transduction is the conversion of energy into neural signals, accommodation is a change in lens shape, and perceptual constancy describes stable perception of objects despite changes in the sensory input.

**A630.** D. the wavelengths of light each type responds to most strongly
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-16 -->

Explanation: The three cone types respond most strongly to short, medium and long wavelengths (roughly blue, green and red), which underlies trichromatic colour vision. Size, location and adaptation speed do not separate the cone types.

**A631.** A. fibres from the nasal half of each retina cross to the opposite side of the brain
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-17 -->

Explanation: At the optic chiasm, nasal fibres from each retina cross over, so each hemisphere receives signals from the opposite visual field. A tempting wrong answer is that each eye sends all its fibres to the same side, which ignores this crossing.

**A632.** B. red from green
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-18 -->

Explanation: Medium-wavelength (green-sensitive) cones are needed to separate red from green signals, so losing them causes red-green colour deficiency. Losing short-wavelength cones affects blue-yellow discrimination, and rods detect light intensity rather than colour.

**A633.** C. It occurs quickly, as photoreceptors reduce their sensitivity in bright light.
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-19 -->

Explanation: Light adaptation is a reduction in photoreceptor sensitivity in bright conditions, and it happens within minutes, much faster than dark adaptation. The pupil does change size, but that reflex is separate and does not define light adaptation.

**A634.** D. greater sensitivity to dim light but poorer detail in the periphery
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-20 -->

Explanation: Pooling signals from many rods makes the periphery very sensitive to dim light, but the pooled signal cannot show fine detail. The foveal cones have more direct connections, giving detailed vision that needs brighter light.

**A635.** A. receive signals from photoreceptors and pass them on to ganglion cells
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-21 -->

Explanation: Bipolar cells form the middle relay layer, passing signals from the photoreceptors forward to the ganglion cells. The photopigment is contained in the photoreceptors themselves, and the thalamus receives visual signals through the optic nerve rather than directly from bipolar cells.

**A636.** B. occipital lobe
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-22 -->

Explanation: The primary visual cortex lies at the back of the brain in the occipital lobe. The temporal and parietal lobes contain other visual processing areas, while the frontal lobe is mainly involved in planning and movement.

**A637.** C. lateral geniculate nucleus of the thalamus
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-23 -->

Explanation: Visual signals synapse in the lateral geniculate nucleus of the thalamus before being sent on to the primary visual cortex. The hypothalamus, hippocampus and cerebellum are involved in other functions such as regulation, memory and coordination.

**A638.** D. convergence
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-24 -->

Explanation: Convergence is the inward turning of both eyes for a near object. Accommodation is the lens changing shape, transduction is the conversion of light energy into neural signals, and adaptation is a change in sensitivity.

**A639.** A. Light must pass through the ganglion and bipolar cell layers before reaching the photoreceptors.
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-25 -->

Explanation: In the retina, light passes through the ganglion and bipolar cell layers before reaching the photoreceptors, which lie at the back next to the pigment layer and choroid. Students often assume light hits the photoreceptors first, which is not the case.

**A640.** B. vitreous humour
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-26 -->

Explanation: The vitreous humour fills the main chamber behind the lens and supports the eyeball's shape. The aqueous humour fills the chamber in front of the lens, while the choroid and cornea are tissue layers rather than fluids.

**A641.** C. carries neural signals from the retina to the brain
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-27 -->

Explanation: The optic nerve is a bundle of ganglion cell axons that transmits visual signals from the retina to the brain. Transduction occurs in the photoreceptors, focusing is done by the cornea and lens, and light entry is controlled by the iris.

**A642.** The iris contracts or relaxes its muscles to change the size of the pupil [1]; the pupil constricts in bright light to limit the light entering, and dilates in dim light to admit more [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-28 -->

Explanation:

**A643.** The cornea bends incoming light and provides most of the eye's focusing power [1]; the lens makes fine adjustments to the focus [1]; the ciliary muscles change the shape of the lens so that light from objects at different distances is focused on the retina [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-29 -->

Explanation:

**A644.** The blind spot in each eye falls in a different part of the visual field, so the other eye detects that region [1]; the brain also fills in the gap using information from the surrounding visual field [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-30 -->

Explanation:

**A645.** Faint light is detected mainly by rods [1]; rods are concentrated in the periphery of the retina and are largely absent from the fovea [1]; looking to the side places the image of the star on the rod-rich periphery, increasing sensitivity [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-31 -->

Explanation:

**A646.** The right visual cortex receives information from the left visual field of both eyes [1]; at the optic chiasm, fibres from the nasal half of each retina cross to the opposite hemisphere, sending left visual field signals to the right hemisphere [1]; damage therefore causes loss of vision in the left visual field of both eyes [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-32 -->

Explanation:

**A647.** Transduction is the conversion of light energy into neural signals by photoreceptors in the retina [1]; accommodation is the change in lens shape that allows light from objects at different distances to be focused on the retina [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-33 -->

Explanation:

**A648.** The area of the external world that can be seen at one moment when the eyes are fixed on a single point [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-34 -->

Explanation:

**A649.** Loss of colour vision, because cones are required to detect colour [1]; reduced visual acuity and detail, because cones are concentrated in the fovea and are needed for sharp vision [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-35 -->

Explanation:

**A650.** The claim is incorrect [1]; rods do not detect colour, as colour vision requires cones [1]; in low light cones are much less active, so colours appear faded and the scene is seen mainly in shades of grey [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-36 -->

Explanation:

**A651.** The iris muscles dilate the pupil within seconds, letting more light reach the retina [1]; this is a fast reflex that changes the amount of light entering the eye [1]; rods and cones increase their sensitivity through regeneration of photopigment, which takes minutes to around 30 minutes [1]; so overall sensitivity adjusts much more slowly than the pupil does [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-sv-37 -->

Explanation:

**A652.** B. Reception, transduction, transmission, selection, organisation, interpretation
<!-- id: vce-psych12-sv-c1 -->

Explanation: Sensation: light is received, converted (transduced) by rods and cones, and transmitted along the optic nerve. Perception: the brain selects, organises and interprets.

#### Top-down and bottom-up processing

**A653.** A. the raw sensory input from a stimulus
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-1 -->

Explanation: Bottom-up (data-driven) processing builds perception from sensory input. Top-down (conceptually driven) processing begins with prior knowledge and expectations.

**A654.** C. top-down processing
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-2 -->

Explanation: Using context and prior knowledge to interpret ambiguous input is top-down processing.

**A655.** A. bottom-up processing, because there is little prior knowledge to guide interpretation
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-3 -->

Explanation: For unfamiliar stimuli, perception is driven mainly by the sensory features themselves (bottom-up).

**A656.** D. The person's expectations, motivation and past experience
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-4 -->

Explanation: Top-down processing is shaped by cognitive factors such as expectation, motivation, culture and context. Stimulus features influence bottom-up processing.

**A657.** B. top-down processing influenced by expectation and emotion
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-5 -->

Explanation: Identical sensory input was interpreted differently owing to expectations and emotional state, a top-down effect.

**A658.** Bottom-up: perception built from the sensory features of the stimulus upward to recognition; example, e.g. noticing the shapes, edges and colours of an unfamiliar object; top-down: perception guided by prior knowledge, context and expectation; example, e.g. reading messy handwriting by using context.
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-6 -->

Explanation:

**A659.** Their perception is influenced by expectations, prior experience or context (e.g. recent priming or interests) rather than only the stimulus; this leads to different interpretations of the same sensory input.
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-7 -->

Explanation:

**A660.** Bottom-up processing registers the visual features of the object (shape, colour, edges); top-down processing uses knowledge and context (e.g. a kitchen setting) to interpret the features and recognise it quickly; together they allow efficient, accurate perception, especially when input is incomplete.
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-8 -->

Explanation:

**A661.** A. top-down processing filling in missing sensory information using knowledge of the word
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-9 -->

Explanation: Knowledge of the word lets the listener fill in the missing sound, which is top-down processing. Registering the cough as a sound describes bottom-up processing, and transduction and adaptation do not explain the completed word.

**A662.** C. top-down processing based on stored knowledge of chess patterns
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-10 -->

Explanation: The expert's stored knowledge of patterns organises the same board into meaningful units, which is top-down processing. The idea that the novice's piece-by-piece processing is superior reverses the actual difference, since the novice lacks the expectations that group the pieces.

**A663.** B. Surrounding context leads to top-down processing that resolves the ambiguous shape differently in each sequence
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-11 -->

Explanation: The shape is identical in both sequences, so the difference comes from expectations set by the surrounding characters rather than from the physical input. Options suggesting different physical features or different sensory processes miss the role of context.

**A664.** C. bottom-up processing, because perception starts from the sensory features of the sounds
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-12 -->

Explanation: Recognition here is built from the sound features with no stored meaning to guide it, so it is bottom-up. The top-down option is tempting because listeners often predict words, but this listener has no knowledge of the language to make predictions.

**A665.** A. It draws on stored knowledge, expectations and context to interpret sensory input
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-13 -->

Explanation: Top-down processing draws on prior knowledge, expectation and context, and it contributes to perception even when input is clear. The 'cortex down to receptors' reading misunderstands the term, and transduction is a separate process.

**A666.** D. top-down processing, because the price expectation shaped how the taste was interpreted
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-14 -->

Explanation: The wine was identical, so the difference must come from the price expectation shaping how the taste was interpreted, which is top-down processing. Options that point to the wine's chemistry or to taste receptors ignore that the input was the same.

**A667.** B. Top-down expectation of a grammatical sentence makes the second word seem to belong, so the repeat goes unnoticed
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-15 -->

Explanation: Expecting a grammatical sentence makes the repeated word seem to fit, so the reader perceives the text as they expect it to be, which is top-down processing. Careful bottom-up reading of each letter would be more likely to catch the repeat.

**A668.** A. Top-down processing is shaping how the same sensory input is interpreted, which can make expected features more likely to be seen
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-16 -->

Explanation: Prior information creates an expectation that influences what is perceived from the same image, a top-down effect. The idea that bottom-up processing switches off is a misconception, because the sensory features are still registered.

**A669.** C. the value of combining bottom-up feature analysis with top-down use of context
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-17 -->

Explanation: Human reading combines the detail of the stimulus with context and expectation, which is why a smudged word is easy to read in a sentence. Claims that bottom-up analysis is superior, or that transduction causes the errors, do not explain the contrast.

**A670.** A. The stimulus being presented clearly and for a long time
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-18 -->

Explanation: A clear, prolonged stimulus gives strong bottom-up information, which reduces reliance on expectation. The other conditions all increase the role of prior knowledge, expectation or emotion in interpretation.

**A671.** D. top-down processing, because prior exposure creates an expectation that speeds recognition
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-19 -->

Explanation: The earlier word creates a context that makes a related word easier to recognise, which is a top-down effect. Bottom-up options describe the stimulus itself, and sensory adaptation to letters has nothing to do with meaning.

**A672.** B. Neither type is more accurate in general; each has strengths, and perception usually combines them
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-20 -->

Explanation: Perception usually combines sensory features with context and expectation, and each contributes strengths. Top-down processing can mislead when expectations are wrong, but neither type is always more accurate, so the student's claim is not correct.

**A673.** D. detection of edges and features, then combining them into a shape, then recognition as an object
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-21 -->

Explanation: Bottom-up processing builds perception upwards from the features of the stimulus to the whole object. The other orders begin with recognition, memory or expectation, which are features of top-down processing.

**A674.** A. Prior experience and cultural expectations shape how the same sensory input is interpreted
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-22 -->

Explanation: Cultural background shapes expectations and interpretation of the same input, which is a top-down influence. Eye structure and receptor counts are largely shared across people, and all people use bottom-up processing.

**A675.** B. Using the context of an intersection to expect a stop sign
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-23 -->

Explanation: Expecting a stop sign because of the intersection draws on context and prior knowledge, which is top-down. The colour, outline and edges are sensory features, so they are bottom-up.

**A676.** C. Perception combines sensory input with prior knowledge, expectation and context
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-24 -->

Explanation: Perception is an interpretation of sensory input that draws on both bottom-up and top-down processes. The 'passive recording' and 'receptors only' ideas ignore the role of knowledge and context in what is perceived.

**A677.** D. Recognising a familiar song from its first few notes because the listener knows the whole tune
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-25 -->

Explanation: Knowledge of the song lets the listener recognise it from very little sensory input, which is top-down processing. The other options describe the physical or neural processing of sound, which is transduction or bottom-up analysis.

**A678.** Stored knowledge of the brand and its logo fills in the hidden part of the image [1]; the shopper's expectation of finding that product guides how the visible features are interpreted [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-26 -->

Explanation:

**A679.** The sensory input is ambiguous, so the bottom-up features alone do not form a recognised face [1]; being told there is a face creates an expectation that is used to organise the blotches [1]; the same input is now interpreted differently, so the perception changes [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-27 -->

Explanation:

**A680.** An unfamiliar stimulus with no relevant prior knowledge, such as encountering a new object or species [1]; perception must then be built from the sensory features themselves [1]; relying on expectations could lead to a misperception because the wrong expectations would be applied [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-28 -->

Explanation:

**A681.** Early reading relies mainly on bottom-up processing, decoding each letter and sound from its sensory features [1]; as skill develops, stored knowledge of words and spelling patterns allows top-down processing [1]; the skilled reader uses context and expectation to recognise words quickly [1]; bottom-up features are still used for unfamiliar or misspelt words [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-29 -->

Explanation:

**A682.** Top-down processing, because the tourist's expectation shapes the interpretation of the sign [1]; the expectation overrides the sensory detail, so the misreading occurs [1]; the error could be reduced by attending more closely to the sensory features of the sign, such as reading each word carefully [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-30 -->

Explanation:

**A683.** Perception also depends on expectations, prior experience and context, which can influence the interpretation of clear input [1]; so the brain may interpret the input in a way that does not match what is actually present, for example by seeing what is expected [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-31 -->

Explanation:

**A684.** Context provides extra information about the likely meaning of an ambiguous stimulus [1]; it narrows the range of possible interpretations, so the most likely one is perceived [1]; e.g. reading the word 'bank' as a river bank when it appears beside a picture of water [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-32 -->

Explanation:

**A685.** Bottom-up: the faint lights and edges are registered from the sensory input [1]; top-down: the pilot's expectation of a landing strip guides the interpretation of those features [1]; both combine to form the perception [1]; an error could arise if the expectation leads the pilot to perceive a runway where the lights do not show one [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-33 -->

Explanation:

**A686.** Perception built up from the raw sensory features of a stimulus, without relying on prior knowledge or expectation [1].
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-34 -->

Explanation:

**A687.** A. Top-down processing, using context and expectations
<!-- id: vce-psych12-td-c1 -->

Explanation: Top-down processing uses prior knowledge and context to interpret incomplete sensory information.

#### Depth cues and Gestalt principles

**A688.** B. linear perspective
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-1 -->

Explanation: Linear perspective is the apparent convergence of parallel lines with distance. Texture gradient refers to a change in the density of surface detail, not converging lines.

**A689.** C. Retinal disparity
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-2 -->

Explanation: Retinal disparity depends on the slightly different images received by each eye. The other three are monocular cues that can be used with one eye.

**A690.** C. relative size
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-3 -->

Explanation: Relative size: when objects are known or assumed to be the same size, the one casting the smaller retinal image is judged to be further away. Interposition would require one object to partially block another.

**A691.** D. closure
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-4 -->

Explanation: Closure is the tendency to fill in gaps to perceive a complete, whole object. Proximity concerns grouping by nearness.

**A692.** D. Similarity
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-5 -->

Explanation: Similarity: elements that look alike (here, colour) are grouped together. If the squares were grouped because they were close together, it would be proximity.

**A693.** Monocular cues require input from only one eye (1); binocular cues require input from both eyes and depend on their different positions (1); example of monocular: relative size, interposition, linear perspective, texture gradient; example of binocular: retinal disparity or convergence (1 for both examples correct).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-6 -->

Explanation:

**A694.** Figure-ground is the tendency to organise a visual scene into a distinct object (the figure) that stands out against its background (1); we perceive the figure as having a defined form and the ground as extending behind it (1); a logo could use contrasting colours so that the shape or lettering clearly stands out as the figure, or deliberately use ambiguity so that the negative space forms a second image (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-7 -->

Explanation:

**A695.** Interposition (overlap) (1); the object that partially blocks the view of another is perceived as closer, and the partly hidden object as further away (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-8 -->

Explanation:

**A696.** D. convergence
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-9 -->

Explanation: Convergence is the inward turning of both eyes to focus on a near object, and the muscle effort signals its distance. Accommodation is a change in lens shape, and retinal disparity is the difference between the two retinal images rather than the eye movement itself.

**A697.** A. accommodation
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-10 -->

Explanation: Accommodation is the change in lens shape for focusing, and the muscle tension signals distance; it works with one eye. Convergence needs both eyes turning inwards, and the reader is not moving, so motion parallax is not involved.

**A698.** B. texture gradient
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-11 -->

Explanation: Texture gradient is the change in the size and density of surface detail with distance. Relative size judges the size of whole objects, and aerial perspective depends on haze rather than surface detail.

**A699.** C. proximity
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-12 -->

Explanation: Proximity is the tendency to group elements that are close together. Similarity groups elements that look alike, and common fate groups elements that move together, so neither explains grouping by spacing.

**A700.** A. figure-ground
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-13 -->

Explanation: Figure-ground organisation separates an image into a figure that stands out and a background, and in this image either region can be the figure. Closure concerns filling in gaps, so it does not explain the reversal.

**A701.** C. interposition, because the fence is judged to be in front of the house
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-14 -->

Explanation: Interposition (overlap) is a monocular cue in which the object that blocks another is judged to be closer. Relative size concerns the size of images, and retinal disparity needs two eyes, so it is not shown in a single drawing.

**A702.** D. similarity
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-15 -->

Explanation: Similarity groups elements that share a feature such as colour, so the spread-out fans are seen as one team. Proximity would require the fans to be grouped by how close they are to one another.

**A703.** A. interposition, linear perspective, relative size
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-16 -->

Explanation: Interposition, linear perspective and relative size can each be used with one eye. Each of the other lists contains a binocular cue, either retinal disparity or convergence.

**A704.** B. relative size
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-17 -->

Explanation: Relative size is a monocular depth cue that judges distance from the size of images. Proximity, similarity and closure are all Gestalt grouping principles, so the answer is the depth cue.

**A705.** C. retinal disparity
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-18 -->

Explanation: Retinal disparity is the difference between the images received by the two eyes, and the 3D effect exploits this difference. Motion parallax needs movement of the observer or the objects, and the other two options are monocular cues.

**A706.** D. texture gradient and linear perspective
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-19 -->

Explanation: Tiles that shrink and crowd together show texture gradient, and walls that converge show linear perspective. The other options either contain binocular cues or name cues, such as aerial perspective, that the photograph does not show.

**A707.** A. Closer objects produce a larger difference between the two retinal images
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-20 -->

Explanation: Because the eyes are separated, a nearby object projects to more different positions on each retina than a distant one. Disparity is binocular, so it does not work when one eye is closed.

**A708.** B. They describe how elements are grouped into organised wholes
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-21 -->

Explanation: Gestalt principles describe how the brain groups separate elements into organised wholes. Monocular cues and binocular disparity are depth cues, which form a different category and are not Gestalt principles.

**A709.** C. Convergence is the inward turning of the eyes, while disparity is the difference between the two retinal images
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-22 -->

Explanation: Convergence is the eye-movement signal for near objects, and disparity is the difference between the two retinal images; both are binocular. The other options swap these definitions, attribute them to lens shape or haze, or wrongly call them monocular.

**A710.** Convergence is the inward turning of the two eyes to focus on a near object, with the muscle effort used as a distance signal [1]; it is a binocular cue because it requires both eyes [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-23 -->

Explanation:

**A711.** Accommodation [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-24 -->

Explanation:

**A712.** The eyes are set apart horizontally, so each eye views the object from a slightly different position [1]; the difference is larger for nearby objects, which the brain uses as a depth cue [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-25 -->

Explanation:

**A713.** Texture gradient is the change in the size and density of surface detail as distance increases [1]; near blades of grass are large and widely spaced, while distant ones are small and tightly packed [1]; the smooth change in detail signals a surface that extends away from the viewer, so the far end is judged to be further away [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-26 -->

Explanation:

**A714.** Partly valid: relative size suggests the smaller bird is further away if both birds are assumed to be the same size [1]; however, the birds may differ in actual size, so relative size alone is unreliable [1]; a second cue, such as interposition if one bird partly blocks the other, or aerial perspective, would support or refute the judgement [1]; the claim is therefore reasonable only when other cues confirm it [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-27 -->

Explanation:

**A715.** Similarity groups elements that share a visual feature such as colour [1]; all red lines are perceived as one type of service and all blue lines as another [1]; the commuter can therefore identify the train network quickly without reading every label [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-28 -->

Explanation:

**A716.** The friend's outline or clothing is organised as the figure that stands out against the crowd as background [1]; because the figure is perceived as a distinct object, the person can locate it quickly [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dcg-29 -->

Explanation:

**A717.** B. Linear perspective, interposition and accommodation
<!-- id: vce-psych12-dc-c1 -->

Explanation: Monocular cues (pictorial cues and accommodation) work with one eye. Retinal disparity and convergence are binocular cues.

#### Gustatory perception

**A718.** A. taste buds, which sit within papillae on the tongue
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-1 -->

Explanation: Taste receptor cells are grouped in taste buds, most of which are found in papillae on the tongue. The olfactory epithelium holds smell receptors.

**A719.** D. Umami
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-2 -->

Explanation: The five basic tastes are sweet, sour, salty, bitter and umami. Spicy heat is detected as pain/temperature, not as a basic taste.
