**A720.** A. the close interaction between gustatory and olfactory sensation in flavour perception
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-3 -->

Explanation: Much of what we call flavour depends on olfaction; a blocked nose reduces smell input, so food seems bland even though taste receptors still work.

**A721.** D. supertaster
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-4 -->

Explanation: Supertasters have a higher density of taste buds and papillae, and experience certain tastes, especially bitter, more intensely.

**A722.** A. receptor cells in taste buds, then sensory neurons, then the gustatory cortex
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-5 -->

Explanation: Chemicals dissolved in saliva stimulate receptor cells in taste buds, which transduce the stimulus into neural impulses sent along sensory neurons to the gustatory cortex (parietal/insula region).

**A723.** Chemicals from the food dissolve in saliva and contact taste receptor cells in taste buds within papillae on the tongue (1); the receptors transduce the chemical stimulus into neural impulses that travel along sensory neurons (1); the information reaches the gustatory cortex where it is interpreted as sweet taste (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A724.** Flavour is the combined perception of taste and smell (1); odour molecules from food travel to the olfactory receptors at the back of the nasal cavity while chewing/swallowing, and the brain integrates this with gustatory input (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A725.** Supertasters have more taste buds/papillae (1); so they would rate the drink as much more intensely bitter (or find it unpleasant) than non-tasters, who would rate it as mild (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A726.** B. hydrogen ions (H⁺) from acidic substances enter taste receptor cells
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-9 -->

Explanation: Sour taste is triggered by hydrogen ions from acids entering taste receptor cells. The salt option mixes up sour with salty, which is triggered by sodium ions.

**A727.** C. in a V-shaped row towards the back of the tongue
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-10 -->

Explanation: Circumvallate papillae form a V-shaped row at the back of the tongue. Fungiform papillae, which are mushroom-shaped, sit on the front and sides, so the tip is not the only place with taste buds.

**A728.** A. contain no taste buds and are mainly involved in texture and grip
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-11 -->

Explanation: Filiform papillae are the most numerous type but have no taste buds, so they do not contribute to taste. Circumvallate papillae hold the most taste buds per papilla, which is why the option claiming filiform papillae hold the most is wrong.

**A729.** D. about 10 days
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-12 -->

Explanation: Taste receptor cells last around 10 days before being replaced from surrounding cells. Claims of a year or a lifetime overstate how long these cells last.

**A730.** B. adaptation
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-13 -->

Explanation: Adaptation is the gradual reduction in sensitivity to a continuing stimulus. Sensory interaction describes one sense influencing another, and conditioned taste aversion is a learned dislike of a food, so neither describes a fall in sensitivity.

**A731.** C. conditioned taste aversion, a learned link between a food and illness
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-14 -->

Explanation: Conditioned taste aversion is a learned avoidance of a food that has been paired with illness, and it can form after a single pairing. Supertasting describes heightened sensitivity to bitterness, not a learned dislike.

**A732.** A. sweet
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-15 -->

Explanation: Newborns are born with a liking for sweet tastes and tend to reject bitter and sour ones, which suggests a biological basis. Bitter is rejected rather than preferred, so it cannot be the answer.

**A733.** D. variation in a gene that codes for bitter taste receptors
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-16 -->

Explanation: Variation in genes such as TAS2R38 affects how strongly people perceive bitter compounds like PTC and PROP. Olfactory receptor density and childhood salt exposure do not explain this difference in bitterness.

**A734.** B. a myth, since receptors for all basic tastes occur across the tongue, with only small differences in sensitivity
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-17 -->

Explanation: Receptors for every basic taste are found across the tongue, and the original tongue map exaggerated small differences in sensitivity. The options describing exclusive zones repeat the common misconception.

**A735.** D. declines, partly because of fewer taste buds and slower replacement of receptor cells
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-18 -->

Explanation: Taste sensitivity generally declines with age, partly because the number of taste buds falls and receptor cell renewal slows. Taste receptors do regenerate, so the claim that they never do is false.

**A736.** A. glutamate, as found in monosodium glutamate (MSG)
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-19 -->

Explanation: Umami is triggered mainly by glutamate and similar amino acids, which are found in MSG, aged cheese and tomatoes. Sucrose is linked to sweet, citric acid to sour, and caffeine is typically perceived as bitter.

**A737.** B. protective response that helps detect potentially toxic substances
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-20 -->

Explanation: Bitterness acts as a warning, so sensitivity to it helped people avoid many toxic plant compounds. Ripeness is signalled more by sweetness, and the other options describe functions unrelated to bitter taste.

**A738.** D. temperature of a hot soup
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-21 -->

Explanation: Temperature is detected by thermoreceptors in the mouth and skin, which are part of the somatosensory system, and it adds to the overall flavour experience. Sweet, salty and sour are basic tastes detected by taste receptor cells.

**A739.** C. sensory interaction, where vision influences taste perception
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-22 -->

Explanation: Sensory interaction occurs when one sense, here vision, changes how another sense is perceived. Adaptation, aversion and supertasting do not explain a change in perceived sweetness caused by colour.

**A740.** A. Sensation is the detection of chemicals by receptors, while perception is the brain's interpretation of that information
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-23 -->

Explanation: Sensation is the detection of a stimulus by receptors, and perception is the interpretation of that sensory input in the brain. The options that reverse the locations or treat the two terms as identical confuse the distinction.

**A741.** C. about 2000 to 8000
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-24 -->

Explanation: Estimates for adults are roughly 2000 to 8000 taste buds, spread across the tongue, palate and throat. The larger figures overstate the count, and 50 is far too few.

**A742.** A. specialised epithelial cells that transduce chemical stimuli and synapse with sensory nerve fibres
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-25 -->

Explanation: Taste receptor cells are specialised epithelial cells in taste buds that convert chemical stimuli into electrical signals and pass them to sensory nerve fibres. The options calling them neurons, muscle cells or glial cells confuse them with other cell types.

**A743.** Soft palate, pharynx or epiglottis (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-26 -->

Explanation:

**A744.** Mushroom-shaped papillae found mainly on the front and sides of the tongue (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-27 -->

Explanation:

**A745.** The thalamus receives taste information from the brainstem (1), and relays it onward to the primary gustatory cortex in the cerebral cortex (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-28 -->

Explanation:

**A746.** Both senses respond to chemical molecules from food or the environment (1); their receptors are activated when molecules bind to them, producing neural signals (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-29 -->

Explanation:

**A747.** Sourness signals acidity, which can indicate unripe or spoiled food (1); avoiding such food reduces the risk of ingesting harmful bacteria or toxins (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-30 -->

Explanation:

**A748.** Taste receptors are in taste buds on the tongue, soft palate, pharynx and epiglottis (1); olfactory receptors are in the olfactory epithelium at the roof of the nasal cavity (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-31 -->

Explanation:

**A749.** Less sodium is available to stimulate the salt-sensitive receptor cells (1); fewer neural impulses are generated, so the signal reaching the gustatory cortex is weaker (1); flavour also depends on smell, texture or aroma, and reduced aroma or crunch can increase the perceived blandness (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-32 -->

Explanation:

**A750.** A chemical from food binds to or enters a receptor on the taste receptor cell (1); this changes the cell's electrical activity, producing a receptor potential (1); the cell releases a neurotransmitter onto a sensory neuron, which generates neural impulses sent to the brain (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-gp-33 -->

Explanation:

**A751.** A. Flavour depends heavily on smell (olfaction) as well as taste
<!-- id: vce-psych12-gu-c1 -->

Explanation: Taste receptors detect only basic tastes; aroma molecules reaching the nose create most of the flavour.

#### Distortions of perception and illusions

**A752.** B. arrowheads or fins at their ends pointing inwards or outwards
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-1 -->

Explanation: The fins at the line ends create different depth/size cues, so the line with outward-pointing fins looks longer than the line with inward-pointing fins.

**A753.** D. the viewer assumes the room is rectangular when it is actually distorted
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-2 -->

Explanation: The room is trapezoidal, but viewed from one point it appears rectangular. The viewer wrongly uses size-distance cues, so the person in the further corner appears larger.

**A754.** B. Ponzo illusion
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-3 -->

Explanation: The Ponzo illusion uses converging lines to create a linear perspective cue; the object that appears further away is perceived as larger.

**A755.** B. is a misperception of a real stimulus
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-4 -->

Explanation: In an illusion a real external stimulus is present but misinterpreted. Hallucinations involve perceptions without a stimulus.

**A756.** D. Perception is a constructive process in which the brain applies assumptions and cues to interpret sensory input
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-5 -->

Explanation: Illusions show that perception depends on interpretation of sensory information, often using depth cues and learned assumptions, and can be misled.

**A757.** The moon’s retinal image is the same size in both positions (1); near the horizon, objects such as trees and buildings provide depth cues (e.g. relative size/texture gradient) making the moon seem further away (1); the brain therefore perceives it as larger, whereas in the empty sky there are no cues, so it appears smaller (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-6 -->

Explanation:

**A758.** Two lines of equal length appear different in length (1); the line with outward-pointing fins (like an arrowhead pointing inwards) looks shorter than the line with inward-pointing fins (1); explanation: the fins are interpreted as depth cues, like the corners of a room, making one line appear further away and hence longer (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-7 -->

Explanation:

**A759.** A perception in which the observer’s experience differs from the physical properties of the stimulus (1); example: Ponzo illusion, Ames room, moon illusion, Rubin’s vase (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-8 -->

Explanation:

**A760.** A. a background pattern can distort the perceived straightness and orientation of nearby lines
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-9 -->

Explanation: The radiating lines alter the perceived orientation of the parallel lines, so the straight lines appear to bow. The distractors wrongly treat a perceptual distortion as a physical or focusing problem.

**A761.** B. each corner looks plausible on its own, but the depth cues across the whole figure cannot be combined into one consistent object
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-10 -->

Explanation: Each part is read as a plausible 3D corner, but when the parts are joined the depth information contradicts itself. Printing errors and eye convergence are not the cause, since the figure is impossible in its design.

**A762.** C. an ambiguous figure, which has more than one stable interpretation
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-11 -->

Explanation: The drawing supports two equally consistent 3D readings, so perception alternates between them. Size constancy and linear perspective concern size and depth cues, and a hallucination involves no stimulus.

**A763.** D. simultaneous brightness contrast, in which the surrounding area changes the perceived lightness of a region
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-12 -->

Explanation: The square reflects the same amount of light in both cases, so the surrounding brightness is what changes its perceived lightness. Motion aftereffects and the Müller-Lyer illusion involve movement and line length, not brightness.

**A764.** A. short diagonal lines crossing the long lines distort the perceived angle of the long lines
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-13 -->

Explanation: The short oblique lines interact with the long lines and change how their angle is judged, so parallel lines appear non-parallel. The lines are physically parallel, so curvature and print thickness are not the cause.

**A765.** B. light and shadow, which the brain reads as showing the direction of light and the shape of a surface
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-14 -->

Explanation: The brain assumes light comes from above, so shading at the bottom signals a bulge and shading at the top signals a hollow. The other cues depend on converging lines, overlap and haze rather than shading.

**A766.** C. a tendency to overestimate vertical length relative to horizontal length
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-15 -->

Explanation: Vertical extents tend to be judged as longer than equal horizontal ones, which produces the illusion. Line thickness and hiding do not explain why identical lines differ in apparent length.

**A767.** B. visual information from the lips can change the sound that is perceived, so speech perception combines the senses
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-16 -->

Explanation: Speech perception integrates auditory and visual input, so the brain blends them into a third sound. The other options contradict this integration or make claims the effect does not show.

**A768.** C. the brain expecting a larger object to be heavier, so the larger box feels lighter than expected
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-17 -->

Explanation: Prior expectation about how heavy objects should be shapes the weight judgment, so the mismatch makes the smaller box feel heavier. Density, lifting speed and fatigue do not explain why equal masses feel different.

**A769.** D. the lack of reference points and small involuntary eye movements make a stationary light appear to move
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-18 -->

Explanation: Without fixed reference points the visual system cannot judge the light’s position, so small eye movements are read as movement of the light. The light does not move and no stimulus is missing, so this is not a hallucination.

**A770.** A. apparent motion, in which a sequence of stationary stimuli is perceived as movement
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-19 -->

Explanation: The brain links the successive flashes into one moving object, even though nothing moves between them, and this principle underlies film and television. A motion aftereffect needs prior adaptation to movement, and the other options describe a physical or depth effect.

**A771.** B. the Shepard tone, a paradox created by overlapping octaves with changing loudness
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-20 -->

Explanation: The tones are built from stacked octaves whose loudness is shaped so the highest and lowest are quiet, which makes the rise seem endless. The McGurk effect involves vision and hearing, the phi phenomenon is visual motion, and the cocktail party effect concerns attention.

**A772.** C. the rectangle’s vertical edges distorting the perceived angle of the diagonal, so the two parts look misaligned
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-21 -->

Explanation: The vertical edges change how the direction of the diagonal is judged where it meets them, so the two collinear parts look offset. The line is continuous, and eye convergence and background brightness are not the cause.

**A773.** After prolonged viewing of downward motion, a stationary scene such as the rocks beside a waterfall appears to move upward [1]; motion-detecting neurons tuned to downward movement become fatigued, so neurons responding to upward movement dominate when the still scene is viewed [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-22 -->

Explanation:

**A774.** A hollow mask viewed from the inside is perceived as a normal convex face [1]; the viewer’s stored knowledge that faces are convex is applied to the sensory input [1]; this prior expectation (top-down processing) overrides depth cues such as shading and perspective that indicate a concave surface [1]; the percept is therefore constructed from sensory data and experience, not a direct copy of the stimulus [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-23 -->

Explanation:

**A775.** Three black circles with wedges removed are arranged so that a triangle with clear edges appears to lie over them, although no triangle outline is drawn [1]; the brain completes the missing contours from the arrangement of the shapes, which is a form of perceptual organisation (closure) [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-24 -->

Explanation:

**A776.** Two tabletops that are identical in shape and size are drawn in perspective and appear to differ [1]; the brain interprets the drawing as a three-dimensional scene and applies perspective and size-distance scaling to the tabletops [1]; the apparent depth makes one tabletop look longer and narrower than the other [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-25 -->

Explanation:

**A777.** People from environments without many right-angled, rectangular buildings (non-carpentered environments) were less susceptible to the Müller-Lyer illusion than people from carpentered urban environments [1]; this suggests that experience with the surroundings shapes the depth cues and assumptions used to perceive illusions [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-26 -->

Explanation:

**A778.** Figure-ground organisation is the perceptual separation of a scene into an object (the figure) and its background, which can reverse in images such as Rubin’s vase, where either the vase or two faces can be seen as the figure [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-27 -->

Explanation:

**A779.** Two identical central circles look different in size when one is surrounded by larger circles and the other by smaller circles [1]; the central circle surrounded by larger circles looks smaller, and the one surrounded by smaller circles looks larger [1]; this is size contrast, in which perceived size is judged relative to the size of nearby objects [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-28 -->

Explanation:

**A780.** Perceptual set is a readiness to perceive a stimulus in a particular way, based on expectations, experience or context [1]; the same ambiguous image is interpreted according to what each viewer expects, so each may see a different object [1]; for example, a viewer told the image shows an animal may see an animal, while a viewer told it shows a plant may see a plant [1]; the resulting perception is shaped by top-down processing rather than determined by the stimulus alone [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-29 -->

Explanation:

**A781.** C. The illusion comes from automatic processes, such as reading the arrowheads as depth cues, that knowledge cannot override
<!-- id: vce-psych12-di-c1 -->

Explanation: One explanation (the carpentered world hypothesis) links it to experience of corners in buildings, which also explains cultural differences.

### Research methods

#### Scientific methodologies and variables

**A782.** D. independent variable
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-1 -->

Explanation: The independent variable is the variable manipulated by the researcher; reaction time is the dependent variable that is measured.

**A783.** B. Participants who sleep for 8 hours will have faster reaction times than participants who sleep for 4 hours
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-2 -->

Explanation: A hypothesis is a testable, predictive statement that specifies the relationship between the IV and DV and the population. Options A and C are vague/question, and D is an aim.

**A784.** D. correlational study
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-3 -->

Explanation: Correlational studies examine the relationship between variables as they naturally occur, without manipulation. No IV is manipulated, so this is not an experiment.

**A785.** B. confounding variable
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-4 -->

Explanation: A confounding variable systematically varies with the IV, making it impossible to tell whether the IV or the confound produced the change in the DV.

**A786.** D. classification study
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-5 -->

Explanation: A classification study uses a naturally occurring characteristic (here, handedness) to categorise participants; the IV is not manipulated.

**A787.** IV: whether participants listen to classical music (or the type of music/no music) while studying (1); DV: number of words recalled correctly (1); extraneous variable e.g. length of word list, time of day, prior knowledge/age, noise in room, study time (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-6 -->

Explanation:

**A788.** A case study is an in-depth investigation of a single individual, group or event (1); it typically collects detailed qualitative data without manipulating variables (1); a controlled experiment manipulates an IV and measures its effect on a DV while controlling other variables, so it can establish cause and effect (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-7 -->

Explanation:

**A789.** An aim states the purpose of the investigation (what is being investigated), e.g. to investigate the effect of caffeine on reaction time (1); a hypothesis is a testable prediction of the outcome, stating the relationship between the IV and DV, e.g. participants given caffeine will have faster reaction times than those given none (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-8 -->

Explanation:

**A790.** B. a placebo that looks identical to the tablet
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-9 -->

Explanation: A control group receives a placebo so that any effect of simply taking a tablet can be separated from the effect of the active drug. Giving a different dose or timing is an experimental condition that tests the IV, not a baseline for comparison.

**A791.** C. make the groups roughly equivalent at the start so differences can be attributed to the IV
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-10 -->

Explanation: Random allocation spreads individual differences across groups by chance, so any differences after the manipulation are more likely due to the IV. Making a sample representative is the purpose of random sampling, which is a separate process.

**A792.** D. It selects participants so each member of the population has an equal chance of being chosen
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-11 -->

Explanation: Random sampling selects participants from the population in an unbiased way, which supports generalisation of the findings. Assigning participants to conditions is random allocation, a different process, and random sampling alone does not control variables or establish cause and effect.

**A793.** A. naturalistic observation
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-12 -->

Explanation: Naturalistic observation records behaviour in its normal setting without manipulating variables. A laboratory experiment would involve changing an IV in a controlled setting, which the researcher does not do here.

**A794.** B. reliable
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-13 -->

Explanation: Reliability refers to the consistency of a measure across repeated use under the same conditions, which is what the near-identical scores show. Validity concerns whether the test measures what it claims, which consistent scores alone do not establish.

**A795.** C. validity
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-14 -->

Explanation: Validity is the extent to which a measure assesses what it is intended to measure, and this test measures something else. Reliability concerns consistency of results, which a test can have even while measuring the wrong thing.

**A796.** A. A third variable, such as a busy lifestyle, may be responsible for both
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-15 -->

Explanation: A correlation shows that two variables are related but not that one causes the other, because a third variable could influence both. Correlational studies always involve two or more variables, so the problem is the inference drawn rather than the number of variables measured.

**A797.** B. demand characteristics
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-16 -->

Explanation: Demand characteristics are cues in a study that lead participants to change their behaviour to fit the expected outcome. Random error is unpredictable variation in measurements, not a participant response to the purpose of the study.

**A798.** C. a controlled experiment
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-17 -->

Explanation: A controlled experiment manipulates the IV (the program) and controls other variables, which allows cause and effect to be inferred. A correlational study could show that program participation and results are related, but could not establish that the program caused the change.

**A799.** D. ecological validity
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-18 -->

Explanation: Ecological validity is the extent to which findings apply to real-life settings and behaviour, and artificial tasks reduce it. Internal validity concerns whether the IV alone caused the change in the DV, not whether the tasks resemble real life.

**A800.** A. experimental group
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-19 -->

Explanation: The experimental group is exposed to the level of the IV being tested, while the control group does not receive it. The other options are not standard terms for the group that receives the IV.

**A801.** B. controlled variables
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-20 -->

Explanation: Controlled variables are held constant across all conditions so they cannot affect the DV. Confounding variables are uncontrolled variables that vary along with the IV, which is the problem these controls prevent.

**A802.** C. placebo effect
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-21 -->

Explanation: The placebo effect is a real change in symptoms or behaviour caused by participants' expectations rather than an active treatment. Random error is unpredictable measurement variation, so it does not explain a consistent improvement produced by belief.

**A803.** D. single-blind
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-22 -->

Explanation: In a single-blind study only the participants are unaware of their condition. In a double-blind study the researchers delivering the treatment are also unaware, so that option describes a stronger design than the one given.

**A804.** A. observation, hypothesis, testing, conclusion
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-23 -->

Explanation: Scientists observe a phenomenon, form a testable hypothesis, test it by collecting data, and then conclude whether the hypothesis is supported. A hypothesis must come before testing, so the other sequences do not describe the process.

**A805.** B. it helps establish whether findings are reliable
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-24 -->

Explanation: Replication repeats a study to check whether its findings can be obtained again, which supports the reliability of the results. A single replication cannot guarantee that the original result was correct, and replication does not remove the need for a hypothesis or operational definitions.

**A806.** C. repeated measures design
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-25 -->

Explanation: A repeated measures design tests the same participants in every condition, so each person serves as their own control. An independent groups design uses different participants in each condition, which is not the case in this study.

**A807.** The consistency of a measure, so that it produces similar results when the same test is repeated under the same conditions [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-26 -->

Explanation:

**A808.** Social desirability bias: students may answer in ways that make them look favourable rather than honestly [1]; this means the questionnaire measures self-presentation rather than true anxiety, reducing validity [1]; it could be reduced by ensuring anonymity, reassuring students that responses are confidential, or supporting results with another measure such as a physiological indicator [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-27 -->

Explanation:

**A809.** Observation of a problem, such as students' low maths scores, leading to a question about whether the app helps [1]; a testable hypothesis, e.g. students who use the app will score higher than students who do not [1]; a controlled experiment with app use as the IV and test score as the DV, using a control group and collecting data [1]; analysis of the results to decide whether the hypothesis is supported, followed by a conclusion that can be reported and replicated [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-28 -->

Explanation:

**A810.** The sample is not representative of the target population, because university students differ from adolescents in general in age, independence and lifestyle [1]; so the findings may not apply to adolescents more broadly [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-29 -->

Explanation:

**A811.** Keep the noise level constant across all participants and sessions, e.g. by testing everyone in a sound-proofed room [1]; this stops noise varying systematically with the IV, so differences in recall can be attributed to the IV [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-30 -->

Explanation:

**A812.** Advantage: fewer participants are needed, and individual differences are controlled because each person serves as their own control [1]; disadvantage: order effects, such as practice or fatigue, may influence performance in the second condition [1]; reduce by counterbalancing the order in which participants complete the conditions [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-31 -->

Explanation:

**A813.** Teacher differences: volunteer teachers may be more motivated or skilled, so gains could reflect the teacher rather than the program [1]; school differences: the schools may differ in resources or student background, so outcome differences could reflect the school [1]; because these variables are not controlled, the effect of the program cannot be isolated [1]; reduce by randomly allocating students or classes from the same school to the program and control groups, which balances these variables across groups [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-32 -->

Explanation:

**A814.** To test the procedures, materials and measures so that problems can be identified and fixed before the main study [1]; to check whether the manipulation of the IV works and the study is feasible, saving time and resources [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-33 -->

Explanation:

**A815.** Method: case study [1]; strength: provides rich, detailed in-depth data about an individual, including rare cases [1]; limitation: findings cannot be generalised to other people, and cause and effect cannot be established because no variables are manipulated [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-34 -->

Explanation:

**A816.** The extent to which a measure or study measures what it is intended to measure [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-35 -->

Explanation:

**A817.** D. It is correlational: nothing was manipulated, and other variables or reverse causation could explain the link
<!-- id: vce-psych12-sm-c1 -->

Explanation: Only a controlled experiment, where the IV is manipulated and other variables controlled, supports cause-and-effect conclusions.

#### Sampling and experimental designs

**A818.** C. random sampling
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-1 -->

Explanation: In random sampling every member of the population has an equal chance of being selected. Stratified sampling would first divide the population into subgroups.

**A819.** D. stratified sampling
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-2 -->

Explanation: Stratified sampling ensures key subgroups (strata) are represented in the same proportions as in the population.

**A820.** C. random allocation
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-3 -->

Explanation: Random allocation gives each participant in the sample an equal chance of being placed in any condition, helping control participant variables.

**A821.** B. Within-subjects (repeated measures)
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-4 -->

Explanation: In a within-subjects design all participants are exposed to every condition, controlling for individual participant differences but risking order effects.

**A822.** D. order effects such as practice or fatigue
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-5 -->

Explanation: Because each participant experiences all conditions, performance may be affected by the order, e.g. practice or fatigue. Counterbalancing can reduce this.

**A823.** Convenience sampling (1); the sample may not be representative of the population, limiting generalisability, or may be biased (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-6 -->

Explanation:

**A824.** Random sampling is the selection of participants from the population so that each member has an equal chance of being chosen (1); random allocation is the assignment of the sample’s participants to experimental or control groups by chance (1); sampling aims to produce a representative sample, whereas allocation aims to distribute participant characteristics evenly across groups to control participant variables (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-7 -->

Explanation:

**A825.** Two separate groups are formed, one taught with the new method and one with the usual method, and their test scores are compared (1, with random allocation as an acceptable addition); each participant experiences only one condition (1); strength: no order effects/practice effects, since participants do not repeat the task (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-8 -->

Explanation:

**A826.** B. so that findings can be generalised to the wider population
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-9 -->

Explanation: A representative sample reflects the characteristics of the population, allowing findings to be generalised beyond the sample. Random allocation and within-subjects designs concern experimental control rather than generalisability.

**A827.** C. counterbalancing
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-10 -->

Explanation: Counterbalancing spreads the order of conditions across participants so that order effects are distributed evenly rather than removed. Random allocation assigns participants to groups, so it does not control the order in which one person completes tasks.

**A828.** B. mixed design
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-11 -->

Explanation: A mixed design includes a between-subjects factor (teaching method, with each class in one condition) and a within-subjects factor (testing before and after). A purely within-subjects design would have every student experience both teaching methods.

**A829.** A. to control for participants' expectations of receiving a treatment
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-12 -->

Explanation: A placebo group receives an inactive treatment, so any effect of expecting treatment can be separated from the effect of the treatment itself. Representativeness depends on how the sample is selected, not on the groups' experimental conditions.

**A830.** D. both the participants and the people who interact with them during testing
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-13 -->

Explanation: Double-blind procedures stop both participant and experimenter expectations from influencing results. In a single-blind design, only the participants are unaware of their group.

**A831.** C. a larger sample reduces sampling error, so it is more likely to reflect the population
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-14 -->

Explanation: Larger samples tend to give estimates closer to the true population values, reducing sampling error. The other options describe features of experimental design rather than of sample size.

**A832.** A. all Year 12 students in Victoria
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-15 -->

Explanation: The population is the whole group the researcher wants to draw conclusions about, while the sample is the 400 students actually studied. The schools are a means of selecting participants, not the population.

**A833.** B. requires fewer participants
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-16 -->

Explanation: Each participant contributes data to every condition, so fewer people are needed than in a between-subjects design. Within-subjects designs still face order effects, so they do not eliminate them.

**A834.** C. it may over-represent people who already exercise regularly
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-17 -->

Explanation: Only people who use the app are invited, so the sample is biased towards active individuals and may not represent the wider population. A missing control group is a concern for experiments rather than surveys.

**A835.** A. 20
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-18 -->

Explanation: Males make up 400 ÷ 1 200 = 1/3 of the population, so 1/3 × 60 = 20 males. Splitting the sample equally (30) ignores the different proportions in each subgroup.

**A836.** B. the prior knowledge or motivation of each participant
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-19 -->

Explanation: Participant variables are characteristics of individuals, such as prior knowledge or motivation, and random allocation tends to distribute them evenly across groups. Room temperature, instructions and time of testing are situational factors that should be standardised across conditions.

**A837.** Systematic error in the way participants are selected, so that the sample is not representative of the population [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-20 -->

Explanation:

**A838.** In a within-subjects design the same participants experience every condition of the independent variable [1]; in a between-subjects design different participants are allocated to each condition [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-21 -->

Explanation:

**A839.** To control order effects such as practice or fatigue [1], by varying the order of conditions across participants so that order effects are spread evenly between conditions [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-22 -->

Explanation:

**A840.** Between-subjects: participants are randomly allocated to a walking group or a sitting group, and each person completes the concentration task in one condition only [1]; within-subjects: the same participants complete the task after walking and after sitting [1]; advantage of within-subjects: fewer participants are needed because each person contributes data to both conditions [1]; disadvantage of between-subjects: individual differences may affect the groups unevenly, since each person completes only one condition [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-sed-23 -->

Explanation:

**A841.** B. Random sampling selects participants from a population; random allocation assigns them to conditions
<!-- id: vce-psych12-se-c1 -->

Explanation: Random sampling helps representativeness (generalisation); random allocation spreads participant variables evenly between groups.

#### Data analysis and conclusions

**A842.** C. The number of errors made on a task
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-1 -->

Explanation: Quantitative data are numerical measurements. The others are qualitative data in the form of descriptions and words.

**A843.** A. 6
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-2 -->

Explanation: The median is the middle value of the ordered scores: with 7 scores, the 4th value is 6. The mean is 44/7 ≈ 6.3 and the mode is 5.

**A844.** D. Mean
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-3 -->

Explanation: The mean uses every value in its calculation, so an outlier pulls it up or down. The median and mode are resistant to outliers.

**A845.** A. The use of a convenience sample from one school
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-4 -->

Explanation: A small, non-random sample from a single setting is not representative, so conclusions can only be applied to the sample and not confidently to the wider population.

**A846.** D. state whether the results support the hypothesis, relate to the aim, and acknowledge limitations
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-5 -->

Explanation: A good conclusion is based on the data, indicates whether the hypothesis was supported, considers the extent to which results can be generalised, and acknowledges limitations.

**A847.** Mean = (210 + 220 + 230 + 240 + 250 + 250 + 260) ÷ 7 = 1660 ÷ 7 ≈ 237.1 ms (1); mode = 250 ms (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-6 -->

Explanation:

**A848.** Qualitative data are descriptive, non-numerical information about qualities or experiences (1); quantitative data are numerical measurements that can be statistically analysed (1); examples: interview responses or observation notes (qualitative); scores on a questionnaire, time taken or number correct (quantitative) (1 for both examples).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-7 -->

Explanation:

**A849.** The conclusion may not be justified (1); without random allocation, participant variables (e.g. age, ability, motivation) may differ between groups and act as confounding variables (1); so the difference may not be due only to sleep deprivation and a causal conclusion cannot be confidently drawn (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-8 -->

Explanation:

**A850.** D. Mode
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-9 -->

Explanation: The mode is the most frequently occurring category, so it can be found for categorical data. The mean and median need numerical values, and the range is a measure of spread rather than central tendency.

**A851.** A. Range
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-10 -->

Explanation: The range is the difference between the highest and lowest scores, so it describes how spread out the scores are. The mean, median and mode all describe a typical or central value.

**A852.** B. 19
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-11 -->

Explanation: With an even number of scores, the median is the average of the two middle values: (18 + 20) ÷ 2 = 19. Choosing 18 or 20 uses only one of the two middle scores.

**A853.** C. 9
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-12 -->

Explanation: The mean is the total divided by the number of scores: (6 + 8 + 10 + 12) ÷ 4 = 36 ÷ 4 = 9. The total of 36 has not been divided by the number of scores.

**A854.** B. Bar graph
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-13 -->

Explanation: The lunch options are categories, so each should be a separate bar, which a bar graph shows. A histogram is for continuous numerical data grouped into intervals, and a line graph implies a trend across an ordered variable.

**A855.** C. Scatterplot
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-14 -->

Explanation: A scatterplot displays paired values of two numerical variables, one point per participant, so any relationship can be seen. A pie chart shows parts of a whole, and a bar graph compares categories rather than paired numerical scores.

**A856.** A. 75%
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-15 -->

Explanation: 30 ÷ 40 = 0.75, so 75% completed the task. Using 30 directly gives the count rather than the percentage, and 25% is the percentage who did not complete it.

**A857.** B. 3.3
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-16 -->

Explanation: 14.5 − 11.2 = 3.3. Adding the two means gives 25.7, and 0.33 results from a misplaced decimal point.

**A858.** D. There are two modes, 3 and 5
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-17 -->

Explanation: The values 3 and 5 each occur twice and no other value occurs more than once, so the data have two modes. The value 4.3 is the mean (26 ÷ 6 ≈ 4.3), not the mode.

**A859.** B. The hypothesis was not supported, as the sample showed no difference between the group means
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-18 -->

Explanation: The predicted difference did not occur in this sample, so the hypothesis is not supported by these data. Equal means cannot show a small effect, and a single sample cannot prove anything about the whole population.

**A860.** D. Social media use is associated with lower sleep quality, but this design cannot establish that one causes the other
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-19 -->

Explanation: The study shows an association between the two variables, and without manipulating either one, causation cannot be concluded. Claiming that either variable causes the other goes beyond the data, and claiming they are unrelated contradicts the findings.

**A861.** C. Line graph
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-20 -->

Explanation: Sessions are ordered over time, and a line graph joins the averages to show the trend. A pie chart shows parts of a whole, a histogram shows the spread of individual scores, and a table does not display the trend visually.

**A862.** D. 15%
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-21 -->

Explanation: The increase is 46 − 40 = 6, and 6 ÷ 40 = 0.15, so the mean increased by 15%. Dividing by the new mean (6 ÷ 46 ≈ 13%) uses the wrong starting value.

**A863.** A. 13
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-22 -->

Explanation: The range is the highest score minus the lowest score: 22 − 9 = 13. Adding the highest and lowest scores gives 31, and 22 is the highest score on its own.

**A864.** C. Thematic analysis of qualitative data
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-23 -->

Explanation: Identifying and grouping recurring themes in non-numerical responses is thematic analysis, which is used for qualitative data. The other options are techniques for numerical data.

**A865.** D. A small sample can produce unstable results that may not replicate, so the finding should be interpreted cautiously
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-24 -->

Explanation: Small samples are more affected by chance and individual differences, so a result may not appear in a larger or different sample. A result from a small sample is less reliable, not more, and sample size strongly affects results.

**A866.** A. 0.40 s
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-25 -->

Explanation: The total is 2.00 s, and 2.00 ÷ 5 = 0.40 s. The total of 2.00 s has not been divided by the number of scores, and 0.45 s is the highest value rather than the mean.

**A867.** The extent to which the findings of a study, based on its sample, can be applied to the wider population or other settings [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-26 -->

Explanation:

**A868.** 45 ÷ 60 × 100 = 75% [1]; the number of participants who improved is numerical, so it is quantitative data [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-27 -->

Explanation:

**A869.** Median = 9, the middle value of the ordered scores [1]; range = 12 − 4 = 8 [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-28 -->

Explanation:

**A870.** Limitations, such as a small or non-random sample or reliance on self-report, affect how confidently the results can be interpreted [1]; stating them shows the extent to which the findings can be generalised [1]; this avoids overstating the findings or implying causation that the design cannot support [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-29 -->

Explanation:

**A871.** The sample is small and specific (15 first-year university students), so the finding cannot be generalised to all adults [1]; with no control group, practice effects or other factors could explain the improvement, so caffeine cannot be confirmed as the cause [1]; a better conclusion would state that reaction times improved after caffeine in this sample of 15 students, and that further research is needed [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-30 -->

Explanation:

**A872.** Difference = 18.4 − 15.1 = 3.3 [1]; the experimental group had the higher mean score [1]; suggested conclusion: the intervention was associated with higher scores in this sample of 40 students [1]; the result may not generalise beyond this one school, a limitation that should be acknowledged [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-31 -->

Explanation:

**A873.** ‘Support’ means the data are consistent with the hypothesis [1]; ‘prove’ overstates certainty, as a single study with a limited sample cannot establish a claim beyond doubt [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-32 -->

Explanation:

**A874.** Mean = 1860 ÷ 6 = 310 ms [1]; median = (305 + 310) ÷ 2 = 307.5 ms [1]; range = 330 − 295 = 35 ms [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-33 -->

Explanation:

**A875.** Excluding participants because of their results, rather than a pre-stated criterion, biases the data and reduces validity [1]; the conclusion ignores the three excluded participants, so the findings do not reflect the whole group tested [1]; the claim that the intervention ‘works for everyone’ overgeneralises beyond the sample studied [1]; a justified conclusion would report the exclusions and limit its claims to the participants tested [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-dac-34 -->

Explanation:

**A876.** B. Only the population the sample came from, and only if the sample represents it
<!-- id: vce-psych12-da-c1 -->

Explanation: A small, convenience sample limits generalisation. Conclusions should match the scope of the sample.

#### Ethics in psychological research

**A877.** C. are told about the nature of the study, procedures and risks and agree to take part voluntarily
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-1 -->

Explanation: Informed consent requires that participants understand what the study involves, including risks, before agreeing to take part. Parental consent is needed for minors.

**A878.** A. confidentiality
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-2 -->

Explanation: Confidentiality means that personal information is not disclosed or made accessible to others without permission.

**A879.** B. a debriefing session
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-3 -->

Explanation: Deception may only be used when justified and no alternative exists; it must be followed by a debrief explaining the true purpose and addressing any distress.

**A880.** B. Participants choose to take part without pressure or coercion and may withdraw at any time without penalty
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-4 -->

Explanation: Participation must be free from coercion, and participants have the right to withdraw at any time without penalty.

**A881.** C. replacement, reduction and refinement
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-5 -->

Explanation: Researchers should replace animals with alternatives where possible, reduce the number used, and refine procedures to minimise harm and distress.

**A882.** Informed consent must be obtained from the students and from their parents/guardians as they are under 18 (1); confidentiality and privacy of responses must be maintained, e.g. de-identified data stored securely (1); other acceptable: right to withdraw, voluntary participation, minimising distress with support available (1 for explaining how each is addressed).
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-6 -->

Explanation:

**A883.** Debriefing occurs at the end of the study to ensure participants leave in the same state in which they entered (1); it explains the true aim and any deception used and answers questions (1); it checks for distress/offers support and gives participants the opportunity to withdraw their data (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-7 -->

Explanation:

**A884.** Deception may be necessary because knowing the true aim could alter participants’ behaviour and invalidate the results (1); it is acceptable only if the potential benefits outweigh the risks, there is no suitable alternative, it causes no more than minimal harm, and participants are fully debriefed (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-8 -->

Explanation:

**A885.** B. no one, including the researcher, can link any response to the person who gave it
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-9 -->

Explanation: Anonymity means identity cannot be linked to responses at any point, even by the researcher. Keeping names but not publishing them is confidentiality, which is a different form of protection.

**A886.** D. the right to withdraw
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-10 -->

Explanation: Participants may withdraw at any time without penalty, and pressuring them to continue breaches this right. Confidentiality concerns protecting personal information, not whether a person may stop taking part.

**A887.** A. stopping the task and offering support if a participant becomes very distressed
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-11 -->

Explanation: A researcher has a duty of care to stop a procedure and provide support when a participant shows significant distress, even if they consented earlier. Increasing difficulty or hiding harm increases risk, and hiding harm also undermines informed consent.

**A888.** C. a Human Research Ethics Committee
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-12 -->

Explanation: Research involving humans should be independently reviewed by an ethics committee, such as a Human Research Ethics Committee, before data collection begins. A tutor’s approval is not independent review, and journal submission does not replace this process.

**A889.** D. presenting another researcher’s ideas as your own without citing them
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-13 -->

Explanation: Plagiarism, which means presenting someone else’s work or ideas as your own without acknowledgement, is research misconduct. The other options are honest and normal parts of the research process.

**A890.** D. a study should proceed only if its potential benefits outweigh the risks to participants
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-14 -->

Explanation: Researchers must minimise risks and ensure they are justified by the benefits to participants or society. A signed consent form does not remove the researcher’s duty to protect participants from harm.

**A891.** A. adults with cognitive impairments who may be unable to give fully informed consent
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-15 -->

Explanation: People with a reduced capacity to understand or consent, such as some adults with cognitive impairments, need extra protection, for example a substitute decision-maker. Responding to an advertisement, holding a degree or being paid does not make someone vulnerable.

**A892.** C. the purpose, procedures and risks of the study, and how to contact the researcher or ethics committee
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-16 -->

Explanation: A participant information statement must describe the study’s purpose, procedures, risks and benefits, and how to ask questions or raise concerns. Predicted results could bias participants’ responses, and sharing other participants’ names breaches confidentiality.

**A893.** B. students may feel pressured to take part because of the teacher’s authority, threatening voluntary participation
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-17 -->

Explanation: Voluntary participation requires that students feel free to decline without pressure, and the presence of a teacher and peers can make refusal difficult. The other options are methodological or practical concerns rather than ethical ones about consent.

**A894.** D. seek ethics approval for the changes and inform participants so they can decide whether to continue
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-18 -->

Explanation: Ethics approval covers specific procedures, so substantial changes need review, and participants must be told about changes that might affect their decision to take part. Destroying existing data is not required and does not replace seeking approval.

**A895.** A. provide support and a referral to appropriate services, recognising that confidentiality has limits when a person is at risk
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-19 -->

Explanation: A researcher’s duty of care to a participant at risk takes priority over normal confidentiality, so support and referral are needed. Continuing as normal or publishing the disclosure ignores the participant’s welfare and privacy.

**A896.** C. confidentiality and privacy
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-20 -->

Explanation: Publishing identifiable details without permission breaches the participant’s privacy and the promise of confidentiality. Validity, reliability and replication describe the quality and repeatability of research rather than ethical principles that protect participants.

**A897.** B. to identify problems that could harm or distress participants before the main study begins
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-21 -->

Explanation: A pilot study helps reveal risks and unclear procedures so they can be fixed before the main study exposes participants to them. It cannot guarantee results, remove ethics requirements or replace informed consent.

**A898.** D. remove it from the dataset where practicable and exclude it from analysis
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-22 -->

Explanation: Respecting the right to withdraw means removing a participant’s data where practicable and excluding it from analysis. Removing names does not undo the participant’s decision, so keeping or sharing the data is not acceptable.

**A899.** A. honesty and integrity in reporting results
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-23 -->

Explanation: Researchers must report results accurately, including harmful effects, so others can make informed decisions and avoid harm. The 3Rs apply only to animal research, and random allocation is a design feature rather than an ethical principle.

**A900.** Anonymity means that no one, including the researcher, can link a participant to their data [1]; confidentiality means the researcher knows who the participant is but does not disclose or publish identifying information [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-24 -->

Explanation:

**A901.** Informed consent requires participants to understand what the study involves, so the statement must be clear [1]; jargon may cause misunderstanding, meaning participants could agree without fully understanding the risks and procedures [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-25 -->

Explanation:

**A902.** Tell participants that they can skip any question and withdraw at any time without penalty [1]; provide a debriefing at the end of the study that checks participants’ wellbeing [1]; give participants a list of support services, such as a counselling line [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-26 -->

Explanation:

**A903.** Concern: the children cannot give informed consent and were not told they were being observed [1]; concern: their privacy is invaded because their behaviour is recorded without permission [1]; address the first by seeking ethics approval and consent from parents or guardians and the school, with the children’s assent where possible [1]; address the second by recording behaviour without identifying individuals and storing data securely [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-27 -->

Explanation:

**A904.** A large payment may pressure people, particularly those experiencing financial hardship, to take part even though they would otherwise decline [1]; their participation is then not free, and they may accept risks they would normally avoid [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-28 -->

Explanation:

**A905.** Replacement: use cell cultures or computer models where possible instead of live mice [1]; reduction: collect multiple measurements from each mouse where scientifically appropriate, so fewer mice are needed [1]; refinement: use humane anaesthetic and appropriate housing to reduce pain and distress [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-29 -->

Explanation:

**A906.** Reviewing research proposals to assess their risks, benefits and ethical acceptability [1]; approving a study, or requiring changes, before data collection begins [1]; monitoring approved research, including adverse events and any changes to procedures [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-30 -->

Explanation:

**A907.** Informed consent: participants must understand the study and their right to withdraw, and agree without pressure [1]; minimising harm: questions about mood may cause distress, so the researcher should monitor wellbeing and stop if needed [1]; confidentiality: sensitive health information must be stored securely and not identifiable in reports [1]; duty of care: participants at risk should be given information about support services and referral procedures [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-31 -->

Explanation:

**A908.** Other researchers, practitioners and the public rely on accurate reports to build knowledge and make decisions [1]; withholding unsupported results would distort the evidence base and waste participants’ contributions [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-32 -->

Explanation:

**A909.** Duty of care [1]
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-33 -->

Explanation:

**A910.** B. Debriefing, so participants learn the true purpose and any harm is addressed
<!-- id: vce-psych12-et-c1 -->

Explanation: Deception is allowed only if it is justified and participants are fully debriefed afterwards.

#### Validity, reliability and errors

**A911.** B. the study measures what it claims to measure and the conclusions drawn are justified by the data
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-1 -->

Explanation: Validity concerns whether the investigation actually investigates what it claims to and whether the conclusions are supported. Option A describes reliability/repeatability.

**A912.** B. an investigator effect
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-2 -->

Explanation: The investigator effect is the unintentional influence of the researcher’s expectations on participants’ behaviour or the measurement of results. It can be reduced by a double-blind procedure.

**A913.** C. placebo effect
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-3 -->

Explanation: The placebo effect is a change in participants’ responses due to their expectations rather than the IV. Placebos help control it.

**A914.** A. systematic error
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-4 -->

Explanation: A systematic error is a consistent, predictable difference from the true value in the same direction, affecting accuracy. Random errors vary unpredictably and affect precision.

**A915.** B. high reliability (repeatability)
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-5 -->

Explanation: Repeatability — consistent results when an investigation is repeated under the same conditions — is an indicator of reliability. It does not guarantee validity or the absence of systematic error.

**A916.** Internal validity is the extent to which the results are due to the manipulation of the IV and not other factors such as confounding variables (1); external validity is the extent to which the results can be generalised to other people, settings and times (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-6 -->

Explanation:

**A917.** Random errors are unpredictable variations in measurement that vary in size and direction and affect precision (1); systematic errors are consistent biases in the same direction that affect accuracy (1); random errors reduced by taking repeated measurements and averaging, or using a larger sample (1); systematic errors reduced by calibrating equipment, standardising procedures, or using a control group/blinding (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-7 -->

Explanation:

**A918.** Threat: participant expectations/demand characteristics or placebo effect, or investigator effect (1); in a double-blind procedure neither participants nor the researcher(s) collecting data know who is in the experimental or control group (1); this reduces the influence of expectations on responses and measurement, improving validity (1).
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-8 -->

Explanation:

**A919.** A. reliability
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-9 -->

Explanation: Consistent scores when the same measure is repeated under the same conditions show reliability. They do not show that the questionnaire measures anxiety, so validity has not been established.

**A920.** B. inter-rater reliability
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-10 -->

Explanation: Inter-rater reliability is the agreement between different observers recording the same behaviour. Agreement does not prove that sharing a toy is the behaviour the researcher intended to measure, so it says nothing about validity.

**A921.** C. reliable but not valid
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-11 -->

Explanation: The consistent scores show the test is reliable, but because it measures reading speed rather than intelligence it is not valid. Consistency alone does not guarantee that a measure is valid.

**A922.** A. demand characteristics
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-12 -->

Explanation: Demand characteristics are cues that lead participants to guess the aim of a study and change their behaviour. A double-blind procedure is a control designed to reduce this problem, not an example of it.

**A923.** B. random error affecting precision
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-13 -->

Explanation: Unpredictable variation in both directions is random error, which reduces precision. Systematic error would shift all the values the same way, so it would affect accuracy instead.

**A924.** C. Adding more questions that assess the same unit content
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-14 -->

Explanation: More items measuring the same content give a more stable estimate of knowledge, so scores are more consistent. Making the quiz easier or increasing the sample size does not make the measure itself more consistent.

**A925.** D. external validity
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-15 -->

Explanation: External validity concerns whether results can be generalised to other settings, people and times. A quiet laboratory may differ greatly from everyday study conditions, so the claim is questionable.

**A926.** A. The scales have a systematic error, so their measurements are inaccurate
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-16 -->

Explanation: A constant offset in one direction is systematic error, which affects accuracy. Consistent readings show precision rather than accuracy, and averaging repeated readings cannot remove a constant bias.

**A927.** B. A score that lies far from the rest of the data set
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-17 -->

Explanation: An outlier is an unusually extreme score that differs markedly from the rest of the data. A faulty instrument may cause an outlier, but that describes a possible cause rather than the definition.

**A928.** C. an order effect
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-18 -->

Explanation: An order effect occurs when the sequence of conditions changes performance, such as through practice or fatigue. Counterbalancing the order of the tasks across participants helps control it.

**A929.** D. Using an independent assessor who does not know which group each student is in to collect the ratings
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-19 -->

Explanation: An assessor who is unaware of group membership cannot unintentionally influence the ratings, so the researcher's expectations are removed from data collection. The other options would increase researcher or participant expectations.

**A930.** A. The group differences may reflect prior ability rather than the study method
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-20 -->

Explanation: Pre-existing differences in ability act as a confounding variable that could produce the result without the study method having any effect. The other options concern generalisation, marking or publication rather than whether the independent variable caused the change.

**A931.** C. A valid measure must also be reliable, but a reliable measure may still be invalid
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-21 -->

Explanation: A measure that accurately measures its construct must give consistent results, but consistency alone does not show that it measures the right thing. The other statements reverse the relationship or confuse the two terms.

**A932.** B. Compare the monitor's readings with those from a criterion (gold-standard) device on the same participants
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-22 -->

Explanation: Accuracy is checked by comparing measurements with a known or criterion standard. Repeating measures on one person assesses consistency (reliability), while participant opinion or sample size cannot establish accuracy.

**A933.** C. social desirability bias
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-23 -->

Explanation: Social desirability bias is the tendency to answer in ways that make the respondent look favourable to others. It reduces the validity of self-reported data.

**A934.** D. To identify and fix problems in the procedure before the full study
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-24 -->

Explanation: A pilot study tests the procedure, materials and timing so that problems can be corrected before the main study, which improves its reliability and validity. It does not prove a hypothesis or replace controls.

**A935.** A. Standardising procedures so that all participants experience the same conditions apart from the independent variable
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-25 -->

Explanation: Holding extraneous variables constant means they cannot vary with the independent variable, so they cannot offer an alternative explanation. Increasing trials mainly reduces random error, while vague instructions and disclosing the aim add error and bias.

**A936.** B. Participants changing their behaviour because they know they are being observed
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-26 -->

Explanation: Knowing they are being watched can make children act differently, so the observations may not reflect natural behaviour, and a one-way mirror reduces this. The other options describe researcher influence, observer agreement or timing error, which this procedure does not address directly.

**A937.** Reliability is the extent to which a test gives consistent results [1]; a reliable test produces similar scores when the same participants are tested again under the same conditions [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-27 -->

Explanation:

**A938.** A variable other than the independent variable that changes systematically with it and may affect the dependent variable, so it provides an alternative explanation for the results [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-28 -->

Explanation:

**A939.** External validity (generalisability) [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-29 -->

Explanation:

**A940.** A pilot study tests the procedures, instructions and equipment with a small group before the main study [1]; problems such as unclear instructions or inconsistent timing are identified and corrected [1]; this leads to standardised procedures that produce more consistent results across all participants [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-30 -->

Explanation:

**A941.** Standardised procedures mean all participants experience the same conditions, reducing variation caused by differences in instructions or handling [1]; this produces more consistent results that can be repeated [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-31 -->

Explanation:

**A942.** Mean reading = 150.6 ÷ 4 = 37.65 °C [1]; mean error = 37.65 − 37.0 = +0.65 °C, so the thermometer reads too high [1]; this consistent offset in one direction is systematic error, which affects accuracy [1]; the small spread of readings (37.5–37.8 °C) shows low random error, so the thermometer is precise [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-32 -->

Explanation:

**A943.** Confusing wording means participants may interpret questions differently, producing inconsistent answers [1]; this lowers the reliability of the questionnaire [1]; responses may reflect confusion about the wording rather than stress, which lowers validity [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-33 -->

Explanation:

**A944.** Accuracy is how close a measurement is to the true value [1]; precision is how close repeated measurements are to each other, showing consistency [1].
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-34 -->

Explanation:

**A945.** C. Reliable (consistent) but not accurate, because of a systematic error
<!-- id: vce-psych12-vr-c1 -->

Explanation: Consistency means reliability; the constant offset is a systematic error that repetition cannot remove.

### Challenge questions

#### Challenge set

**A946.** Pre-operational (about 2–7): thinking is egocentric and focused on appearance, so a child thinks a tall thin glass holds more juice. Concrete operational (about 7–11): children can conserve, understanding that quantity stays the same, and can think logically about concrete situations.
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-1 -->

Explanation: Give an age range, a key feature and an example for each stage.

**A947.** D. Withdrawal rights, because the experimenter used prods to make participants continue
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-2 -->

Explanation: Participants were told 'you have no other choice, you must go on', which pressured them not to withdraw. They were also deceived.

**A948.** A. Integrity versus despair
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-3 -->

Explanation: The final stage involves looking back on life with acceptance (integrity) or regret (despair).

**A949.** The conflict between the belief and the behaviour causes discomfort. Because the behaviour cannot be undone, the student may change the belief (for example, 'everyone cheats; it isn't a big deal') to reduce the dissonance.
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-4 -->

Explanation: Name the conflicting cognitions and the way the dissonance is reduced.

**A950.** A. Confirmation bias
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-5 -->

Explanation: Confirmation bias is seeking or remembering information that supports an existing belief.

**A951.** D. Closure
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-6 -->

Explanation: Closure is the tendency to fill gaps to perceive whole objects.

**A952.** A. Contact comfort was more important than food in forming attachment
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-7 -->

Explanation: Infants clung to the soft cloth surrogate, especially when frightened, even when the wire surrogate provided milk.

**A953.** Biological: the prefrontal cortex is still developing, while reward systems are highly active. Psychological: optimism bias ('it won't happen to me') and sensation-seeking. Social: peer pressure and wanting approval from friends in the car.
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-8 -->

Explanation: Give at least one specific factor from each domain and explain how it contributes.

**A954.** C. MRI
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-9 -->

Explanation: MRI produces high-resolution structural images. PET and fMRI show function; EEG records electrical activity.

**A955.** C. Counterbalancing the order of conditions
<!-- id: vce-psych12-challenge-10 -->

Explanation: Counterbalancing spreads practice and fatigue effects evenly across conditions.

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**A956.** A skateboarding accident and the adolescent brain
<!-- id: vce-psych12-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** Any two: planning, decision-making and problem-solving; controlling emotions and personality; voluntary movement (primary motor cortex); producing speech (Broca's area, usually in the left hemisphere). *(2 marks)*

1. First function, for example: higher-order thinking such as planning, problem-solving and decision-making. *(1 mark)*
2. Second function, for example: initiating voluntary movement (primary motor cortex) or producing speech (Broca's area). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming functions of other lobes, such as vision (occipital) or hearing (temporal).

Topic: Structure and function of the brain (vce-psych12-structure-and-function-of-the-brain)

**b.** It is brain damage that happened after birth, caused here by an external force (a blow to the head), rather than being present from birth or genetic. *(1 mark)*

1. An acquired brain injury is damage to the brain that occurs after birth; Jordan's was caused by a blow to the head. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying it is acquired because it is permanent. 'Acquired' refers to when and how it happened, not how long it lasts.

Topic: Acquired brain injury and neurological disorders (vce-psych12-acquired-brain-injury-and-neurological-disorders)

**c.** Undamaged neurons can grow new branches and form new connections (sprouting) and take over functions of damaged areas (rerouting). Adolescent brains are especially plastic, which helps recovery. *(2 marks)*

1. Neuroplasticity is the brain's ability to change its structure in response to experience or injury. *(1 mark)*
2. Undamaged neurons form new connections (sprouting and rerouting) so other areas can take over some functions of the damaged area. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying damaged neurons regrow. Recovery mostly comes from healthy neurons forming new connections.

Topic: Neuroplasticity and adolescent brain development (vce-psych12-neuroplasticity-and-adolescent-brain-development)

**d.** The prefrontal cortex, which controls planning, judgement and impulse control, is not fully developed until the mid-20s. The amygdala, involved in emotional responses, matures earlier, so emotions and rewards can outweigh rational judgement, leading to impulsive risk-taking. *(2 marks)*

1. The prefrontal cortex, which supports judgement and impulse control, is still developing in adolescence. *(1 mark)*
2. The amygdala matures earlier, so emotional and reward-driven responses are more influential than rational control, which encourages risk-taking. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the adolescent brain is simply smaller or 'less developed' overall. The point is the timing: emotional areas mature before the prefrontal cortex.

Topic: Neuroplasticity and adolescent brain development (vce-psych12-neuroplasticity-and-adolescent-brain-development)

**e.** A correlational study, because the researcher does not manipulate whether participants have had a concussion; they compare groups that already exist. *(1 mark)*

1. Correlational study: the variable (having had a concussion) is not manipulated by the researcher; existing groups are compared. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling it a controlled experiment. In an experiment the researcher allocates participants to conditions, which is impossible (and unethical) for concussion.

Topic: Scientific methodologies and variables (vce-psych12-scientific-methodologies-and-variables)

**f.** For example, informed consent: the participants are under 18, so a parent or guardian must give informed consent after the procedure and risks are explained. (Confidentiality or withdrawal rights are also acceptable with a suitable explanation.) *(2 marks)*

1. Names a relevant ethical guideline, such as informed consent, confidentiality or withdrawal rights. *(1 mark)*
2. Applies it to this study, for example: because participants are under 18, a parent or guardian must give informed consent. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming a guideline without linking it to the study. Say what the researcher must actually do here.

Topic: Ethics in psychological research (vce-psych12-ethics-in-psychological-research)

**A957.** First day at a new school
<!-- id: vce-psych12-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** A dispositional attribution. Sam may be making the fundamental attribution error: overestimating personal factors and underestimating situational ones (for example, Alex's bus may have been late). *(2 marks)*

1. Dispositional (internal) attribution: Sam explains the behaviour by Alex's personality. *(1 mark)*
2. This may be the fundamental attribution error, overestimating personal causes and underestimating the situation, such as a late bus. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling it situational. Laziness is a personal trait, so this is a dispositional attribution.

Topic: Person perception and attributions (vce-psych12-person-perception-and-attributions)

**b.** A stereotype is a generalised belief about the characteristics of members of a group. Sam may assume a student has certain traits just because of a group they belong to, instead of judging them as an individual. *(2 marks)*

1. A stereotype is a generalised belief that all members of a group share the same characteristics. *(1 mark)*
2. Sam may assume a new student has those characteristics because of their group (for example, assuming a sporty student is not studious), rather than getting to know them. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Treating a stereotype as the same as prejudice. A stereotype is a belief; prejudice is a (usually negative) attitude.

Topic: Stereotypes, prejudice and discrimination (vce-psych12-stereotypes-prejudice-and-discrimination)

**c.** Conformity. One factor: unanimity, because every member of the group behaves the same way. (Normative influence, the wish to be accepted by the group, is also acceptable.) *(2 marks)*

1. Conformity: changing behaviour to match a group. *(1 mark)*
2. A factor that increases it here, such as unanimity (all members do the same thing) or normative influence (Sam wants to fit in). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling it obedience. Obedience follows an instruction from an authority figure; nobody told Sam to do this.

Topic: Conformity and group influence (vce-psych12-conformity-and-group-influence)

**d.** Top-down processing uses past experience, knowledge and context to interpret sensory information. Sam knows the word 'please' and the context of a canteen sign, so fills in the missing letter and reads 'Please'. *(2 marks)*
