Explanation: In DSPD the circadian rhythm is delayed so that the person's natural sleep and wake times are later than conventional. Falling asleep and waking too early describes advanced sleep phase disorder.

**A1170.** D. Bright light therapy
<!-- id: psych34a3-crsd-2 -->

Explanation: Morning bright light exposure suppresses melatonin and advances the circadian phase, which helps move a delayed cycle earlier.

**A1171.** A. Her work schedule conflicts with her circadian rhythm, which is synchronised by light and other zeitgebers
<!-- id: psych34a3-crsd-3 -->

Explanation: Shift work forces wakefulness and sleep at times that conflict with the endogenous circadian rhythm and the cues from the light-dark cycle, producing circadian misalignment.

**A1172.** C. it is a hormone secreted by the pineal gland that signals darkness and promotes sleepiness, so timed doses can shift the sleep-wake cycle
<!-- id: psych34a3-crsd-4 -->

Explanation: Melatonin is secreted by the pineal gland in darkness; taken at an appropriate time it helps shift the timing of the circadian rhythm. The GABA option describes benzodiazepine-type action.

**A1173.** D. It is caused by a mismatch between her internal body clock and the new local time, so her zeitgebers are out of step with her circadian rhythm
<!-- id: psych34a3-crsd-5 -->

Explanation: Jet lag is a temporary misalignment between the circadian rhythm and external cues at the new time zone. It resolves as the body clock re-entrains.

**A1174.** DSPD: a circadian rhythm sleep disorder where the sleep-wake cycle is delayed so the person cannot fall asleep until very late and wakes late / has difficulty waking at socially required times; effect: e.g. daytime sleepiness at school due to partial sleep deprivation, reduced concentration or poorer performance, or irritability; the cause is a mismatch between the body clock and school schedule.
<!-- id: psych34a3-crsd-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1175.** Bright light therapy: exposure to bright light soon after waking suppresses melatonin and shifts the circadian rhythm earlier; so the person feels sleepy earlier at night; Melatonin: synthetic melatonin taken several hours before the desired bedtime (in the evening) acts like the natural darkness signal; promotes sleepiness and advances the sleep-wake cycle; timing is important as wrongly timed light or melatonin could shift the cycle in the wrong direction.
<!-- id: psych34a3-crsd-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1176.** Light is the main zeitgeber that entrains the circadian rhythm; daylight exposure signals wakefulness and suppresses melatonin, so during the day her body is cued to be alert; her night work conflicts with her body clock, so she is sleepy at work and has trouble sleeping in daytime (circadian misalignment); noise and social cues (zeitgebers) in daytime may also reduce sleep.
<!-- id: psych34a3-crsd-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1177.** B. In the morning, soon after waking, to suppress melatonin and shift the rhythm earlier
<!-- id: vce-psych34-cd-c1 -->

Explanation: Morning light advances the circadian rhythm. (Evening light is used for advanced sleep phase disorder.)

#### Sleep hygiene and zeitgebers

**A1178.** C. an external environmental cue that helps synchronise the body's circadian rhythm
<!-- id: psych34a3-shz-1 -->

Explanation: Zeitgebers (time-givers) such as light, meals and social cues entrain the endogenous circadian rhythm to the 24-hour day.

**A1179.** A. Keeping a consistent bedtime and wake time, including on weekends
<!-- id: psych34a3-shz-2 -->

Explanation: A regular sleep-wake schedule supports the circadian rhythm. Screens, caffeine and long late naps all interfere with sleep onset.

**A1180.** C. Light
<!-- id: psych34a3-shz-3 -->

Explanation: Light detected by the retina signals the suprachiasmatic nucleus and regulates melatonin release, so it is the dominant zeitgeber.

**A1181.** A. screen emits light, which suppresses melatonin secretion and delays sleep onset
<!-- id: psych34a3-shz-4 -->

Explanation: Light, especially blue-enriched light from screens, suppresses melatonin from the pineal gland and delays sleepiness. Removing the device is a sleep hygiene strategy.

**A1182.** C. A decrease in the mean sleep onset latency
<!-- id: psych34a3-shz-5 -->

Explanation: Sleep onset latency is the time taken to fall asleep; a reduction after the program indicates improved sleep, though a control group would be needed to attribute this to the program.

**A1183.** Sleep hygiene: habits and practices that promote good quality and sufficient sleep; e.g. avoid screens / bright light for an hour before bed; no caffeine in the afternoon or evening; consistent sleep and wake times; a cool, dark, quiet bedroom.
<!-- id: psych34a3-shz-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1184.** Light is detected by the retina and signals the suprachiasmatic nucleus (the body clock) in the hypothalamus; the SCN regulates the pineal gland's secretion of melatonin: darkness increases melatonin and sleepiness, light suppresses it; so regular light exposure keeps the circadian rhythm aligned with the 24-hour day.
<!-- id: psych34a3-shz-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1185.** His weekend schedule delays his exposure to morning light and other zeitgebers, shifting the circadian rhythm later; on Monday his body clock is out of sync with the early school start, so he is sleepy and has difficulty waking; solution: keep a consistent wake time and get morning light exposure (or avoid late-night light / screens); this re-entrains the cycle and reduces social jet lag.
<!-- id: psych34a3-shz-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1186.** C. Blue light acts as a zeitgeber, signalling the SCN to suppress melatonin release
<!-- id: vce-psych34-sh-c1 -->

Explanation: Light, especially blue light, tells the SCN it is daytime. Mental stimulation from the content adds to the problem.

### Unit 4 AOS 2: Mental wellbeing

#### Mental wellbeing and the mental health continuum

**A1187.** A. A state in which a person can realise their abilities, cope with normal stressors, work productively and contribute to their community
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-1 -->

Explanation: Mental wellbeing is more than the absence of illness; it is a state of functioning and capacity to cope and contribute. The first option confuses wellbeing with the absence of disorder.

**A1188.** B. at the mentally healthy / good mental wellbeing end
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-2 -->

Explanation: The continuum runs from mentally healthy functioning, through mental health problems and mental disorders, to severe mental illness. Position can move over time.

**A1189.** A. A mental health problem interferes with a person's thoughts, feelings or behaviour but is generally less severe and shorter lasting than a mental disorder, which meets diagnostic criteria and persists over time
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-3 -->

Explanation: Problems are milder and often temporary (e.g. stress after an exam); a mental disorder is diagnosed against criteria (e.g. DSM-5-TR) and involves significant, persistent distress or impairment.

**A1190.** C. a mental health problem
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-4 -->

Explanation: Temporary difficulties that affect functioning but do not meet diagnostic criteria in severity or duration are mental health problems.

**A1191.** A. People's mental health can vary in degree and shift position over time in response to internal and external factors
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-5 -->

Explanation: A continuum recognises gradations and change over time. Fixed categories ignore the fluctuation that occurs with stress, support, treatment and life events.

**A1192.** The continuum shows mental health ranging from good mental wellbeing/optimal functioning through mental health problems and mental disorder to severe mental illness; position changes with internal factors (e.g. coping, biological state) and external factors (e.g. stressors, social support, treatment); so someone can move toward either end over time.
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1193.** Mental wellbeing: a positive state of functioning in which a person can cope, realise abilities and contribute (e.g. a student who copes with exams and maintains friendships); mental illness: a diagnosable condition involving significant distress and impairment (e.g. major depressive disorder); wellbeing is not just the absence of illness, and illness is more severe and persistent.
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1194.** He has moved from the mentally healthy end toward a mental health problem due to external factors (job loss, reduced social support) which strained his coping; he is not necessarily a mental disorder unless criteria are met; moving back could involve new employment, rebuilding social connections and developing coping strategies/seeking professional help.
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1195.** B. A person's position can move over time, and someone with a disorder can still have high wellbeing
<!-- id: vce-psych34-mw-c1 -->

Explanation: The continuum (mentally healthy, mental health problems, mental disorders) is fluid. Temporary, situational stress is usually a 'problem', not a disorder.

#### Social and emotional wellbeing

**A1196.** A. A holistic concept that includes connection to body, mind and emotions, family and kinship, community, culture, Country, and spirituality and ancestors
<!-- id: psych34a3-sew-1 -->

Explanation: SEWB is a multidimensional, holistic concept, with wellbeing of the individual understood as bound up with family, community, culture and Country.

**A1197.** C. Taking part in cultural ceremonies and caring for land that holds ancestral and spiritual significance
<!-- id: psych34a3-sew-2 -->

Explanation: Connection to Country, including the land, waters and community, is one of the interrelated domains of SEWB.

**A1198.** A. views wellbeing as shaped by the person's connections to family, community, culture and Country, as well as individual factors
<!-- id: psych34a3-sew-3 -->

Explanation: SEWB integrates individual, community, cultural and historical contexts, whereas a narrow biomedical approach emphasises individual biology.

**A1199.** C. Racism and discrimination
<!-- id: psych34a3-sew-4 -->

Explanation: Racism, discrimination, and the ongoing impacts of dispossession and removal from family are risk factors, while kinship, culture and culturally safe services are protective.

**A1200.** A. Spending time with extended family and fulfilling kinship obligations
<!-- id: psych34a3-sew-5 -->

Explanation: Kinship systems and extended family roles and responsibilities are central to identity and support, and so to SEWB.

**A1201.** SEWB is a holistic, multidimensional concept of health where an individual's wellbeing is linked to their connections to others, culture and land; and not just absence of illness; two domains from: body, mind and emotions; family and kinship; community; culture; Country; spirituality and ancestors.
<!-- id: psych34a3-sew-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1202.** Connection to Country and culture is part of identity and belonging in SEWB; its loss (e.g. through displacement or disconnection from community) may reduce sense of identity, spiritual connection and support; so could lead to lowered self-esteem, isolation and increased risk of a mental health problem; the domains are interrelated, so a loss in one affects others.
<!-- id: psych34a3-sew-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1203.** Strategy 1: run programs led by elders/community members that include cultural activities (e.g. language, ceremony, art); justification: strengthens connection to culture and community; Strategy 2: Country-based programs such as caring for Country or on-Country camps; justification: strengthens connection to Country and spirituality/ancestors; other valid: family and kinship support groups; services should be culturally safe and community-controlled.
<!-- id: psych34a3-sew-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1204.** C. Connection to personal wealth
<!-- id: vce-psych34-se-c1 -->

Explanation: The domains are connection to body; mind and emotions; family and kinship; community; culture; Country; and spirituality and ancestors.

#### Specific phobia: contributing factors

**A1205.** A. Excessive, persistent fear of a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger and leads to avoidance
<!-- id: psych34a3-sp-1 -->

Explanation: A specific phobia involves marked, irrational fear of a particular object or situation, usually lasting six months or more, with avoidance or intense distress.

**A1206.** C. Dysfunction in GABA, an inhibitory neurotransmitter, can reduce its ability to calm the fear response, so anxiety is less regulated
<!-- id: psych34a3-sp-2 -->

Explanation: GABA is the main inhibitory neurotransmitter that dampens neural activity; dysfunction in its action can lead to heightened fear and anxiety responses.

**A1207.** B. conditioned stimulus
<!-- id: psych34a3-sp-3 -->

Explanation: Before the incident the dog was a neutral stimulus; paired with the fall (UCS) it becomes a conditioned stimulus that elicits fear (CR). Fear of small dogs shows stimulus generalisation.

**A1208.** B. Observing a parent react with extreme fear to spiders
<!-- id: psych34a3-sp-4 -->

Explanation: Observational learning from a family member or media is part of the social/psychological learning pathway. Low GABA is biological. The conditioning option is psychological.

**A1209.** A. It strengthens synaptic connections associated with the feared stimulus and fear response, making the fear memory more easily triggered
<!-- id: psych34a3-sp-5 -->

Explanation: Repeated activation of the neural pathways linking the stimulus and fear response strengthens the synapses (LTP), supporting persistence of the phobia.

**A1210.** Biological: dysfunction in GABA reduces inhibition of fear response / LTP strengthens the fear memory pathway; Psychological: a frightening experience of turbulence classically conditions fear of flying (flight = CS), or avoidance reinforces fear (negative reinforcement); Social: seeing a family member or media coverage showing fear of air travel (observational learning); overall these factors interact.
<!-- id: psych34a3-sp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1211.** UCS: the frightening event (e.g. being trapped in a lift when it breaks down); UCR: fear/panic; before conditioning the lift is a neutral stimulus; after pairing, the lift becomes the CS; CR: fear response to the lift alone; generalisation to other enclosed spaces may follow.
<!-- id: psych34a3-sp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1212.** Avoiding the object reduces anxiety, which negatively reinforces the avoidance behaviour (anxiety relief is rewarding); the person never learns the feared stimulus is safe, so the conditioned response is not extinguished; the fear memory remains and the phobia persists.
<!-- id: psych34a3-sp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1213.** A. Operant conditioning: avoidance is negatively reinforced by the removal of anxiety
<!-- id: vce-psych34-pf-c1 -->

Explanation: Classical conditioning can precipitate a phobia; operant conditioning (negative reinforcement of avoidance) perpetuates it.

#### Specific phobia: evidence-based interventions

**A1214.** D. a gradual exposure to feared stimuli in a hierarchy from least to most feared while using relaxation to replace the fear response
<!-- id: psych34a3-spi-1 -->

Explanation: Systematic desensitisation uses counterconditioning: relaxation is paired with each step of a fear hierarchy. Immediate full exposure describes flooding.

**A1215.** A. reduces anxiety by enhancing the inhibitory effect of GABA on neural activity
<!-- id: psych34a3-spi-2 -->

Explanation: Benzodiazepines are GABA agonists: they enhance GABA's inhibitory action and reduce arousal. They provide short-term symptom relief only, and have risks such as dependence.

**A1216.** D. Cognitive behavioural therapy (CBT)
<!-- id: psych34a3-spi-3 -->

Explanation: CBT targets unhelpful thoughts and behaviours, and often includes exposure. Medication and diet are biological.

**A1217.** D. flooding
<!-- id: psych34a3-spi-4 -->

Explanation: Flooding involves immediate, prolonged exposure to the feared stimulus until the fear response extinguishes. It is effective but can be distressing, so informed consent matters.

**A1218.** B. Psychoeducation delivered to the person's family so they understand the phobia and can support the treatment
<!-- id: psych34a3-spi-5 -->

Explanation: Social interventions involve the person's social environment, such as psychoeducation, support groups and family involvement.

**A1219.** Person learns a relaxation technique (e.g. deep breathing); a fear hierarchy is constructed from least feared (e.g. word 'spider') to most feared (holding one); person is gradually exposed at each step while relaxed; progression only occurs when anxiety is controlled; by counterconditioning, relaxation replaces fear as the response.
<!-- id: psych34a3-spi-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1220.** Benefit: act quickly to reduce anxiety by increasing GABA's inhibitory effect, useful short-term; limitation: do not address the cause (e.g. learned fear), so symptoms may return when stopped; risks include drowsiness, tolerance and dependence; therefore best combined with psychological interventions such as CBT or exposure.
<!-- id: psych34a3-spi-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1221.** Both are exposure-based and rely on the person learning that the stimulus is not dangerous; systematic desensitisation is gradual with relaxation, flooding is immediate full-intensity exposure; advantage of flooding: often faster results / fewer sessions; disadvantage: very distressing, higher drop-out risk, needs careful consent and monitoring.
<!-- id: psych34a3-spi-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1222.** C. They reduce anxiety by enhancing GABA but do not change the learned fear, and can cause dependence
<!-- id: vce-psych34-pi-c1 -->

Explanation: Benzodiazepines are GABA agonists that relieve symptoms short term. Systematic desensitisation and CBT target the conditioned response and thinking.

#### Protective factors for mental wellbeing

**A1223.** A. a characteristic or circumstance that reduces the likelihood of developing a mental health problem or helps a person cope with adversity
<!-- id: psych34a3-pf-1 -->

Explanation: Protective factors promote resilience and wellbeing; risk factors increase vulnerability.

**A1224.** D. Regular physical exercise and adequate sleep
<!-- id: psych34a3-pf-2 -->

Explanation: Exercise and sleep support brain function and stress regulation. Self-efficacy is psychological; friendships and housing are social.

**A1225.** B. A positive sense of self-efficacy and effective coping strategies
<!-- id: psych34a3-pf-3 -->

Explanation: Self-efficacy (belief in one's ability to succeed) and adaptive coping help a person manage stressors. The others are social or biological factors.

**A1226.** C. Social
<!-- id: psych34a3-pf-4 -->

Explanation: Family support and community connectedness are social protective factors that buffer against stress.

**A1227.** A. There is an association between social support and lower depressive symptoms, but causation cannot be concluded
<!-- id: psych34a3-pf-5 -->

Explanation: A correlational design shows association only. Reverse causation or a third variable could explain the relationship.

**A1228.** Risk factor: increases the likelihood of developing a mental health problem (e.g. chronic stress, social isolation, poverty); protective factor: reduces the likelihood or buffers impact (e.g. social support, exercise, self-efficacy); they influence mental wellbeing in opposite directions and may interact.
<!-- id: psych34a3-pf-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1229.** Biological: e.g. regular sleep or exercise, supports brain function and stress regulation; Psychological: e.g. self-efficacy or effective coping, helps person manage challenges; Social: e.g. family support or social connectedness, provides emotional/practical help and belonging.
<!-- id: psych34a3-pf-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1230.** Social: strong support from family and friends provides emotional and practical help; Psychological: adaptive coping and self-efficacy let him actively search for work and view the situation as challenge; Biological: good sleep/exercise habits maintain stress regulation; protective factors buffer the effect of the stressor.
<!-- id: psych34a3-pf-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1231.** B. Adequate sleep; cognitive behavioural strategies; support from family and friends
<!-- id: vce-psych34-pr-c1 -->

Explanation: Biological: adequate diet and sleep. Psychological: cognitive behavioural strategies, mindfulness. Social: support from family, friends and community.

### Research methods

#### Experimental design and variables

**A1232.** C. the variable that the researcher manipulates or that differs between conditions
<!-- id: psych34a3-ed-1 -->

Explanation: The IV is systematically manipulated; the DV is measured. Uncontrolled variables that affect the DV are extraneous or confounding.

**A1233.** A. control group
<!-- id: psych34a3-ed-2 -->

Explanation: The control group is the baseline for comparison and receives no active treatment (here, a placebo). The experimental group receives the IV.

**A1234.** C. repeated measures design
<!-- id: psych34a3-ed-3 -->

Explanation: In a repeated measures design every participant experiences all conditions, which controls individual participant differences but risks order effects.

**A1235.** A. Counterbalancing the order of conditions
<!-- id: psych34a3-ed-4 -->

Explanation: Counterbalancing varies the order in which participants complete conditions so that practice or fatigue effects are spread equally.

**A1236.** D. An extraneous variable that systematically varies with the IV and may affect the DV, making it hard to determine the cause of the effect
<!-- id: psych34a3-ed-5 -->

Explanation: A confounding variable is an uncontrolled variable that influences the DV in a way that is mixed up with the IV's effect. Random error is not systematic.

**A1237.** IV: listening to music during study (music versus no music); DV: exam scores; controlled variables e.g. time spent studying, difficulty of material, prior knowledge, noise level, time of day, same exam.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ed-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1238.** Random allocation: assigns participants to groups by chance so each has equal likelihood of being in either; spreads participant characteristics evenly, reducing the effect of extraneous variables; placebo: an inactive treatment that looks like the real treatment; controls for participant expectation / placebo effect so differences can be attributed to the IV.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ed-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1239.** Participants self-selected, so groups may differ in other ways (e.g. age, motivation, caffeine use) giving confounding variables; this weakens internal validity as the difference in memory may not be due to sleep; improvement: random allocation of participants to sleep-deprived or normal-sleep conditions by researcher.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ed-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1240.** D. A confounding variable, because it differs systematically between groups and may affect the DV
<!-- id: vce-psych34-ed-c1 -->

Explanation: An extraneous variable becomes confounding when it varies with the IV, so the results cannot be attributed to caffeine alone. Random allocation helps prevent this.

#### Data, statistics and conclusions

**A1241.** C. The number of words recalled from a list
<!-- id: psych34a3-ds-1 -->

Explanation: Quantitative data are numerical measurements. Descriptions in words are qualitative.

**A1242.** A. There are a few unusually high scores
<!-- id: psych34a3-ds-2 -->

Explanation: A mean greater than the median suggests that some high outliers are pulling the mean up (positive skew).

**A1243.** B. the results are statistically significant, so the null hypothesis can be rejected at the 0.05 level, and there is a 3% probability that results at least this extreme occurred by chance alone if there were no effect
<!-- id: psych34a3-ds-3 -->

Explanation: p < 0.05 means the results are statistically significant. A p-value does not give the probability that the hypothesis is true or the effect's size.

**A1244.** B. There is a strong negative association: more screen use is associated with less sleep
<!-- id: psych34a3-ds-4 -->

Explanation: −0.85 shows a strong negative relationship. Correlation does not prove causation.

**A1245.** C. the small sample size limiting the generalisability of the results
<!-- id: psych34a3-ds-5 -->

Explanation: Small samples may not be representative, so conclusions may not generalise to the population.

**A1246.** Mean = 50 ÷ 6 = 8.3 (8.33); median = (7 + 8) ÷ 2 = 7.5; the median is better as the score of 14 is an outlier that pulls the mean up, but median is not affected by extreme scores.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ds-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1247.** Statistical significance means the results are unlikely to be due to chance (p below the chosen level, e.g. 0.05), so the null hypothesis can be rejected; it does not give effect size; a very small difference can be significant in a large sample; so the result may have little real-world importance.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ds-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1248.** Conclusion: higher levels of exercise were associated with lower anxiety scores in the sample (correlation not causation); the results support / do not prove the hypothesis; limitation e.g. sample not representative (so cannot generalise), self-report bias, or uncontrolled third variables.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ds-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1249.** B. Median (3), because the outlier of 18 distorts the mean
<!-- id: vce-psych34-ds-c1 -->

Explanation: The mean is pulled upward by the outlier; the median is resistant to extreme values. The range measures spread, not central tendency.

#### Ethics and validity

**A1250.** A. Right to withdraw
<!-- id: psych34a3-ev-1 -->

Explanation: The right to withdraw is part of voluntary participation. Participants may leave, and may withdraw their data, without penalty.

**A1251.** C. Debriefing the participants and explaining the true purpose
<!-- id: psych34a3-ev-2 -->

Explanation: Where deception is used, it must be justified, minimal and followed by a debrief that explains the true aim and addresses any distress.

**A1252.** A. The extent to which the experiment measures what it claims to measure and the results are due to the IV, not other factors
<!-- id: psych34a3-ev-3 -->

Explanation: Internal validity is about whether changes in the DV are caused by the IV. Generalisability is external validity.

**A1253.** C. Using a random sample from a range of schools across the state
<!-- id: psych34a3-ev-4 -->

Explanation: A larger, randomly selected representative sample allows results to be generalised to the population.

**A1254.** B. precise but not accurate
<!-- id: psych34a3-ev-5 -->

Explanation: Precision is closeness of repeated measurements to each other; accuracy (trueness) is closeness to the true value. A systematic error can cause precise but inaccurate data.

**A1255.** Informed consent: participants agree after being told the nature, risks and procedures (parental consent for minors); confidentiality: personal information and data are protected; voluntary participation / right to withdraw: free to leave at any time; also valid: debriefing, minimising harm (beneficence), deception only if justified.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ev-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1256.** Deception must be justified as necessary and no alternative method is available, with minimal risk of harm and approved by ethics committee; full debriefing after the study explaining the true purpose; participants must be able to withdraw their data / not be exposed to undue distress.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ev-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1257.** Reliability: consistency of results if the study or measure is repeated; validity: whether the study measures what it claims and results are due to the IV (internal) / generalise (external); improve reliability e.g. standardised instructions, clearer or more questionnaire items, test-retest checks, or training of researchers.
<!-- id: psych34a3-ev-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1258.** B. External validity
<!-- id: vce-psych34-ev-c1 -->

Explanation: External validity is whether results can be generalised beyond the sample and setting. Internal validity is whether the study measured what it claimed.

### Challenge questions

#### Challenge set

**A1259.** Precipitation: a dog (NS) is paired with a frightening bite (UCS → fear as UCR), so dogs become a CS producing fear (CR). Perpetuation: avoiding dogs removes the anxiety, which negatively reinforces avoidance, so the fear is never extinguished.
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-1 -->

Explanation: Name each element of classical conditioning and the type of reinforcement.

**A1260.** B. Both the placebo effect and the experimenter effect
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-2 -->

Explanation: Neither participants nor the researcher who interacts with them knows the condition, so expectations cannot bias either.

**A1261.** A. Control order effects such as practice and fatigue
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-3 -->

Explanation: Half the participants do the conditions in one order and half in the reverse order, so order effects are spread evenly.

**A1262.** C. The amygdala is needed for conditioned fear, while the hippocampus supports explicit memory of the pairing
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-4 -->

Explanation: Explicit and implicit aspects of the same event depend on different brain regions.

**A1263.** Affective: more irritability, mood swings and stronger negative emotional reactions; poorer emotional regulation. Cognitive: slower reaction time, poorer attention, concentration and decision-making.
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-5 -->

Explanation: Give one specific effect for each type and state that both are impaired.

**A1264.** A. Can affect many neurons at once and alter their responsiveness over a longer period
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-6 -->

Explanation: Neuromodulators such as dopamine and serotonin spread more widely and change how neurons respond to other neurotransmitters.

**A1265.** A. It is a convenience sample that may not represent the population of interest
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-7 -->

Explanation: Gym users at one university may differ from the wider population (age, fitness, attitudes), limiting generalisation.

**A1266.** A stressor is the event or situation; stress is the psychological and physiological response. In the transactional model, people appraise the stressor differently (primary appraisal) and judge their resources differently (secondary appraisal), so their stress responses differ.
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-8 -->

Explanation: The model focuses on the person–environment interaction, not the stressor alone.

**A1267.** A. Beneficence
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-9 -->

Explanation: Beneficence is commitment to maximising benefits and minimising risks and harms. Non-maleficence is avoiding harm.

**A1268.** D. Bright light during the night shift and dark, blackout conditions when sleeping during the day
<!-- id: vce-psych34-challenge-10 -->

Explanation: Light during the shift promotes alertness; darkness during daytime sleep allows melatonin release. A consistent schedule also helps.

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**A1269.** Exam stress and a breathing study
<!-- id: vce-psych34-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** The sympathetic nervous system (part of the autonomic nervous system). It activates the fight-flight-freeze response, increasing heart rate to deliver more oxygen and glucose to the muscles so the body is ready to respond to a threat. *(2 marks)*

1. Sympathetic nervous system, a division of the autonomic nervous system. *(1 mark)*
2. It prepares the body to respond to a perceived threat (fight-flight-freeze), for example by increasing heart rate so more oxygen and glucose reach the muscles. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming the somatic nervous system. Heart rate and sweating are not under voluntary control, so they are autonomic.

Topic: Divisions of the nervous system (vce-psych34-divisions-of-the-nervous-system)

**b.** In primary appraisal, a person judges whether a situation is relevant to them and whether it is stressful. Mia judges the exam as stressful, most likely as a threat (possible future harm, such as failing). *(2 marks)*

1. Primary appraisal is the evaluation of whether a situation is irrelevant, benign–positive or stressful. *(1 mark)*
2. Mia appraises the exam as stressful, most likely as a threat of future harm or loss. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing secondary appraisal. Judging whether you have the resources to cope comes after primary appraisal.

Topic: Transactional model of stress and coping (vce-psych34-transactional-model-of-stress-and-coping)

**c.** In the resistance stage, cortisol stays high to help the body cope with the stressor. Prolonged cortisol suppresses the immune system, so Mia is more vulnerable to infection; if stress continues into the exhaustion stage, her resistance is lower still. *(2 marks)*

1. During the resistance stage, cortisol is released and stays at a high level. *(1 mark)*
2. Prolonged high cortisol suppresses the immune system, making Mia more susceptible to illness such as a cold, especially if she reaches the exhaustion stage. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying cortisol is released mainly in the alarm reaction. Its sustained effect on immunity comes in the resistance stage.

Topic: Stress responses and the GAS (vce-psych34-stress-responses-and-the-gas)

**d.** For example: making a study timetable, doing practice exams, or asking her teacher about the topics she finds hard. *(1 mark)*

1. An approach strategy deals directly with the stressor, for example making a study plan or asking her teacher for help with difficult topics. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving an avoidance strategy, such as avoiding thinking about the exam. Approach strategies confront the source of stress.

Topic: Coping strategies and flexibility (vce-psych34-coping-strategies-and-flexibility)

**e.** Whether students did 10 minutes of slow breathing or 10 minutes of quiet reading before the test. *(1 mark)*

1. The variable the researcher manipulated: the activity before the test (slow breathing or quiet reading). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming the anxiety rating. That is the dependent variable, which is measured.

Topic: Experimental design and variables (vce-psych34-experimental-design-and-variables)

**f.** Between-subjects (independent groups) design *(1 mark)*

1. Each student was in only one condition, so this is a between-subjects design. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling it a within-subjects design. In a within-subjects design every participant would do both activities.

Topic: Experimental design and variables (vce-psych34-experimental-design-and-variables)

**g.** For example, how anxious each student usually is. Random allocation gives every student an equal chance of being in either group, so students with high and low usual anxiety are likely to be spread evenly, and a difference between the groups is more likely to be caused by the independent variable. *(2 marks)*

1. An extraneous variable such as students' usual (trait) anxiety, sleep the night before, or how well they had prepared for the test. *(1 mark)*
2. Random allocation spreads such participant characteristics evenly between the groups, so they are less likely to explain a difference in anxiety ratings. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Confusing random allocation with random sampling. Random sampling is about who is chosen from the population; random allocation is about which group they join.

Topic: Experimental design and variables (vce-psych34-experimental-design-and-variables)

**A1270.** Late nights, memory and a nervous bell
<!-- id: vce-psych34-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** Sensory memory holds a large amount of incoming sensory information very briefly (for visual information, about 0.2–0.4 seconds). Only information Liam pays attention to is transferred to short-term memory; the rest decays. *(2 marks)*

1. Sensory memory has an unlimited capacity but a very short duration, about 0.2–0.4 seconds for visual (iconic) information. *(1 mark)*
2. Information that is attended to passes to short-term memory; unattended information is lost. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying all of the information moves on to short-term memory. Attention decides what is transferred.

Topic: Atkinson-Shiffrin multi-store model (vce-psych34-atkinson-shiffrin-multi-store-model)

**b.** About 5 to 9 items (7 ± 2), for about 18 to 30 seconds without rehearsal *(1 mark)*

1. Capacity about 7 ± 2 items; duration about 18–30 seconds unless the information is rehearsed. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving only one of the two, or mixing up capacity (how much) with duration (how long).

Topic: Atkinson-Shiffrin multi-store model (vce-psych34-atkinson-shiffrin-multi-store-model)

**c.** The hippocampus encodes and consolidates new explicit (declarative) memories, such as facts Liam studies, and helps transfer them to long-term storage in the neocortex. *(2 marks)*

1. The hippocampus is involved in encoding and consolidating new explicit memories. *(1 mark)*
2. It transfers those memories to the neocortex for long-term storage (it is not where they are permanently stored). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the hippocampus stores all long-term memories. Explicit memories are stored across the neocortex.

Topic: Brain regions in memory (vce-psych34-brain-regions-in-memory)

**d.** Any two, for example: poorer concentration and attention (cognitive); poorer memory for what he studies (cognitive); irritability or mood swings (affective); more intense emotional reactions (affective). *(2 marks)*

1. One effect, for example a cognitive effect: difficulty concentrating or slower thinking. *(1 mark)*
2. A second effect, for example an affective effect: irritability, low mood or bigger emotional reactions. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing effects of total sleep deprivation, such as hallucinations. Liam is getting some sleep each night, so his deprivation is partial.

Topic: Sleep deprivation (vce-psych34-sleep-deprivation)

**e.** Bright (especially blue) light from the screen is detected by the eyes and signalled to the suprachiasmatic nucleus, which reduces the release of melatonin from the pineal gland. Less melatonin means Liam feels less sleepy, so sleep onset is delayed. *(2 marks)*

1. Light from the screen acts as a zeitgeber: it is detected by the eyes and signalled to the suprachiasmatic nucleus. *(1 mark)*
2. The SCN reduces melatonin secretion from the pineal gland, so sleepiness is delayed. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the phone makes Liam produce more melatonin. Light suppresses melatonin.

Topic: Sleep hygiene and zeitgebers (vce-psych34-sleep-hygiene-and-zeitgebers)

**f.** Neutral stimulus: the exam bell before conditioning. Conditioned response: the nervousness Liam now feels when he hears the bell. *(2 marks)*

1. Neutral stimulus: the bell, which before conditioning did not cause nervousness. *(1 mark)*
2. Conditioned response: nervousness in response to the bell alone, after the bell was repeatedly paired with sitting exams. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming the exam as the conditioned stimulus. The exam is the unconditioned stimulus; the bell becomes the conditioned stimulus.

Topic: Classical conditioning (vce-psych34-classical-conditioning)

**A1271.** A fear of dogs
<!-- id: vce-psych34-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** Her fear is excessive and persistent, out of proportion to the actual danger, and her avoidance disrupts her daily life and relationships. *(2 marks)*

1. The fear is intense, persistent and out of proportion to the real danger posed by most dogs. *(1 mark)*
2. It causes significant avoidance that interferes with her everyday functioning, such as visiting friends and parks. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying it is a phobia simply because it is a strong fear. Specific phobia involves disproportionate fear and disruption to daily life.

Topic: Specific phobia: contributing factors (vce-psych34-specific-phobia-contributing-factors)

**b.** Dogs (neutral stimulus) were paired with the bite (unconditioned stimulus), which caused fear (unconditioned response). Dogs became a conditioned stimulus that now produces fear (conditioned response). *(2 marks)*

1. The bite was an unconditioned stimulus producing fear as an unconditioned response; dogs were initially a neutral stimulus. *(1 mark)*
2. After pairing, dogs alone became a conditioned stimulus producing fear as a conditioned response. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling the bite the conditioned stimulus. The bite causes fear naturally, so it is the unconditioned stimulus.

Topic: Classical conditioning (vce-psych34-classical-conditioning)

**c.** Avoiding dogs removes her anxiety. Removing an unpleasant feeling is negative reinforcement, so avoidance becomes more likely, and she never learns that most dogs are safe. *(2 marks)*

1. Avoiding dogs removes her anxiety, which is negative reinforcement. *(1 mark)*
2. Avoidance is therefore strengthened, and it stops her from having experiences that would weaken the fear, so the phobia is maintained. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling this punishment. Removing something unpleasant to increase a behaviour is negative reinforcement.

Topic: Operant conditioning (vce-psych34-operant-conditioning)

**d.** GABA is the main inhibitory neurotransmitter. Low GABA activity means less inhibition of neural firing, so the stress response is triggered more easily and anxiety is heightened. *(2 marks)*

1. GABA is an inhibitory neurotransmitter that reduces the likelihood of postsynaptic neurons firing. *(1 mark)*
2. If GABA is not working effectively, neurons fire more readily, contributing to excessive anxiety and fear responses. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling GABA an excitatory neurotransmitter. Glutamate is the main excitatory one.

Topic: Neurotransmitters and neuromodulators (vce-psych34-neurotransmitters-and-neuromodulators)

**e.** Priya learns relaxation techniques and builds a fear hierarchy, then is gradually exposed to each step (from a picture of a dog to touching a calm dog) while staying relaxed, until the fear response is replaced. *(2 marks)*

1. Priya is taught relaxation techniques and creates a fear hierarchy from least to most anxiety-provoking situations involving dogs. *(1 mark)*
2. She is exposed to each step gradually while relaxed, moving up only when calm, so relaxation replaces fear (reciprocal inhibition). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing flooding, where she would be exposed to the most feared situation at once.

Topic: Specific phobia: evidence-based interventions (vce-psych34-specific-phobia-evidence-based-interventions)

**A1272.** Night shifts and sleep
<!-- id: vce-psych34-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** The suprachiasmatic nucleus is entrained to daylight. Sam is awake at night and tries to sleep in daylight, so light exposure suppresses melatonin when he needs to sleep, and his body clock is misaligned with his schedule. *(2 marks)*

1. The suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) uses light as a zeitgeber to set the timing of the sleep–wake cycle. *(1 mark)*
2. Sam's exposure to daylight when he needs to sleep suppresses melatonin release, so his circadian rhythm is out of step with his sleep schedule. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying shift work stops melatonin being produced altogether. It shifts when melatonin is released.

Topic: Circadian rhythm sleep disorders and treatments (vce-psych34-circadian-rhythm-sleep-disorders-and-treatments)

**b.** 17 hours awake is roughly equivalent to a BAC of 0.05; 24 hours awake is roughly equivalent to 0.10. *(2 marks)*

1. About 17 hours of sustained wakefulness produces impairment similar to a BAC of 0.05. *(1 mark)*
2. Impairment keeps rising: about 24 hours awake is similar to a BAC of 0.10. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving the values the wrong way round. The longer awake, the higher the equivalent BAC.

Topic: Sleep deprivation (vce-psych34-sleep-deprivation)

**c.** Exposure to bright artificial light at chosen times (for example at the start of his shift) helps shift the SCN's timing, delaying melatonin release so that he is alert at night and sleepy in the day. *(2 marks)*

1. Sam is exposed to bright artificial light for a set time at a specific point in his schedule. *(1 mark)*
2. The light acts on the SCN to shift the circadian rhythm and the timing of melatonin release, realigning it with his work schedule. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the light makes him tired. Bright light promotes alertness; its timing shifts the rhythm.

Topic: Circadian rhythm sleep disorders and treatments (vce-psych34-circadian-rhythm-sleep-disorders-and-treatments)

**d.** Deep NREM sleep (stage 3) dominates the first half; REM sleep periods become longer in the second half. *(2 marks)*

1. First half: more deep NREM sleep (stage 3). *(1 mark)*
2. Second half: REM periods lengthen and dominate. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Reversing them. REM periods are short early in the night and lengthen towards morning.

Topic: NREM and REM sleep (vce-psych34-nrem-and-rem-sleep)

**e.** Within-subjects (repeated measures) design; each nurse is compared with themselves, which controls participant-related extraneous variables such as natural reaction speed. *(2 marks)*

1. Within-subjects design: every nurse is tested in both conditions. *(1 mark)*
2. Advantage: it controls participant differences (such as natural reaction speed), so differences are more likely due to the shift type; it also needs fewer participants. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming a between-subjects design. The same nurses are tested twice.

Topic: Experimental design and variables (vce-psych34-experimental-design-and-variables)

**A1273.** Challenge: exam stress and sleep
<!-- id: vce-psych34-cx1 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** Primary: stressful (threat), as she anticipates future harm (failing). Secondary: she judges her coping resources as inadequate (no time for help). *(2 marks)*

1. Primary appraisal: stressful, threat (potential future harm). *(1 mark)*
2. Secondary appraisal: evaluation of coping resources, judged insufficient. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Labelling it a challenge appraisal. She focuses on potential harm, not gain.

Topic: Transactional model of stress and coping (vce-psych34-transactional-model-of-stress-and-coping)

**b.** Cortisol keeps energy available and resistance above normal, but prolonged release suppresses the immune system, making illness more likely. *(2 marks)*

1. Cortisol maintains raised energy and alertness (resistance stage). *(1 mark)*
2. Prolonged cortisol suppresses immune function. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Confusing cortisol with adrenaline, which acts within seconds.

Topic: Stress responses and the GAS (vce-psych34-stress-responses-and-the-gas)

**c.** Blue light from the screen is a zeitgeber that signals the SCN to suppress melatonin, so sleep onset is delayed beyond her already later adolescent sleep phase. *(2 marks)*

1. Light acts on the SCN and suppresses melatonin. *(1 mark)*
2. Sleep onset is delayed, adding to the adolescent sleep–wake shift. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying screens make melatonin go up.

Topic: Sleep hygiene and zeitgebers (vce-psych34-sleep-hygiene-and-zeitgebers)

**d.** Affective: irritability or poorer emotional regulation. Cognitive: poorer concentration, slower thinking or worse decisions. *(2 marks)*

1. A correct affective effect. *(1 mark)*
2. A correct cognitive effect. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing behavioural effects only (such as yawning).

Topic: Sleep deprivation (vce-psych34-sleep-deprivation)

**e.** Making a study plan and asking teachers for help: it deals directly with the stressor, reducing the threat, whereas avoidance gives short-term relief but leaves the problem. *(2 marks)*

1. A valid approach strategy linked to her stressor. *(1 mark)*
2. Explains why it is more effective than avoidance in this context. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Recommending exercise without explaining how it suits her situation.

Topic: Coping strategies and flexibility (vce-psych34-coping-strategies-and-flexibility)

**A1274.** Challenge: testing a phobia treatment
<!-- id: vce-psych34-cx2 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** Adults with a spider phobia who receive systematic desensitisation will show a greater decrease in anxiety rating when shown a live spider than those who receive psychoeducation only. *(2 marks)*

1. Names the IV (type of treatment) and the DV (change in anxiety rating). *(1 mark)*
2. Is directional and refers to the population. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing a question rather than a testable prediction.

Topic: Experimental design and variables (vce-psych34-experimental-design-and-variables)

**b.** Between-subjects design; there are no order effects, since each participant receives only one treatment. *(2 marks)*

1. Between-subjects (independent groups). *(1 mark)*
2. A valid advantage, such as no order effects or less time needed per participant. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling it within-subjects because anxiety was measured twice.

Topic: Experimental design and variables (vce-psych34-experimental-design-and-variables)

**c.** A fell by 5.1; B fell by 1.5. Systematic desensitisation appears more effective than psychoeducation alone, which supports the hypothesis for this sample. *(2 marks)*

1. Correct changes: 5.1 and 1.5. *(1 mark)*
2. A conclusion limited to the sample, linked to the hypothesis. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Claiming the treatment is proven to work for everyone.

Topic: Data, statistics and conclusions (vce-psych34-data-statistics-and-conclusions)

**d.** The person learns relaxation, then is gradually exposed to a fear hierarchy while relaxed. Relaxation is incompatible with fear, so the conditioned fear response is replaced (counterconditioning) and extinguished. *(2 marks)*

1. Relaxation training plus gradual exposure through a fear hierarchy. *(1 mark)*
2. Relaxation replaces the conditioned fear response. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing flooding (immediate full exposure).

Topic: Specific phobia: evidence-based interventions (vce-psych34-specific-phobia-evidence-based-interventions)

**e.** Withdrawal rights: being shown a spider could be very distressing, so participants must be able to stop at any time without penalty. *(2 marks)*

1. Names a relevant guideline. *(1 mark)*
2. Explains its importance in this study. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming a guideline without linking it to the phobia study.

Topic: Ethics and validity (vce-psych34-ethics-and-validity)
